#5978. Dems aim to block Iran war funding. And, Army delays lead azide detonation in Iowa

Democrats who won this month's election are looking to use their influence to block funding for the war in Iran before officially taking charge of Congress. President Trump launched attacks last winter without congressional approval, breaking from the tradition of seeking bipartisan support for wars.

Reporters set up for a press conference outside of the U.S. Capitol Building on Nov. 19, 2026, in Washington, D.C. Finn Gomez/Getty Images hide caption

Trump and Chinese President Xi Jinping met for the third time this year, underscoring a dilemma for Americans who see China as a business opportunity and a national security risk. Nvidia's former CEO, Jensen Huang, convinced Trump to approve several chip sales before his departure. The House Foreign Affairs Committee yesterday, in a highly unusual step, released a list of requests this year from Nvidia and AMD to sell chips directly to Chinese companies.

The U.S. Army has rescheduled the detonation of around 9,500 pounds of unstable lead azide at the Iowa Army Ammunition Plant in Middletown to Monday, Nov. 23. This decision comes after Iowa lawmakers and two environmental groups pushed for a delay. The plant, which manufactures various types of ammunition, has stored lead azide in a water-ethanol solution in underground tanks for 20 years. Recent inspections found that the tanks were too dry, leading to crystallization and instability. To prevent an accidental explosion, the Army wants to detonate the lead azide under controlled conditions.

Environmentalist Jassim al-Asadi stands on a boat and looks out over the marshes where he grew up in southern Iraq. Despite having been kidnapped, tortured and accused by an Iran-backed militia of being an Israeli agent, Asadi is back in the marshes. Claire Harbage/NPR hide caption

Iraqi environmentalist Jassim al-Asadi has dedicated his life to protecting Iraq's ancient marshlands. He even helped reflood areas that Saddam Hussein drained and set fire to decades ago to flush out resistance fighters. In February 2023, an Iran-backed militia kidnapped and tortured Asadi for two weeks on false charges that he was an Israeli spy who had ignited anti-government protests. He endured the trauma by reciting poetry until his Bani Asad tribe mobilized armed tribal fighters to secure his release. Once freed, he refused European asylum so he could continue his work. Today, despite drought and other hardships on the land, Asadi continues to monitor recovering water levels as wildlife slowly starts to return to the marshes.

#5968. Texas execution took over an hour as staff struggled to find vein; Reinaldo Dennes moaned about pain

This photo combination shows Reinaldo Dennes, left, on April 14, 2026 and Thomas Mitchell Overton. Texas Department of Criminal Justice/AP hide caption

HUNTSVILLE, Texas — Texas executed Reinaldo Dennes on Thursday in a procedure that took over an hour, as prison staffers struggled to find a suitable vein for the two intravenous needles carrying the lethal dose of the sedative pentobarbital.

And once it began, he moaned repeatedly about pain, groaned several times and asked God to take him away from the pain. A prison spokesperson said all procedures were followed appropriately and suggested that Dennes might have exaggerated or faked the pain.

The execution followed renewed scrutiny of capital punishment in the U.S. in the aftermath of Tennessee's failed execution in September of Christa Pike, who survived two doses of pentobarbital that her lawyers say probably did not enter her bloodstream correctly and instead pooled in her arms, leaving them burned and blistered.

Dennes, who was found guilty of capital murder in the death of Janos Szucs, a diamond dealer found shot multiple times in his Houston office in January 1996, was the second person put to death in the country on the day, after Florida carried out a record-breaking 20th execution of the year, of Thomas Mitchell Overton.

Dennes gave a brief statement in which he said he wanted his loved ones to know he was blessed the past 30 years "knowing the love of the father." Then he told the warden: "Go, warden!"

Almost immediately he exclaimed: "The pain! Go! Go!" He uttered an expletive and implored "the father" to "take this pain." "Let me go, father," he said again and again. "Let me go. … I'm here. I've been your son all these years."

Then he shouted: "My right leg is killing me!" and described it as a cramp. After another profanity, he grunted, then said, "I don't think this is normal. Take me, father. Don't let me suffer like this. C'mon."

After three loud groans, Dennes began snoring sounds — eight of them — that progressively got quieter before a final loud snore. Then he began breathing quietly, about a dozen breaths, also getting progressively less pronounced.

The procedure was completed at 7:07 p.m., 63 minutes after he was strapped to the gurney. A minute later, after being examined by a physician, Dennes was pronounced dead at 7:08 p.m. at the penitentiary in Huntsville.

Prison officials said all procedures were followed appropriately. Spokesperson Amanda Hernandez acknowledged a number of unsuccessful attempts to find a suitable vein before the execution team managed to insert the needles in Dennes' left foot and on the left side of his neck.

She did not know how many attempts were unsuccessful but said the sites ultimately used were in accordance with the state's procedures.

Hernandez also suggested that Dennes may have been exaggerating the pain, saying he began complaining before the drugs began to flow and also of pain in places where there was no IV.

Asked if he was faking, she replied, "I think, yeah, there were allegations. ... There was nothing abnormal on our part."

"This isn't the first time we had to insert IVs into other locations. It's also not the first time we've had an inmate make allegations of pain immediately," Hernandez said.

Witness Janice Copeland's husband, David, was a security guard shot during the robbery. He survived his wounds and died five years ago.

"David always felt Reinaldo would outlive him. Unfortunately he was right," she said Thursday. "Finally after 30 years, justice has been served."

Earlier in the day Florida carried out its execution of Overton, 71, who was convicted of murdering a pregnant woman and her husband, Susan and Michael MacIvor, during a home invasion.

Overton was pronounced dead at 6:19 p.m. following a three-drug injection at Florida State Prison. In a final statement, he denied ever breaking into the couple's home and attacking them, saying, "I never killed anyone."

Florida has now carried out more than half the nation's 39 executions to date this year under Republican Gov. Ron DeSantis, who has made aggressive use of the death penalty like few other governors.

DeSantis, who leaves office in January, has said he wanted justice for families who wait decades for death sentences to be carried out. He signed the death warrants that led to a record of 19 executions in 2025, then the most in a single year in Florida since the death penalty was reinstated in 1976. Florida still plans two more executions, in December.

Texas set a record for executions there when 40 people were put to death in 2000.

The executions of two men in their 70s cast a renewed spotlight on the aging of the U.S. death row population.

#5958. Trump touts meeting with Xi and Putin but offers little results

President Trump speaks as Secretary of State Marco Rubio listens during a news briefing at the White House on Friday. Trump is addressing the media after returning from a visit to China where he held trilateral talks with Russian President Vladimir Putin and Chinese President Xi Jinping. Kevin Dietsch/Getty Images hide caption

President Trump took to the White House briefing room Friday telling reporters his trip to Asia this week – where he met with Chinese leader Xi Jinping and Russian leader Vladimir Putin – was a resounding success, despite offering few tangible results.

"The United States entered this trip from a position of unmatched economic and diplomatic strength," Trump said, standing at the podium with VIce President JD Vance and Secretary of State Marco Rubio behind him.

But the trip resulted more in agreements to continue discussions rather than any concrete changes. The meeting with Putin and Xi, though historic as it was the first time they met, only resulted in an agreement to continue discussing reducing nuclear risks.

In Manila, where Trump met with allies before he traveled to China, Trump signed a trade agreement with Thailand. But there are still details to the deal being finalized and the document signed simply marked a "substantial conclusion" in negotiations.

At the White House, Trump gave a wide-ranging opening statement to the press that veered from recounting his trip and who he met with, to asides about Iran, the U.S. Federal Reserve, his quest for a Nobel Peace Prize and domestic manufacturing. It was the first time he took questions from the press since being in China.

One significant change Trump announced in the middle of his press remarks was that he and XI have agreed to establish "joint guardrails" on artificial intelligence – a new move from Trump, who has been adamant that regulation over the technology would inhibit US innovation and help China lead in the global AI race. No further details about what the guardrails would look like, when they would be negotiated and released and how they would be applied between the two nations has been announced.

Trump, who said Xi also likes his term "super intelligence" rather than artificial, said, "Problem is, it may be too super. So, President Xi and I agree that we're going to do some joint guardrails, the two of us."

The announcement of future guardrails comes as Trump is facing headwinds from growing tech outages in the U.S. that ultimately led to Trump replacing the White House's cyber director.

In the briefing, Trump added that he would continue to push for the construction of data centers in the U.S. This comes after midterm election voters from both parties showed their opposition to future unfettered growth.

"Make them pay your community, but take advantage. Don't shut them off," Trump said of the massive complexes that have drawn criticism from communities due to environmental and noise pollution.

He also announced penalizations against companies that try to build data centers outside the U.S.

"If it's not this country, there's somebody else outside. And we're not going to allow our companies to build data centers outside of our country. We're not going to allow that to happen," Trump said.

Trump's trip to China came as Trump's own cabinet on what approach to take toward China. Trump's Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick has been in favor of selling China U.S. chips, a stance that former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang shared. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent has expressed a more hawkish view toward China, and Trump has acknowledged both perspectives.

The divide has become more prominent as Lutnick and Trump face backlash for their relationship to former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, who resigned from his position after admitting to an affair with a woman who may be a Chinese spy.

On Friday, Trump said he only wants "older chips" to be sold to China, rather than the most cutting-edge technology the U.S. has, and he said he'd be OK with no chips going to China at all, though he pointed out the downside to that.

"You have to understand, China makes chips too," Trump said. "And if we're not going to sell them, they'll make them themselves."

#5951. Trump initially accepted the midterm results, but most GOP voters doubt they were fair

A firefighter walks past voting booths at a firehouse serving as a polling location in Detroit on Nov. 3. Spencer Platt/Getty Images hide caption

President Trump may have initially accepted the results of his party's midterm losses, but data released Friday shows there is still broad distrust in the elections process among Republicans.

Less than half of Republican voters nationwide considered the midterm elections "free and fair," according to new polling by the University of California San Diego. Just 52% of Republicans said they trusted votes were counted accurately.

In comparison, after the 2024 election that Trump won, more than 80% of Republicans said they had confidence in the vote-counting process.

While trust in elections often dips for voters of the losing party, the GOP has seen bigger swings in confidence in recent years, as Trump has falsely claimed that U.S. elections are corrupt.

"There are still a lot of people who remain unconvinced that they can trust elections," said Thad Kousser, a co-director of UC San Diego's Center for Transparent and Trusted Elections. "And they're predominantly located in one [political] party."

Overall, a wide majority — 66% — of Americans said they think the midterms were fair. That represents an improvement from before the election, when less than half of U.S. adults said they expected free and fair contests.

But that post-election increase in trust was buoyed by Democrats and independents — many of whom worried before Election Day about potential federal interference at the polls that didn't come to pass.

"It would be a mistake to [look at the 66%] and think, 'Oh, everyone has a pretty good view of the election,'" said Lauren Prather, also a co-director of UC San Diego's election center. "It really buries the lede a little bit about the partisan gap."

The widespread doubt among Republicans may be a reality check for those wondering whether Trump's brand of election denial has run its course in American politics.

On the one hand, there will be fewer politicians who deny or question election results in office next year. And the vast majority of election-denying candidates running for positions involved in overseeing statewide elections lost their races this year, according to tracking by the advocacy group States United Action.

Election experts have also celebrated as Trump and high-profile losing candidates on the ballot this year conceded their losses.

"Sure, I'll accept [the results]," Trump told Fox News' Bret Baier on election night, as it became clear Democrats would retake the House and Senate. "Why wouldn't I?"

But just a few days later, he began conspiracy theorizing about those same results.

"Does anybody really believe that this 'Election' was honest," Trump posted online.

The new polling from UC San Diego shows that among Republicans, there still may be a receptive audience to such claims. When asked if politicians are discussing voter fraud too much, too little or about the right amount, just 15% of Republicans answered "too much."

"There is both demand among the Republican base for continued focus on this issue, and as we saw even after the election, politicians are continuing to supply it," Kousser said. "As long as those two dynamics are there, there's still going to be a market for these concerns."

To be clear, in most elections — domestically and internationally — supporters of the losing party usually see a dip in trust.

"It's sort of like asking people after an overtime basketball game, do you trust the referees?" Kousser said.

But in an ideal world, he said that oscillation would be more like 10 percentage points in either direction. Not the 30-point swing seen among Republicans between 2024 and 2026.

The numbers do still represent an improvement from 2020, however. At that time, leading up to the Jan. 6 riot at the U.S. Capitol in 2021, just 22% of Republicans said they had confidence in the nationwide vote count, according to similar polling done by MIT's Election Data and Science Lab.

#5943. A second judge blocks Trump's arch, saying it's unlikely to succeed in court

President Trump holds up a model of his proposed triumphal arch at a White House event in Oct. 2025. Two different federal judges have ruled against it this week. John McDonnell/AP hide caption

A federal judge in Virginia plans to issue a preliminary injunction blocking President Trump's triumphal arch indefinitely.

District Judge Leonie Brinkema said during a Friday hearing that she would grant the request of the county board of Arlington, Va., which sued over the arch in October. Democracy Forward, the nonprofit representing the plaintiffs, confirmed the ruling in a statement shared with NPR.

"We are pleased that the court has recognized the irreparable harm that will come to people who live and work in Arlington and stopped the massive vanity project while our case is heard and considered by the court," said senior counsel Kate Talmor.

Justice Department spokesperson Matthew Nies told NPR it will appeal the decision immediately.

The 250-foot arch would be built in Memorial Circle, which is part of D.C. but sits on the Virginia side of the Potomac River, a short distance from both Arlington National Cemetery and Ronald Reagan Washington National Airport, both in Arlington.

Arlington County has argued that the arch would "cause significant harm" to its residents in multiple ways, including increasing noise and air pollution and "clogging thoroughfares needed for emergency transport services." And it said an arch that doubles as a military complex — as Trump has suggested — would pose public safety risks and require additional county resources to manage.

Arlington also argues the arch would disrupt historic sightlines between Arlington National Cemetery and D.C.'s National Mall, and pose logistical challenges for the roughly 24 to 27 funerals the cemetery holds every weekday. The county also has concerns about air and road traffic, saying construction-related delays could cause more accidents and disrupt emergency vehicles far beyond the traffic circle.

"In the last 60 days, Arlington police, fire, and emergency units have responded to approximately 200 incidents in the area surrounding the Circle, including traffic stops and assisting with unconscious persons within Arlington National Cemetery," they wrote. "And the visitors the Arch is intended to attract will increase demand for those same emergency services."

The plaintiffs, like many critics of the arch, accused the Trump administration of rushing through federally mandated environmental reviews and failing to consider alternatives. The National Park Service has defended the federal review process — which ended in approval earlier this month — and said building the arch somewhere else would defeat its purpose.

"The County brings this lawsuit to halt the agency's feverish pace toward construction of the Arch and to ensure that the 'hard look' mandated by [the National Environmental Preservation Act] is completed before the agency commences disruptive and harmful ground-disturbing activities," the plaintiffs wrote last month.

NPR has reached out to the White House for comment. Spokesperson Davis Ingle told NPR earlier this week that the administration will "continue making the case for this beautiful and fitting monument and are confident we will be fully vindicated as this litigation proceeds."

Brinkema, an appointee of President Clinton, is the second judge to rule against the arch this week.

On Wednesday, D.C. District Judge Tanya Chutkan extended a restraining order that prevents the Trump administration from breaking ground through Dec. 3 while she considers the underlying legal arguments.

That case was brought earlier this year by a group that includes Vietnam War veterans and was joined by the governments of D.C. and Virginia last month. Nine Kennedy family members moved to get involved last week.

Friday's ruling goes even further, preventing construction while Arlington's legal challenge plays out.

And Brinkema suggested that Arlington is likely to prevail. The judge said in court that she didn't see "any way" the government could win the case on the merits.

She reiterated that the project lacks congressional approval, which the Trump administration has not sought. It argues that the arch is covered by a 1925 law authorizing a pair of much shorter columns at that same location, which were never built. Both judges have frowned upon that argument.

"It hurts your credibility with the court by focusing on that," Brinkema told lawyers Friday, according to Reuters.

Brinkema also called it a "rushed job," and seemed to find the government's analysis of the structure's environmental and economic impact inadequate, particularly concerning the impact of construction cranes — which the government has said would be taller than the arch itself — on airspace.

The Federal Aviation Administration has said the arch would not pose a hazard to aircraft as long as it is lit a certain way, including with a controversial eternal flame on top. But aviation safety experts are concerned that the structure would add an additional layer of risk to an already notoriously complicated airspace. They say that's especially true if it has any military capabilities, which were not included in the FAA's analysis.

#5935. Outer Spaces takes solar beyond the rooftop with integrated backyard energy system

Residential solar is finding a new place to call home: the backyard.

Outer Spaces, a new company founded by the entrepreneurs behind outdoor living brand Outer, is introducing a solar-powered pavilion that combines photovoltaic generation, battery storage and usable outdoor living space. The company’s first product, OS1, can be designed for multiple functions, including an outdoor kitchen, office, gym or gathering space, while generating electricity and providing backup power for the home.

Outer Spaces is planning to produce outdoor structures that integrate solar PV and energy storage. Credit: Outer Spaces

The California-based company is positioning OS1 as a new category of residential energy infrastructure that goes beyond conventional rooftop arrays and garage-mounted batteries. It builds on the growing interest in solar structures that also serve a secondary purpose.

OS1 combines a solar pavilion with a finished outdoor power deck. Solar panels integrated into the overhead structure generate electricity, while a thin battery storage system is housed beneath the deck. According to Outer Spaces, the combination allows the structure to produce energy throughout the day and provide backup power during outages.

Rather than competing exclusively with traditional rooftop solar, OS1 offers homeowners an additional way to use available property for energy production, particularly when roof aesthetics, space limitations, or homeowner preferences become obstacles to conventional installations.

Outer Spaces describes OS1 as a modular system designed for rapid on-site assembly, with minimal to no excavation required. The company says installation avoids traditional construction steps such as concrete pouring and lumber cutting. Once the structure is assembled, a licensed electrician then connects its energy system to the customer’s home.

The product’s first availability is scheduled for 2027, beginning in Southern California and select additional markets; pricing is expected to be announced in early 2027.

Solar canopies and pergolas have increasingly incorporated features such as EV charging, lighting, fans and weather protection, giving homeowners additional reasons to invest in solar outside the traditional rooftop model.

Outer Spaces represents another potential avenue for expanding residential solar adoption through design-driven products. The opportunity will ultimately depend on pricing, installation economics, permitting requirements and the system’s measurable energy performance.

Outer Spaces plans to showcase OS1 publicly for the first time at RE+ 2026, Nov. 16 to 19, in Las Vegas.

#5926. Secure Solar Futures set to install 313-kW array on Virginia elementary school

Secure Solar Futures, a commercial solar contractor, will build a 313-kW solar power system at Preston Park Elementary School in Roanoke, Virginia. Construction of the system is expected to start later this year at no cost to the Roanoke Schools through a 25-year power purchase agreement with Secure Solar Futures.

A total of 531 bifacial solar panels manufactured by VSUN Solar in Houston, Texas, will be installed on the rooftop of the school, and the array is expected to offset 67% of the power used by the school annually. In its first year, the school’s solar system is projected to produce 421 MWh of clean energy, enough to power 44 average Virginia homes.

Under the PPA, the solar power equipment will be provided at no upfront cost to Roanoke Schools. For the 25-year term of the agreement, Pardee Renewable Energy and Secure Solar Futures will own, operate and maintain the solar power system and will sell the energy produced to the school division at a rate lower than the local electric utility.

Preston Park Elementary School is expected to save $581,000 electricity costs over the term of the PPA. After the conclusion of the term, Roanoke Schools can opt to take ownership of the solar power system at a nominal cost, producing its own energy for the remainder of the equipment’s lifespan.

#5916. SOLRITE and sonnen collaborate on integrated VPP breaker

SOLRITE and sonnen plan to digitally integrate VPP smart breakers into what the companies are calling the SOLRITE sonnen VPP. The expanded VPP Node combines sonnenHub with ADAPT Energy’s VPP smart breaker technology to manage major household loads. The VPP smart breaker, controlled by ADAPT Energy, can also participate in on-grid VPP events, enabling the same electrical infrastructure to be used for both backup and grid participation.

“Our work with ADAPT Energy is a meaningful step forward in how we think about residential battery storage and VPP participation,” said Regan George, CEO and founder of SOLRITE Energy. “We have already demonstrated the tremendous value of connecting sonnen batteries together as a pooled grid asset. Now, we’re going inside the home and unlocking another layer of flexibility that has been sitting there all along. By combining the battery with additional load management made possible by the VPP smart breaker technology, we’re creating a more valuable node for the grid while also giving homeowners a clever backup solution.”

The system can shed large loads without requiring those circuits to be relocated. ADAPT Energy keeps the smart breakers synchronized with the sonnen battery during backup mode and automatically restores the breakers when grid power returns.

This integration is designed to manage whole-home electrical loads in the background, not requiring homeowners to reset circuits during an outage.

“This is about making whole-home backup work the way homeowners expect it to,” said Troy Morgan, president of PanTech Design, the developer of ADAPT Energy. “Instead of putting the pressure on homeowners to change the way their home is wired, and to manually manipulate loads during an outage, we’re putting intelligence into their energy system, which provides a seamless and automatic backup power experience for their home. Our technology empowers home loads to supply optimal backup power during an outage or provide valuable flexibility to the grid.”

When a VPP event happens, the sonnen battery can discharge or charge with direct load shedding via ADAPT. ADAPT Energy will strictly shed pre-approved household loads. A customer who has opted to include a pool pump, for example, can have that load temporarily turned off while the sonnen battery is discharging to the grid.

“With the addition of ADAPT Energy’s VPP smart-breaker technology to the SOLRITE–sonnen ecosystem, we are expanding the virtual power plant from a battery-centered resource into a fully orchestrated home-energy platform,” said Blake Richetta, chairman and CEO of sonnen. “By synchronizing energy that has been harnessed in our battery with intelligent, real-time control of household electrical loads, we can deliver more flexible, responsive capacity to the grid precisely when it is needed most. This integration materially strengthens the value of SOLRITE’s virtual power plant—supporting grid reliability, creating greater customer value, and enabling SOLRITE to continue delivering industry-leading solar-plus-storage VPA solutions to homeowners.”

#5906. Solar lease holdouts still see value in privately-owned residential projects

The One Big Beautiful Bill Act drastically reduced the lifespan of federal subsidies available to certain segments of the solar market, and among the first to go was the investment tax credit for residential projects. The ITC is a historic renewable energy subsidy that provides a 30% tax credit against the total cost of an eligible project, and it is currently available to the commercial and utility-scale solar segments.

A residential solar project in New York installed by Nickels Energy Solutions. Credit: Nickels Energy Solutions

After 2025, homeowners hoping to install solar on their house couldn’t subsidize the project’s cost with this federal subsidy if it was privately owned. However, a loophole in the tax credit language has allowed leased or “third party-owned” (TPO) solar projects to still receive the ITC (25D) on residential solar projects. In this scenario, it matters who owns the solar project, not necessarily where it’s built. The project can be subsidized because it is owned by a commercial company, and not the homeowner.

Some residential solar installers are still strictly pursuing and installing privately-owned solar projects. Nickels Energy Solutions (NES), a residential solar contractor working in Central New York believes a privately-owned PV system is still the better deal for the customer.

“We don’t have the same offers as some of the bigger companies,” said Kevin Nickels, VP of sales with NES. “If you can offer leases, it’s like the ‘haves and have-nots.’ I don’t have that loophole or that trick up my sleeve, so it forces me to get the project sold, while I’m as sharp as I can be with margins to make it somewhat competitive.”

TPO solar transactions are typically arranged at a lower up-front cost than a wholly owned solar project. Lease contract lengths vary by provider, and financiers have surety in these transactions because the projects will be built with products from an approved vendor list (AVL). Products on an AVL can qualify for a domestic content subsidy, another tax credit in addition to the ITC, because they are manufactured using a certain percentage of domestic components.

These are premium products vetted for AVLs. But it can also mean that installers are paying premium prices for components in building TPO solar projects.

Nickels believes that, while a privately owned residential solar project will cost more at the start, the combination of expensive components and interest rates for financing over a lease term — with factors like rate increases — can cost a customer more in the long run.

“I don’t think all leases are bad,” he said. “I just don’t think it’s what’s best for the industry or the homeowner.”

Rudy Wright, owner of Wright-Way Solar of Tyler, Texas, has shifted the company’s focus from building new projects to servicing existing systems. As the ITC exited the residential solar market, so did many of the lenders financing these projects. Without lending options, Wright-Way’s installation output has slowed down, and those clients who can afford solar are paying with cash or using a home equity loan to cover the cost.

Despite that, Wright said he isn’t very interested in building leased solar projects.

Wright-Way Solar built a solar plus storage project at a Ranch in Texas featuring arrays that are facing east, west and south. Credit: Wright-Way Solar

“I do think it cost me some of those entry level clients, but every time I analyzed the financing end of it, I felt like I was benefitting my company and the industry more than the customer,” Wright said. “I haven’t seen the angle where bringing a pre-purchase agreement or bringing a lease agreement has enough ironclad return for the customer.”

Tyler is a unique market for solar because many of Wright-Way’s customers are trying to make their properties more resilient, and less reliant on the grid, by installing solar and storage, and not just seeking cost savings for their energy use. Wright has installed solar plus storage systems on Texan ranches at a scale close a small commercial project.

That promise of resilience is still enough reason for some people to commission a solar project in Texas, where the state’s independent electrical grid operator has faltered and experienced dangerous outages in recent years.

“Folks realize money in the bank doesn’t do you any good if the power goes out,” Wright said.

NES is still building a steady pipeline of residential solar projects in New York. Nickels said that has been possible in part because many of its clients are upper-middle class; but New York also maintains an incentive program for solar projects, NY-Sun, which includes a 25% state tax credit for residential systems.

“The OBBBA caught a lot of states flat-footed,” Nickels said. “Without federal support, it puts a lot of pressure on what the state is offering.”

Some states’ solar subsidy programs have stepped in where federal support has diminished since the OBBBA was passed. The North Carolina Clean Energy Technology Center maintains the Database of State Incentives for Renewables & Efficiency, which analyzes which states currently have policies and incentives supporting clean energy developments. Every state has policy enabling solar projects, but not necessarily cost incentives for individual projects.

While traditional residential solar projects have lost a significant subsidy, the reality is that energy costs have risen in response to new grid demands from hyperscale data centers and oil scarcity from the United States’ war in Iran. Sure, a privately owned residential solar project won’t receive the ITC, but it can still stabilize energy costs without them.

“We lost a big incentive, but the rate increase is proportional,” Nickels said.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5893. Three Routes to VPP Revenue for Installers, EPCs, and Developers Utility programs, wholesale markets, and bilateral contracts each come with different eligibility rules, payment structures, and commercial responsibilities.

By Intersolar & Energy Storage North America

In June 2026, Google and Voltus announced a three-year agreement to unlock up to 100 megawatts (MW) of distributed capacity annually in PJM. Under Voltus’s Bring Your Own Capacity model, Google funds procurement while Voltus aggregates qualifying distributed energy resources (DERs) and compensates participating customers.1

The deal is one of three distinct paths to earn revenue from DERs. Across the U.S., utility programs, wholesale electricity markets, and bilateral contracts each have their own eligibility requirements, payment structures and commercial responsibilities.

Utility programs offer an established pathway for compensating customers and asset owners who reduce demand, shift consumption or discharge stored energy during periods of grid need. Payments may be tied to enrollment, committed capacity, measured performance or a combination of these factors. Massachusetts, for example, offers residential battery customers $275 per kW of average summer performance through ConnectedSolutions2.

Beyond customer incentive programs, utilities also procure distributed flexibility through contracts to address local grid constraints or broader capacity needs. Unlike standardized incentives, these arrangements can establish specific delivery requirements and compensation terms, creating opportunities for developers and service providers to assemble and manage DER portfolios. During a July 2026 heat event, Green Mountain Power dispatched 90 MW of stored energy capacity, helping customers avoid an estimated $6 million in peak-related costs during one peak hour3.

Aggregated DERs can earn revenue from energy, capacity, and ancillary services in wholesale electricity markets, subject to regional market rules and resource qualification. Unlike utility-specific programs, wholesale markets can provide access to broader regional revenue opportunities and multiple grid services. For developers and installers, participation typically occurs through an aggregator or other qualified market participant rather than through direct market registration.

While Federal Energy Regulatory Commission (FERC) Order 2222 established a broader framework for DER participation, uneven implementation across regional markets continues to constrain commercial opportunities for aggregated resources. Market access does not automatically translate into revenue, as individual resources must still meet qualification requirements and comply with market-specific rules. New York Independent System Operator (NYISO), for example, requires DER aggregations to meet minimum capacity and real-time telemetry requirements, which can increase participation costs and limit the economic viability of smaller resources.

Wholesale participation can also expose VPP portfolios to fluctuating market prices, performance obligations and restrictions on receiving compensation for overlapping services. In Texas, ERCOT’s Aggregate Distributed Energy Resource (ADER) pilot allows aggregated DERs to participate in wholesale energy and ancillary services markets, subject to qualification, telemetry, and dispatch requirements.

Bilateral contracts allow utilities and large electricity consumers to procure distributed flexibility directly from aggregators and service providers. Growing interest from data center operators and other large energy buyers is expanding the potential market for VPP capacity beyond traditional utility programs. Unlike standardized programs, bilateral agreements can be tailored to a buyer’s specific needs, with negotiated terms for capacity, delivery periods, compensation, and performance obligations.

The appropriate route depends on existing customer relationships, market access, and the responsibilities each partner assumes. Developers and EPCs working on commercial storage projects may find opportunities through utility procurement or capacity contracts, while residential installers with established battery customer bases may be well positioned to incorporate VPP enrollment into utility programs. Wholesale markets can expand revenue opportunities through aggregation partners, and bilateral contracts can create opportunities to develop resources around specific buyer needs.

Sponsored Content by Intersolar & Energy Storage North America

#5889. Evidence Grows for Link Between Imaging and Brain Tumor Risk in Kids

— However, findings "should not be used alone when counseling families," experts say

Medical imaging with ionizing radiation was associated with a dose-related increase in brain tumor risk among children and adolescents, a retrospective cohort study suggested.

Compared with no exposure, brain tumor risk increased with cumulative radiation dose, with hazard ratios of 1.12 (95% CI 1.01-1.26) for the lowest exposure category (>0 to <1 mGy) to 3.85 (95% CI 2.43-5.75) for the highest category (≥150 mGy).

Cumulative dose was associated with brain tumor risk overall, with an excess relative risk (ERR) per 100 mGy of 1.27 (95% CI 0.83-1.77, P<0.001), and with a stronger association for aggressive histologies (ERR per 100 mGy 1.51, 95% CI 1.00-2.11, P<0.001) reported Diana L. Miglioretti, PhD, of the University of California Davis School of Medicine, and colleagues.

"This study adds to the growing evidence that pediatric medical imaging with ionizing radiation is associated with increased cancer risk," they wrote in JAMA.

"These findings reinforce the importance of ensuring that pediatric imaging examinations that use ionizing radiation are ordered only when clinically justified and performed using optimized protocols that minimize dose," they concluded.

Miglioretti and team also noted that the cumulative incidence of brain tumors by age 21 increased from 5.4 per 10,000 children with no exposure to 22.1 per 10,000 children with a cumulative dose of 150 mGy or greater, corresponding to an excess risk of 16.7 brain tumors per 10,000 at that dose level.

For children with cumulative doses of 50 to less than 75 mGy -- a dose range comparable to the dose from one head CT -- the cumulative incidence of brain tumors was 13.3 per 10,000 children, corresponding to an excess risk of 7.9 brain tumors per 10,000 exposed children. The estimated population-attributable fraction of brain tumors associated with radiation exposure from medical imaging was 10.1% (95% CI 5.8-14.2).

In an editorial accompanying the study, Ruggero De Maria, MD, of the Università Cattolica del Sacro Cuore in Rome, and colleagues noted that the study "strengthens an existing epidemiologic signal and makes its potential population impact easier to understand."

However, while these findings "place a rare outcome in perspective," they "should not be used alone when counseling families," they cautioned. "For an individual child, the small potential long-term risk must be considered alongside the often much larger and immediate benefit of obtaining the correct diagnosis."

"For clinical practice, the standard remains straightforward: select the examination most likely to answer the clinical question, use ionizing radiation when its expected benefit justifies the exposure, and obtain the necessary diagnostic information with the lowest dose compatible with adequate image quality," they wrote.

The results from this study support those from other studies, including the European EPI-CT cohort study, which found a significant dose-response relationship, such that for every 10,000 people who received a single head CT scan, approximately one patient developed brain cancer attributable to radiation exposure in the 5 to 15 years following the examination.

However, "these studies were conducted outside North America and therefore reflect CT practices and radiation doses in those settings ... and all focused on radiation exposure from CT rather than cumulative exposure across the full range of medical imaging modalities using ionizing radiation," Miglioretti and colleagues pointed out.

The current study included children born in the U.S. or Ontario, Canada, from 1996 through 2016, and captures imaging histories from birth during a more contemporary era of pediatric imaging. The researchers estimated organ-specific radiation dose from CT, radiography, fluoroscopy, angiography, and nuclear medicine.

A previous analysis of this cohort evaluated hematologic cancer risk and found that up to 10% of blood cancers in children may result from radiation exposure associated with imaging.

The cohort included 3,704,860 children (51.3% male) with 45,671,641 person-years of follow-up (mean follow-up 12.3 years); 82.6% were followed until study end or age 21 years.

A total of 1,536 brain tumors were diagnosed. Of these cases, 55.7% were in males, and 64.5% were diagnosed between the ages of 2 and 10 years. Most tumors (82.5%) were classified as having aggressive histology.

Cumulative dose at the end of follow-up was 1 mGy or greater in 6.8% of all children and 7.9% of those who developed a brain tumor. Overall, 4.4% of all children and 5.9% of those who developed a brain tumor had a cumulative dose of 50 mGy or greater, and 1.1% and 2.3%, respectively, had a cumulative dose of 100 mGy or greater.

Before the end of follow-up, 4.3% of children had one head CT scan, 0.9% had two, and 0.4% had three or more. Among children with a brain tumor, 1.6% had two head CT scans and 1.2% had three or more.

Miglioretti and colleagues acknowledged their study had several limitations, including the fact that it could not account for cancer-predisposing syndromes, and that it did not capture radiation exposure from dental radiographs or cone beam CT.

#5868. enSights brings AI-powered business management to solar and storage operations

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 16, 2026

As solar and energy storage portfolios grow in both size and complexity, managing performance is just one part of the equation. Owners and operators must also keep track of power purchase agreements (PPAs), warranties, service contracts, billing, compliance requirements and project-level profitability.

enSights is looking to bring those responsibilities together with the launch of its AI Studio, a new addition to its Energy Business Management (EBM) platform. The company announced the launch Nov. 10.

The AI Studio is designed to give solar and storage owners, operators, and asset managers access to a customizable team of AI specialists that can support business and operational workflows. Through no-code tools, users can create AI dashboards and reports using natural-language instructions, while still establishing their own workflows, routines, data-access permissions and approval requirements.

The approach here reflects a broader shift toward renewable energy software, it’s moving beyond monitoring individual assets toward managing the financial and operational performance of entire portfolios.

According to enSights, its EBM platform brings together energy management systems (EMS), production data, billing, PPA information, operations and maintenance, contractual obligations and compliance on a unified data foundation. That integration is central to the company’s AI strategy.

Rather than limiting AI applications to identifying equipment issues or answering generation-related questions, the platform is designed to connect energy production with the contracts and financial obligations associated with each project.

The AI Studio includes an orchestrator that coordinates specialized AI agents focused on areas like monitoring, operations, revenue, warranties, permits, lenders, insurers, and asset management. For portfolio managers, those capabilities could help reduce the time spent reviewing disconnected systems and manually reconciling information across departments. Automated reviews of contractual obligations, revenue performance and operational alerts could also help teams identify issues earlier and direct attention to matters that still require human intervention.

The enSights AI Studio dashboard provides solar and energy storage operators with AI-powered tools for asset management, operational workflows, and financial oversight. Credit: enSights

enSights emphasized that people remain in control of the AI Team. Users determine which workflows the agents handle and where approvals are required. Physical asset controls remain governed by established EMS and site-level systems.

The AI Studio also builds on an expanded financial management offering within the EBM platform. Its Financial Asset Management Module includes revenue assurance, contract, and obligation management, and profit-and-loss reporting.

The module is intended to provide project-level financial visibility, helping companies identify and prevent revenue losses while generating reports for investors.

The platform also includes real-time EMS, weather and tariff forecasting, along with dispatch planning intended to optimize revenue while maintaining grid safety and local compliance.

enSights says its platform is deployed globally across more than 100,000 sites, representing over 5 GW of assets. The company plans to demonstrate the AI Studio and its broader EBM platform at RE+ 2026.

As AI gains traction across the clean energy industry, the next competitive advantage could come from connecting operational intelligence with the financial and contractual decisions that determine how successfully renewable energy portfolios perform.

#5863. The grid will fall short of AI’s power needs. These 6 resources can close the gap.

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

No single option is fast, firm and large enough on its own, writes Simon Irish, CEO of Terrestrial Energy.

Simon Irish is CEO of Terrestrial Energy, a developer of small modular nuclear reactors.

Most planning for artificial intelligence’s electricity demand starts with a forecast and asks whether it is right.

I would start somewhere else: Assume the grid will fall short, then ask how to close the gap — and how fast.

The evidence supports that starting point. McKinsey & Company estimates the U.S. will need 30 GW to 55 GW of additional capacity by 2030 as data center demand grows and aging plants retire. PJM Interconnection, the largest U.S. grid operator, has already seen the impacts of this trend. Its latest capacity auction fell about 6.6 GW short of its reliability requirement and cleared at the price cap.

Forecasts can be wrong, and requested load can be speculative or duplicative. But the PJM result is a market outcome, not a forecast. The useful question is which tools can close the gap, by when and at what cost.

The first four options below address serving data centers through the grid. The final two consider generation at or near the data center, with an optional grid connection. These categories overlap; on-site generation is a delivery strategy, not a generation technology, and can use multiple technologies.

Flexible demand. Duke University’s Nicholas Institute estimates the grid could absorb 76 GW of new load if that load accepted curtailment averaging 0.25% of annual consumption, in events of about 1.7 hours. It is the fastest option because it needs no new generation. It makes better use of capacity already on the grid.

But that capacity is finite, and beyond it, further load growth requires new supply. There is also a practical hurdle; to date, many data centers have shown little willingness to curtail AI workloads, because the value of computing far exceeds the cost of power.

Existing nuclear plants. Increasing output at operating plants through uprates and restarting closed plants can add reliable electricity supply, while extending operating licenses keeps existing plants running. A U.S. Department of Energy initiative targets 5 GW of added capacity at existing nuclear plants by 2029, and DOE has closed a $1.9 billion loan to restart Iowa’s Duane Arnold plant, returning 615 MW to service.

The limit is opportunity: Few operating plants remain candidates for significant uprates, and restarts are underway at nearly every plant where one is practical.

Natural gas. Gas offers firm power and a familiar path, but gas turbine supply has become the constraint. GE Vernova’s gas turbine backlog and slot reservations reached 116 GW at midyear 2026, and the company has said a heavy-duty turbine ordered today will not arrive until 2031. Buyers also take on fuel-price exposure and decades of emissions.

Wind, solar and storage. These resources can be deployed quickly and at scale. Their limit is that data centers require dependable power around the clock, so matching that requirement takes substantial overbuilding, energy storage and transmission infrastructure to deliver the same capacity as a firm resource.

On-site generation. Building a power plant at a data center can bring electricity online sooner by avoiding some grid upgrades and connection delays, where permitted.

However, it does not speed up equipment deliveries. An on-site gas turbine plant faces the same turbine shortages as one supplying the grid. Reliable operation also requires sufficient on-site backup capability or access to grid power during outages and maintenance. If a project relies on the grid, developers need clear terms defining that access and its cost.

Advanced nuclear energy. Advanced reactors offer firm, clean power and can be built as a fleet. They can serve data centers directly, with an optional grid connection. They will not close the gap this decade, and their real test is cost at fleet scale.

No single option is fast, firm and large enough on its own. Three principles follow.

Flexible demand, uprates at existing nuclear plants, and new wind, solar and storage can help meet near-term electricity needs.

Natural gas and on-site generation can help fill the gap while longer-term resources are developed, but only if equipment deliveries, permits and grid interconnection align with the data center’s schedule.

Advanced nuclear energy can contribute in the 2030s and beyond.

Utilities must plan now for both natural gas and advanced nuclear energy. DOE’s long-term modeling indicates 550 to 770 GW of additional firm, clean generating capacity could be needed by 2050, with advanced nuclear energy providing about 200 GW.

Infrastructure built to meet an immediate shortfall should also have a useful role later. A gas plant could provide firming capacity or operate during periods of peak demand as longer-term supply becomes available.

Make flexibility the price of speed. Utilities can offer earlier interconnection to customers accepting curtailment under defined, metered and enforceable terms. For data center operators, that is a choice between some power sooner and firm power later. Customers that can curtail load may get power sooner; those that cannot must wait for firm supply. This converts flexibility from an assumption into a contract and rewards developers whose flexibility is real.

Secure the long-lead items for firm, clean power now. Buyers who reserved gas turbine production slots early secured earlier deliveries; those ordering now may have to wait until 2031.

Firm, clean power has its own long-lead items, including fuel supply and equipment manufacturing capacity. Utilities and data center operators can sign reservation or offtake agreements that give suppliers the demand signal to build capacity before it is needed.

These principles also address the forecast debate. Some investments — flexibility, uprates and transmission — remain valuable across demand scenarios. Others are options worth buying, such as reservations for firm supply in the 2030s and beyond.

And some are bets to avoid: irreversible commitments sized to a demand number that may not arrive. If forecasts prove too high, the cost is the price paid to preserve an option. If they prove right and no one bought the option, the cost is the kind of shortfall PJM has already seen.

Planning for a shortfall is not pessimism. Of all the inputs AI needs, reliable power may prove the scarcest. The race to scale AI is a race to deliver power. Utilities, regulators and developers need tools that arrive on time, hold their value if forecasts change, and keep electricity reliable and affordable for existing customers.

In North America, data centers are boosting the long-term investment case for energy storage and renewables as gas turbine backlogs and rising fuel volatility drive up peaking costs, according to a note from Wood Mackenzie.

NERC’s Board of Trustees on Monday voted to adopt reliability standards for AI data centers and other computational loads. The proposed rules now go to the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission for approval.

#5862. Blood Test May Detect CTE During Life, Study Shows

— "This is a game changer," leading CTE researcher says

Detecting chronic traumatic encephalopathy (CTE) in living patients may soon be possible, according to preliminary data from a case series.

CTE, a neurodegenerative disease associated with repetitive head impacts from contact sports, currently can be diagnosed only at autopsy.

In a study of 21 former American football players who had donated their brains, however, blood levels of endogenously cleaved microtubule-binding region tau (eMTBR-tau) were higher in CTE-positive participants than in CTE-negative participants (β=1.61, 95% CI 0.51-2.70), reported Annalise Miner, a PhD candidate working on research under Michael Alosco, PhD, of Boston University, at the Clinical Trials on Alzheimer's Disease conference in Boston.

Concentrations of eMTBR-tau were highest in former players who also had Alzheimer's pathology at autopsy. Levels were intermediate in those with high-stage CTE who did not have Alzheimer's pathology, Miner said.

Players with high-stage CTE had higher eMTBR-tau concentrations than those with low-stage or no CTE, she added. Blood levels of phosphorylated tau 217 (p-tau217) were negative or intermediate in CTE cases, except in athletes with comorbid Alzheimer's disease.

The study was published simultaneously in JAMA.

"This is a game changer," co-author Alosco said in a statement. "We are still in the discovery phase and it's a small sample size, but if future studies validate these findings, this blood test will allow us to detect CTE with a high level of accuracy and open the doors to clinical trials and treatment."

The findings mirrored those of a case-control study led by Washington University in St. Louis, which was presented at a scientific meeting in Japan last week and also published in JAMA. That study showed plasma eMTBR-tau243 was elevated in late-stage CTE and some other tauopathies.

Both eMTBR-tau and eMTBR-tau243 measure the same key biological features, Miner noted. "Recent findings demonstrate a strong correlation between the immunoassay-derived eMTBR-tau and the mass spectrometry-derived eMTBR-tau243, supporting the biological relevance of this immunoassay," she said.

CTE remains a major diagnostic challenge, noted Carla Palleis, MD, and Günter Höglinger, MD, both of LMU University Hospital in Munich. "A biomarker capable of identifying CTE-associated tau pathology could improve selection of appropriate participants for research studies, facilitate clinicopathological studies, and ultimately support the evaluation of preventive and therapeutic interventions," they wrote in a JAMA editorial.

Detecting CTE at an early stage could be particularly valuable when clinical features seem nonspecific, the editorialists pointed out. Distinguishing traumatic encephalopathy syndrome -- a clinical syndrome associated with CTE -- from other causes of cognitive or behavioral symptoms can be difficult.

"Future studies should determine whether eMTBR-tau improves the ability of the criteria for traumatic encephalopathy syndrome to distinguish it and identify individuals with underlying CTE neuropathology, particularly in the presence of Alzheimer's disease copathology," Palleis and Höglinger wrote.

Over several years, eMTBR has emerged as blood biomarker for Alzheimer's disease. A 2025 study showed that eMTBR-tau243 was more strongly associated with tau tangle pathology in Alzheimer's than other established blood biomarkers. More recent work showed that blood tests measuring p-tau217 and eMTBR-tau243 improved triage and reduced overdiagnosis in people with asymptomatic Alzheimer's pathology.

Of the 21 former American football players included in the new study, 12 played professionally, seven played in college, and two played at the high school level. Eighteen former players had an autopsy diagnosis of CTE; of these, 16 had high-stage CTE (stage III or IV) and two had low-stage CTE (stage I or II). Five CTE-positive participants had intermediate or high Alzheimer's disease neuropathology.

Most participants were white and were diagnosed with traumatic encephalopathy syndrome cognitive impairment, Miner noted.

This work represents a first step toward a CTE biomarker framework, she observed. "By combining plasma eMTBR tau with Alzheimer's biomarkers like p-tau217, we may be able to capture complementary information that helps us move closer to diagnosing CTE during life," Miner said.

#5861. Senate Republicans Seek to Again Raid States’ Coffers & Threaten EV Charging

FY 2027 Transportation Proposal Puts EV Charging Investments at Risk

WASHINGTON, D.C. — On Monday, Senate Republicans released an Appropriations package that proposes to take back nearly $2 billion in federal funding already allocated to states to build out electric vehicle (EV) charging infrastructure under the Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act and repurpose it for general transportation uses, putting critical clean transportation investments at risk.

Republicans already clawed back roughly $500 million from the National Electric Vehicle Infrastructure (NEVI) Formula Program in the FY 2026 appropriations bill. Now, they are proposing to further divert these funds, even as all 50 states, Washington, D.C., and Puerto Rico have approved plans for their use and are working to deliver critical charging infrastructure to their communities. More than half of available NEVI funding has already been committed to projects, and the proposal threatens to upend additional awards that states continue to make to charging developers.

For example, Texas recently made an announcement of $226 million in awards that will support 321 stations. The Senate proposal would threaten Texas’s remaining NEVI funding, along with funding for states like Ohio, which has one of the largest numbers of operational NEVI-funded stations in the country.

In response, Clean Transportation for All Director Katherine García released the following statement:

“It is unacceptable for Senate Republicans to once again seek to claw back critical EV charging investments. Trump’s day-one freeze of NEVI set states back, and they are only now back on track after we successfully challenged that freeze in court. This latest proposal threatens affordable transportation for American families, clean air for communities, and states’ efforts to build the infrastructure of the future. We have successfully defended NEVI against the Trump administration’s attacks, and we will continue fighting to protect the program and ensure these investments reach communities across the country.”

The NEVI program, established through the Bipartisan Infrastructure Law in 2021, provides funding to states to build a nationwide network of EV fast-charging stations. Protecting that investment is essential to expanding charging access, supporting EV adoption, reducing dependence on volatile gasoline prices, and cutting transportation pollution.

Since the Trump administration’s unlawful freeze of the NEVI program was lifted following successful litigation by Sierra Club, its partners, and a coalition of states, hundreds of millions of dollars in NEVI funding have been committed to charging projects.

Congress must preserve funding for EV charging infrastructure while providing dedicated resources for passenger rail, public transit, and other clean transportation priorities.

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5860. Soneil Spark introduces its Mobile Battery Energy Storage System

This week, Soneil Spark rolls out its Mobile Battery Energy Storage System (Mobile BESS), the portable, modular battery storage built into a towable trailer.

The company, offering bespoke, modular energy solutions, is introducing Mobile BESS at RE+ 2026. It delivers temporary backup and off-grid power, working with what’s available by taking power from the grid, a generator, or solar.

“Wherever your business goes, the power goes with it. Simply tow it where energy demand grows,” said Sonya Jain, CEO of Soneil Spark, in a statement. “Start with one unit and add more as your needs change.”

It’s a simple set up, the company said. Mobile BESS works with no crane, no trenching, and no utility wait. That makes it a natural fit for a job site which may be months away from permanent service, a film set or event that can’t tolerate generator noise and fumes, a utility crew restoring power after a storm, or a remote mine or farm beyond the grid’s reach.

On sites that already run generators, the Mobile BESS syncs with them to cover peaks and start-up gaps, so the generators run fewer hours. Where diesel is restricted, it offers a quiet alternative.

Where containerized storage is built to be placed once, the Mobile BESS is built to be towed and to work in any weather. It includes:

The Mobile BESS is built for generator and equipment rental, construction, emergency and backup power, utilities, electrical contractors, events, film and entertainment, and off-grid sites such as mining and agriculture. A hybrid option adds EV charging.

#5859. New York state is short electric resources beginning 2033: ISO

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

New York will face an approximately 225 MW statewide resource deficiency, with the need for power resources growing to 1,850 MW by 2036, the state’s grid operator said Wednesday.

New York ISO’s 2026 Reliability Needs Assessment reveals the state’s increasing reliance on emergency operating procedures to maintain grid stability, highlighting the imminent resource shortfall. The ISO said it expects to call on demand response, voltage reductions, operating reserves, load curtailment and other measures, approximately 3.5 days next year, with that total growing to 21 days by 2036.

“New York’s reliability needs are no longer a distant concern,” Zach Smith, senior vice president of system and resource planning, said in a statement. “New generation and transmission resources take years to plan, permit, and build, so the reliability needs identified in this report must be addressed today.”

Along with the statewide resource deficiency, the report also identified local reliability needs. New York City is projected to be deficient by approximately 400 MW in 2032, growing to approximately 1,850 MW by 2036, while the Lower Hudson Valley deficiency grows from approximately 130 MW in 2032 to approximately 1,950 MW in 2036.

“While transmission investments can help address local reliability needs ... they cannot resolve the statewide resource shortfall,” the ISO said. “A combination of new generation, repowered resources, energy storage, dependable demand reductions, flexible large loads, and additional transmission will be required to maintain reliability over the next decade.”

NYISO expects demand growth of about 1,000 MW from manufacturing facilities and 1,700 MW from data centers will contribute to the shortfall. “The age and availability” of the state’s generation fleet is also a growing reliability concern, the grid operator said.

Approximately 4,600 MW of fossil generation will be more than 70 years old by 2036, according to the report. The assessment “accounts for this risk by projecting approximately 2,600 MW of generation as unavailable by 2036,” it said.

The ISO said it will continue to monitor the situation quarterly, reporting on the issue through its short-term reliability processes.

Once the 2026 RNA is approved by the NYISO board of directors, the grid operator said it will request updates to the base case “that may reduce or eliminate the identified reliability needs,” including changes in the status of generation and transmission projects, demand forecasts, demand response resources, large load assumptions and other system developments.

“If reliability needs remain after those updates, the NYISO will solicit and evaluate market-based solutions, regulated backstop solutions, and alternative regulated solutions” through the development of a 10-year reliability plan, the operator said.

#5857. Fuel constraints could challenge grid this winter, despite resource improvements: NERC

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Some areas in the Midwest, Southeast and New England could face resource shortages if transmission and pipeline constraints occur during periods of extreme weather, the reliability watchdog said.

Electric demand is growing, but “there has also been strong growth in resources,” Mark Olson, manager of reliability assessments, said Wednesday in a call discussing the outlook. “In many ways, the industry is on better footing going into winter this year,” he said.

However, Olson added that “historically, winter is the toughest operating period for the North American electric grid.”

While some models show the El Niño weather pattern can bring milder-than-average conditions that are a help to a constrained power system, “the reality is that extreme storms with ice and abnormal cold temperatures can lie within those averages,” Olson said.

“We're talking to our industry stakeholders about the need for extra vigilance. El Niño is a variable that adds to uncertainty for the winter,” he said.

Peak winter electric heating demand often coincides with higher residential gas usage, potentially leaving single-fuel generators that don’t have long-term gas contracts unable to show up when their generation is most needed, NERC officials said.

In the Northeast Power Coordinating Council’s Québec and Maritimes regions, “the supply can become strained during extreme winter peak demand,” NERC Senior Engineer Bob Tallman said on the call. “New England, as we've identified in the past several years, is assessed as elevated [risk] due to the constrained or limited gas pipeline infrastructure in that region.”

In the SERC Reliability Corp. East area, resources can fall short of extreme peak demand “during morning and low solar output periods of the day,” Tallman added.  And in the Southwest Power Pool and Midcontinent Independent System Operator territories, NERC identified subregions “where there are localized transmission constraints and it could be difficult to import into those areas if there was a wide-area winter event limiting local generation and increasing their reliance on imports.”

#5856. Two Tuberculosis Regimens Containing Novel Agents Show Promise

— Phase II platform trial's design could speed development of new combinations for TB

Two four-drug regimens that combined new agents with commonly used drugs showed promise in tuberculosis (TB), and should be evaluated further, results from a phase II trial suggested.

The time to positivity slope -- the rate of change from baseline to week 12 in the time to Mycobacterium tuberculosis growth in a positive sputum culture, with a steeper time indicating faster TB clearance -- was 0.129 log10 (days) per week with investigational ganfeborole plus bedaquiline, pretomanid, and moxifloxacin (BPMG) and 0.142 log10 (days) per week with investigational BTZ-043 plus bedaquiline, delamanid, and moxifloxacin (BDMT), as compared with 0.118 log10 (days) per week with standard isoniazid, rifampin, pyrazinamide, and ethambutol (HRZE), for relative differences of 9.1% and 20.5%, respectively.

The posterior probability of superiority was 0.83 and 0.99 with the BPMG and BDMT regimens versus HRZE, reported Nicholas Paton, MD, of National University of Singapore, and colleagues in the New England Journal of Medicine.

At week 48, 80% of those in the BPMG group and 93% of those in the BDMT group had favorable-outcome status compared with 89% of the HRZE group.

"Our findings show the potential of large-scale, early-phase clinical trials for evaluating multiple regimens simultaneously with a design that permits the use of early data on treatment failure or relapse to inform selection of regimens for further evaluation," Paton and colleagues wrote.

The four-drug combinations will move on to testing in the phase II trial's second stage.

In an accompanying editorial, Eric Rubin, MD, PhD, of the Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health in Boston, cautioned that the number of new TB agents in the development pipeline "creates a combinational nightmare."

This study outlines "a potentially very useful approach to prioritizing which regimens should be tested in very long and expensive phase III trials," Rubin noted. "The use of phase II trials with a design similar to that of the current trial might get some of these new agents into later-phase clinical trials more rapidly."

Despite the encouraging results, it's not clear whether they will lead to improved TB outcomes, he added. "Although almost all drug combinations can kill the bulk of bacteria, a small subpopulation seems to be responsible for the lengthy regimens that are necessary to cure all patients," Rubin wrote. The trial's time-to-positivity results "most likely tell us more about the bulk population than about the rarer bacteria that might survive for extended times."

While the current trial "provides useful guidelines for prioritizing late-phase trials," those later trials will still be critical, he pointed out. Referencing the movie "The Princess Bride," Rubin reminded investigators and clinicians alike that, "as Miracle Max says, 'You rush a miracle man, you get rotten miracles.'"

The PARADIGM-4TB adaptive platform trial assigned participants with rifampin-susceptible pulmonary TB to receive standard HRZE for 24 weeks; bedaquiline, delamanid, and moxifloxacin for 16 weeks; or one of 10 new four-drug combinations for 16 weeks. The new regimens contained ganfeborole, BTZ-043, or both, with combinations of bedaquiline, delamanid, moxifloxacin, pyrazinamide, pretomanid, and linezolid.

Ganfeborole blocks an enzyme essential to mycobacterial protein synthesis, while BTZ-043 inhibits an essential enzyme in cell-wall synthesis.

The study enrolled 401 adults at 14 clinical sites in South Africa, Tanzania, Uganda, Vietnam, and Moldova. Patients had TB symptoms or chest radiography evidence, as well as a medium or high semiquantitative result on the Xpert MTB/RIF Ultra assay. Patients with HIV were excluded. Median patient age was 30 years, 84% were male, 68% had a high bacterial load, and 77% had cavitation on a chest radiograph.

Severe adverse events occurred in 38% of the BPMG group, 28% of the BDMT group, and 24% of the HRZE group. Among the trial's 118 patients who had a serious adverse event, 45% had a decrease from baseline in creatinine clearance, "which contributed to excess severe adverse events in some new-regimen groups," Paton and team noted. Most of those patients had creatinine clearance fall by more than 30%.

Study limitations included the open-label design and the small sizes of each treatment group. Time to positivity is a surrogate endpoint that hasn't always predicted clinical endpoints, the authors cautioned. While the study population included only those with rifampin-susceptible TB, any treatments' target clinical population would include patients with rifampin-resistant TB.

#5855. REPORT: U.S. Exceeds 200 GWh of Energy Storage Capacity Installed to Date

Rapid Growth in Energy Storage Is Helping Stabilize Electricity Prices and Strengthen Grid Reliability; Industry Urges Congress to Pass Permitting Reform to Accelerate Deployment

LAS VEGAS — The U.S. energy storage industry installed 14.3 gigawatt-hours (GWh) of new capacity in Q3, bringing total installations in the first nine months of 2026 to 48.3 GWh, according to the U.S. Energy Storage Market Outlook Q4 2026 (ESMO) released today by the Solar Energy Industries Association (SEIA) and Benchmark Mineral Intelligence.

U.S. energy storage capacity installed has now exceeded 200 GWh to date as grid operators, utilities, and energy buyers turn to storage to lower energy costs and strengthen grid reliability. After voters sent a clear message in the midterm elections that lowering energy costs must be a priority, the U.S. solar and storage industry is urging lawmakers to send the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act (BAAJA) to the President’s desk.

Last week, more than 300 solar and storage companies sent a letter to Congressional leadership urging swift passage of BAAJA. The legislation will accelerate energy storage deployment, reduce project costs, and expand access to lower-cost, reliable electricity for households and businesses.

“Voters made it clear that they want lower prices, and the growing deployment of energy storage is helping make that happen. We’re urging Congress to pass BAAJA to build out America’s energy infrastructure and put downward pressure on prices,” said SEIA CEO Tim Pawlenty.

The outlook for energy storage continues to strengthen, with the report increasing its 2030 cumulative U.S. deployment forecast by 3.7% to 708 GWh across grid and BTM. The revised forecast reflects sustained industry momentum, with U.S. installations up 20% year over year through the first nine months of 2026.

Texas led the nation in utility-scale storage deployment through Q3, adding 12.3 GWh of capacity. Arizona and California followed with 7.7 GWh and 6.8 GWh, respectively. Recent SEIA analysis shows that rapid growth in solar and energy storage helped drive down average wholesale electricity prices by 42% in Texas and 51% in California from summer 2021 to summer 2026.

11 states surpassed their previous annual energy storage installation records in Q3, even with three months remaining in 2026: Colorado, Massachusetts, Utah, Michigan, Georgia, Mississippi, Virginia, Iowa, Montana, Alabama, and Illinois.

“States are moving from targets to centralized procurements,” said Pete Tillotson, Senior Analyst at Benchmark Minerals. “Across utility mandates and bulk capacity contracts, energy storage pipelines are growing as a result of both investment appetite and flexibility requirements.”

U.S. storage manufacturing continues to accelerate, ensuring that more of the batteries being deployed to strengthen the grid are made in the United States. In July, a new battery cell manufacturing facility opened in Tennessee. In October, a new cell manufacturing facility opened in Indiana, bringing total cell manufacturing capacity to 92.4 GWh, a 50% increase.

Utility-scale projects accounted for the vast majority of Q3 installations, with 12.2 GWh deployed, followed by 1.3 GWh of commercial and industrial storage, and 746 MWh of residential storage.
