#5854. PJM urged to delay base capacity auction until data centers cut from forecast

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

State ratepayer advocates and the grid operator’s market monitor say holding the auction in December as planned threatens grid reliability and could hit consumers with billions in unnecessary costs.

The request from the ratepayer advocates comes amid nationwide concern about energy affordability and data centers, as reflected in the midterm elections. The letter says that given “the urgency of the approaching auction,” its signatories request a written response from PJM’s board no later than Nov. 23.

In PJM, affordability concerns have been driven by a recent spike in capacity prices caused by growing data center load forecasts. PJM’s current capacity market rules effectively prevent data center companies from paying for their own power supply and infrastructure costs.

PJM recognizes the financial impacts rising electricity bills place on households and businesses, Jeffrey Shields, a spokesman for the grid operator, told Utility Dive in an email.

“The December auction includes an established price cap, providing an important safeguard against how high prices can rise,” he said. “The system urgently needs capacity to meet rising demand. Postponing the auction this close to its execution could destabilize the market and increase the risk of power interruptions when customers need electricity most.”

Electricity demand is growing faster than new power supplies can be built, according to Shields.

“While decisions about whether data centers and other large electricity users can connect to the grid rest with policymakers and utilities, PJM must plan for the demand those decisions create.” Shields said. “That means moving forward with securing the power we need while continuing to advance solutions that address the challenges of growing demand and the pressure on electricity costs.”

So far, including existing and forecast data center demand in PJM’s capacity market has increased capacity costs by $29.4 billion for all customers, according to Monitoring Analytics’ Nov. 12 report that called for the auction to be postponed.

PJM has responded to pressure from states, federal regulators and consumers by proposing a two-part plan to meet data center demand by facilitating bilateral contracts outside its normal capacity auctions, and also holding a backstop procurement for large loads that do not have bilateral contracts. It is also pursuing reforms to its rules for interconnecting large loads and generating supplies. The status of these efforts is pending.

The market monitor’s concerns were amplified by Exelon’s Nov. 3 complaint at FERC that challenged PJM’s three-year forward auction, partly over the uncertainty in forecasting data center load and the resulting billions in potential excess capacity costs, the ratepayer advocates said.

The advocates noted that PJM plans to publish the final “planning parameters” for the upcoming auction, which may remove canceled or delayed data center projects from the auction’s demand curve, on Nov. 19.

However, the market monitor’s recommendation is not simply to update PJM’s load forecast, they added.

“It is to fundamentally change how forecasted data-center demand is treated in the capacity auction so that existing consumers are not required to subsidize speculative or uncommitted data center demand,” the advocates said. “The delay would allow PJM to implement the IMM recommendations, subject to FERC approval and appropriate reliability safeguards.”

A “statement of principles” endorsed in January by PJM and the governors of its 13 states emphasized that large-load forecasts must be “real and verified,” the ratepayer advocates said. “Proceeding with an auction that incorporates both real and speculative data-center demand, despite the [market monitor’s] findings, would undermine that commitment and expose consumers to billions in potentially avoidable costs.”

The letter was sent to PJM by the Office of the Ohio Consumers’ Counsel, the Delaware Public Advocate, the West Virginia Consumer Advocate, the Maryland Office of People’s Counsel, the Pennsylvania Office of Consumer Advocate, the New Jersey Division of Rate Counsel, the Illinois Attorney General’s Office and the Illinois Citizens Utility Board.

#5853. Third Pillar Constructing Largest Floating Solar Complex in New York

Crown City Solar will be North America’s first floating community solar project. Phases 1 and 2 are under construction, and the three-phase, 22.5 MW DC complex is expected to be fully operational in 2027.

CORTLANDVILLE, N.Y. — Third Pillar Power today announced it is constructing Crown City Solar, North America’s first floating community solar project, at Suit-Kote Corporation’s Polkville Crushed Stone Facility, an active sand and gravel mining operation co-located with an asphalt plant and storage terminal in Cortlandville, N.Y.

Crown City Solar 1 is the first of three 7.5 MW-dc phases and is expected to begin commercial operation in February 2027. Crown City Solar 2 and Crown City Solar 3 are scheduled to begin commercial operation in October 2027, bringing the site to 22.5 MW-dc in total and making Crown City Solar North America’s largest floating solar complex. The complex takes its name from nearby Cortland, known locally as the Crown City because it sits on an elevated plain over 1,000 feet above sea level.

“Every quarry and reservoir in this country already has a job to do. Crown City Solar shows that those sites can take on a second one: powering their neighbors through a Central New York winter, at a community scale, on a working industrial site. The question is no longer whether floating solar works here. It’s how quickly we can bring it to the next site, like Polkville, and the one after that,” said Jaimeet Gulati, CEO of Third Pillar Power.

Third Pillar identified the approximately 120-acre flooded mine pit as an ideal host for the project in early 2024. Thanks to the collaborative efforts of Suit-Kote, the Town of Cortlandville, and Cortland County, the project team brought this first-of-its-kind project to construction less than two years after securing site control and applying for interconnection. Suit-Kote was acquired by Colas USA, the U.S. subsidiary of Colas, in October 2026.

Crown City Solar also represents a series of technical firsts. It is engineered for the conditions of a Central New York winter, including a ground snow load of 69 pounds per square foot, the highest of any floating solar project in the country. Additionally, the project will float its medium-voltage transformers and switchgear on custom-designed barges—another first for a U.S. floating solar project. Floating this equipment further reduces the use of agricultural land, addresses floodplain siting concerns, and improves electrical efficiency. These firsts reflect Third Pillar’s specialization in floating solar, from identifying and engineering projects on working industrial water bodies to securing the permits and interconnection needed to bring them online.

D3Energy is serving as the engineering, procurement, and construction contractor. The floating system uses Alps’ Solar PV modules, Hazelett Marine’s specialized elastic mooring systems, and Profloating’s floats.

“With more than a decade of building floating solar projects across the country, D3Energy is proud to bring that experience to a project of this magnitude. Crown City Solar demonstrates what is possible with floating solar. We’re grateful for our partnership with Third Pillar Power and the trust they’ve placed in D3Energy to help bring North America’s largest floating solar complex to life,” said Stetson Tchividjian, Managing Director of D3Energy.

Crown City Solar 1 is expected to achieve mechanical completion in December 2026.

#5852. Grid operators need data center ride-through rules that protect reliability

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The first facilities built to Texas’ new standard are about to come online, proving that firm, predictable regulation can protect reliability without slowing development, says ON.energy’s Alan Cooper.

Alan Cooper is CEO of ON.energy, a developer of power infrastructure.

On July 22, a mechanical failure forced a 230-kV transmission line in Northern Virginia offline. Within milliseconds, grid protection systems cleared the fault, as they are designed to do. The event would not have been a particularly noteworthy story, had it stopped there. It’s what happened next, though, that pointed to a growing challenge for grid reliability.

In response to the fault, data centers that had been drawing nearly 4 GW of energy, close to 4% of PJM’s load, collectively disconnected and switched to their on-site backup generation. The move sent voltage and frequency spiking across the system, a technically wonky but threatening result that caused power flickers as far away as Chicago. Thankfully, the prevailing grid conditions were strong enough to absorb the shock of the fault, so the grid held. But if the same event had hit Texas or even Northern Virginia at a time of higher demand, it could have caused a widespread outage. In West Texas, the Electric Reliability Counil of Texas estimates that a loss of as little as 3.2 GW could threaten grid reliability.

The power sector is not unfamiliar with the need for things hooked up to the electricity grid to “ride through” disturbance events. In 2023, the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission told the North American Electricity Reliability Corp. to develop requirements that inverter-based supply resources like wind and solar facilities must follow to ensure they “ride through,” i.e., stay online, during certain disturbances. And this time around, NERC, FERC and several grid regions have been quick to identify a similar need for large loads to adhere to analogous standards.

In an Oct. 14 presentation to ERCOT, Dominion Virginia confirmed that the disturbance fell within the ride-through parameters that have now been finalized or proposed by NERC and two grid operators (ERCOT and PJM Interconnection). They concluded that if those requirements had been in place, the event would have been avoided.

Clear ride-through standards protect the grid, and they protect data centers’ ability to keep connecting to it. The open question for grid operators and regulators isn’t whether to create them, but rather which facilities the rules should cover. Some proposals would exempt projects that aren’t likely to draw grid power for years. Texas took a firmer line and in doing so, gave other operators and regulators an effective blueprint to follow.

The Public Utility Commission of Texas approved ERCOT’s ride through rule and it took effect in August. ERCOT made three choices that are key to making it work.

It drew the applicability line at energization. Facilities that energize roughly 18 months or more after ERCOT’s first market notice must comply with the rule. That exempts projects that are nearly finished, with equipment already ordered or even installed, and it applies to projects that exist only on paper, which can still change their electrical design for a small share of what a retrofit would cost later.

It measured the risk in exempt capacity within a cluster. ERCOT studies ride-through risk in its quarterly stability assessments. Those studies identify the clusters of large load that could trip together and the rule limits the amount of exempted load — i.e., the amount that is not equipped to ride through disturbances within each cluster — to 3,200 MW per single fault. Its latest results flag North Texas and Central Texas as active risk areas, with far West Texas being monitored.

It made the risk transparent and predictable. ERCOT watches each load cluster’s combined consumption in real time. When a cluster’s actual demand reaches about 2,500 MW, a mitigation plan kicks in: each utility and scheduling entity gets a “not to exceed” limit based on its share of recent peak use, and ERCOT keeps the authority to curtail non-compliant load. Because ERCOT publishes the study results and updates them over time, developers can see how much headroom is left long before they hit the limit. This transparency gives them time to decide whether to install ride-through capability, change their plans, or accept the risk of curtailment.

The result is that existing data centers keep running, and projects under construction generally avoid a forced redesign or need to buy different equipment. Other grid users stay protected. The first facilities built to the new standard will connect in the coming months.

In general, regulators and regional grid operators across the country are right to be careful about borrowing from ERCOT. Its isolated grid and energy-only market make much of its experience hard to apply elsewhere.

This isn’t one of those cases. A data center’s response to a voltage dip is the same in Virginia, Ohio or Texas, and so is the physics of thousands of megawatts disconnecting at once. NERC, PJM and other grid operators should adopt the core tenants of the Texas approach:

A single large-scale event would change the conversation overnight. Instead of debating exemption criteria, policymakers would be questioning if data centers can remain connected. Clear standards now are the best way to ensure the industry has a path forward.

The impact of a major event wouldn’t stop at the fence line: a multistate outage would affect the communities where data centers are concentrated and working to become good grid citizens. Proposed exceptions to ride-through requirements leave the door open for exactly the kind of event that consumers and policymakers are concerned about.

None of this protection is theoretical or depends on a technology breakthrough. Equipment to keep data centers connected through grid faults is commercially available today and already running in the field. The first facilities built to Texas’s new standard are about to come online, proving that a firm, predictable regulation can protect reliability without slowing market development. All too often, reliability problems are left lingering until there’s an emergency.

Large load ride through concerns can be solved now. The only thing missing is clear, consistent rules that protect grid reliability while providing certainty to the industry.

#5851. OMCO Solar offers solar-powered tracker control system

OMCO Solar now offers a version of its OMCO Star Tracker Control System powered entirely by solar energy, allowing tracker controls to operate without AC power at the site.

The system, first launched last year, is designed for OMCO Origin Factory-Direct Trackers. Its tracker controllers, array controllers and sensor bridges use all-weather battery packs charged by solar modules.

According to OMCO, the technology eliminates the need for AC power to tracker control components and an uninterruptible power supply. It also allows tracker controls to operate and protects modules against wind, snow and hail before AC power is available.

“Our industry has solved so many problems as we have grown – we’re now the leading source for new electric energy capacity in the US. This addition to the OMCO Star product line solves one more – how to protect modules if there’s no AC power to the site even for a short time. We’re excited to offer this solution to our existing customers and to new partners,” said Matt Kesler, vice president of solar technology at OMCO Solar, in a statement.

The OMCO Star system uses LoRa, or long-range, wireless technology with a communication range greater than 1 mile. According to OMCO, this reduces the number of network controllers required and allows direct communication with every tracker without relying on a mesh network.

The company says LoRa is 100 times less susceptible to electromagnetic noise than Zigbee and better able to connect to distant trackers despite obstacles.

OMCO says its all-weather battery packs allow the solar-powered controls to operate reliably in cold climates. The system includes field-replaceable batteries, a phone app that eliminates the need for buttons on controllers and a remote monitoring and control portal.

The portal allows operators to move trackers for activities such as mowing, snow plowing and module cleaning. The system is developed, manufactured and supported by OMCO Solar in the United States.

“We are excited about bringing this innovation to the Utility Scale and DG tracker markets. Over the past few years, OMCO has expanded its capabilities beyond just being a US steel manufacturer to now being a true technology leader in our space. We look forward to supporting our customers/partners with the OMCO Star Tracker Control System, improving reliability, and reducing the labor and materials needed to build their projects,” said Eric Goodwin, vice president of business development at OMCO Solar.

OMCO Solar will display the all-DC tracker control system at RE+ in Las Vegas, November 17–19, in the North Hall, booth N516.

#5850. The Harm From Plastic Is Happening In Plain Sight

It is known. Plastic is a major cause of the climate crisis, yet governments continue to ignore it.

The Environmental Justice Foundation (EJF) has issued a new briefing published to coincide with the UN Climate Change Conference, COP31, in Antalya, Türkiye. The briefing, “Reducing plastic production in a post-1.5°C world: A test of resolve for the international community,” calls on governments to reduce global plastic production.

Plastic already emits more than two billion tons of CO₂e a year, about twice as much as aviation, and under current policies this would more than double as the fossil fuel industry turns to plastic as its hedge against electrification.

“There’s only so much carbon the world can still emit if we want to avoid blasting past the Paris Agreement targets. Plastic’s harm to our climate is happening in plain sight, and governments are choosing to look away. Every new plastics plant locks in decades of emissions the planet simply cannot afford. As leaders gather at COP31, they must recognise plastic as a major cause of carbon pollution that is rapidly heating our world.” — Steve Trent, CEO, Environmental Justice Foundation

Over 90% of plastic emissions come before a plastic product is even used, during extraction, conversion and production. If current policies remain unchanged, global plastic production is projected to almost triple by 2060, and annual emissions from fossil-based plastics to more than double, to 4.3 billion tons of CO2e.

On this trajectory, plastics would emit 67 billion tons of CO2e between 2026 and 2050. That is just over half of the remaining carbon budget for a 50% chance of meeting the 1.5°C target, and 27% of the budget for a very good chance of limiting the overshoot to 1.7°C. None of this is hidden; emissions are quantified, the projections are published and the industry’s expansion plans are public, yet plastic remains on the margins of climate negotiations.

EJF is calling on governments at COP31 to recognize plastic for what it is: a major driver of climate change that must be tackled at its source, by cutting production.

The petrochemical industry’s bet on plastics

As the world electrifies transport and power, the fossil fuel industry is using plastic as its hedge against electrification. With oil demand for transport and energy expected to decline, petrochemical companies are pivoting to plastics to keep oil and gas flowing. The plastics sector could account for 20% of global oil consumption by 2050, up from around 12% today. The top fifteen petrochemical companies received an estimated US$590.8 billion in financing between 2019 and 2025, and the sector was propped up by state subsidies estimated at over $80 billion in 2024 alone, almost half of which came from Saudi Arabia.

A test of resolve for the plastics treaty

A total of 145 countries from every continent have expressed support for provisions to reduce plastic production during the global plastics treaty negotiations. Rwanda and Peru have proposed a global target to cut plastic production by 40% by 2040 against a 2025 baseline. Modelling cited in the briefing shows that a 40% cut in high-ambition countries, joined by China and emerging producers, could reduce global emissions from plastics by as much as 21% compared with business as usual.

But negotiations have reached a deadlock, driven by a small group of oil- and plastic-producing states, including Saudi Arabia, Russia, Iran and China, with the United States recently aligning with their position. At least 234 fossil fuel and chemical industry lobbyists attended the latest round of talks in Geneva — more than the combined delegations of the EU and its 27 Member States.

Final Thoughts about Plastic and our Leaders

The truth about plastics is perverse. Plastics are widely used around the world due to ease of manufacturing, low cost, stable chemical properties, and good water resistance. They have infiltrated every part of the environment and human life cycle. More than 16,000 chemicals are used in manufacturing plastics, and at least 4,200 of these have been identified as hazardous to human health and the environment. Thousands more chemicals used in plastic production have never been tested. Many of these chemicals migrate out of plastic packaging into our foods, drinks, household products, and bodies.

Researchers have come up with many possible solutions to plastic pollution, as well as ways to mitigate microplastics in the environment. But will governments have the wherewithal to stand up to the powerful fossil fuel lobby that is behind plastic propaganda?

Since joining the publication in 2016, Fortuna has authored nearly 1,900 articles focusing on ecojustice, clean technology adoption, and the intersection of science and public policy. #5849. Google signs 50-MW superhot geothermal PPA with Quaise Energy

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The agreement will support Google’s Oregon data center operations beginning as early as 2030, advancing development of a technology designed to access geothermal resources deeper underground.

Google's agreement with Quaise gives superhot geothermal an important commercial partner as developers seek to move enhanced geothermal systems from demonstration projects to utility-scale deployment.

“Geothermal energy remains largely underutilized. It supplies less than 0.5% of global electricity and is limited to just a handful of states in the U.S.,” Diane Hughes, senior vice president of marketing and communications at Quaise, said in an email.

Unlike conventional geothermal plants, which depend on naturally occurring underground hot-water reservoirs that are geographically constrained and rarely exceed 400 degrees Fahrenheit, enhanced geothermal uses engineered reservoirs to access heat in deeper rock formations.

Quaise is developing millimeter-wave drilling designed to ablate rock without mechanical contact. The company is designing wells to reach depths of up to 65,000 feet, where rock temperatures can exceed 932 degrees Fahrenheit and conventional drill bits cannot withstand the heat, according to Hughes.

“Superhot wells like these can produce up to 10x more power per well than what's currently in play and bring geothermal to places where it isn't viable or economical today," she said.

The agreement comes as technology companies increasingly seek firm power sources to meet data center demand. Google has signed nearly 35 GW of clean energy purchase agreements globally since 2010, including previous investments in next-generation geothermal, according to its most recent environmental report. Google says its three Oregon data center campuses are sourced with 83% carbon-free energy.

Google is also pursuing other emerging technologies, including advanced nuclear power and long-duration energy storage, as part of its efforts to achieve net-zero emissions.

Quaise said in its 2025 year-end update that it had drilled 118 meters through granite at its central Texas test site and is now working toward a depth of 1 kilometer.

#5848. Simple Lab Test May Predict Recurrence Risk in Most Common Type of Breast Cancer

— Higher eosinophil levels predicted better outcomes, better response to CDK4/6 inhibitors

Higher levels of circulating eosinophils had a significant association with improved recurrence-free survival (RFS) and distant metastasis-free survival (DMFS) in hormone receptor (HR)-positive breast cancer, a large retrospective analysis showed.

With an eosinophil cutoff of 0.08 × 109/L, patients with eosinophil counts above the cutoff had significantly better RFS (P=0.001) and DMFS (P=0.002). Higher levels of circulating eosinophils remained an independent predictor of improved outcomes in multivariable-adjusted statistical models. Reductions in the RFS and DMFS hazard ratios ranged between 50% and 60% before and after adjustment. The results were replicated in a large validation cohort of patients with HR-positive/HER2-negative breast cancer, albeit with smaller reductions in the hazard ratios.

Additionally, higher eosinophil counts predicted benefit from treatment with a CDK4/6 inhibitor, reported Zhi-Ming Shao, MD, PhD, of Fudan University Shanghai Cancer Center in China, and colleagues in the Journal of Clinical Oncology.

"Circulating eosinophil count is a robust prognostic biomarker, including during endocrine therapy," the authors stated. "Although the prognostic [hazard ratio] for eosinophil count differed between the discovery and expanded cohorts, this reflects differences in cohort design, case mix, and sample size; importantly, both estimates were directionally consistent, showing a protective effect."

"Second, [eosinophil count] provides significant additive prognostic value beyond the 21-gene risk score, suggesting that eosinophil levels in peripheral blood capture an immune dimension distinct from tumor genomics," they continued. "Third, eosinophils predict adjuvant CDK4/6 inhibitor efficacy, optimizing patient selection... . Importantly, the treatment-by-eosinophil interaction remained robust and the direction of effect was consistently protective across all analyses, supporting eosinophil count as a predictive biomarker regardless of absolute [hazard ratio] magnitude."

The findings add to previous studies showing that higher peripheral lymphocyte count and lower neutrophil-to-lymphocyte ratio are associated with better outcomes in early-stage breast cancer, primarily HR-negative subtypes, noted the authors of an accompanying editorial. Collectively, they highlight the biomarker potential of readily available laboratory parameters.

"None of these prognostic factors is in routine use, however, despite being readily available from a complete blood count for virtually every patient with breast cancer globally," wrote Peter Savas, MBBS, PhD, of Peter MacCallum Cancer Center in Melbourne, Australia, and Roberto Salgado, MD, PhD, of ZAS Hospitals in Antwerp, Belgium. "To achieve greater clinical relevance for circulating biomarkers, we can seek guidance from the success of risk-of-recurrence assays and the popularity of prognostic calculators such as PREDICT breast. These tools take a carefully considered approach to implementation, integrating standard clinicopathologic variables in a form that supports shared decision making and communication with patients."

"This should be the aim for any proposed biomarker that hopes to see widespread uptake, in addition to robust analytical and clinical validation."

HR-positive/HER2-negative breast cancer accounts for about two-thirds of all new cases of breast cancer, and despite treatment advances, late recurrence and distant relapse have persisted, the authors noted in their introduction. The absence of simple, noninvasive, blood-based biomarkers to monitor dynamic systemic immune status remains an unmet need. In particular, robust biomarkers to predict therapeutic benefit from CDK4/6 inhibitors have remained elusive, despite the drugs' established role in treating patients with HR-positive/HER2-negative breast cancer.

To date research into immune prognostic markers has focused primarily on tumor-infiltrating lymphocytes, ignoring the "prognostically relevant" myeloid compartment, the authors continued. To address the information void, investigators evaluated the prognostic potential of eosinophils, which previous studies have shown can prime antitumor T-cell responses. Investigators hypothesized that circulating eosinophil count could serve as a dynamic systemic marker of immune competence, distinct from tumor lymphoid infiltration.

Shao and colleagues retrospectively analyzed eosinophil counts in 472 patients with HR-positive/HER2-negative breast cancer treated with surgery and followed for a median of 83.1 months. The cohort had a median eosinophil count of 0.08 × 109/L. The cohort comprised 259 patients with eosinophil counts higher than the median and 213 with counts at the median or lower.

The data showed that patients with eosinophil counts exceeding the median had a 54% lower hazard for RFS (95% CI 0.28-0.77) and a 56% lower hazard for DMFS (95% CI 0.25-0.78). Multiple variable analyses produced hazard ratios of 0.49 (P=0.007) and 0.50 (P=0.017) for RFS and DMFS, respectively. Among 406 patients treated with endocrine therapy, higher eosinophil counts predicted better RFS (HR 0.41, 95% CI 0.24-0.70, P=0.001) and DMFS (HR 0.39, 95% CI 0.21-0.72, P=0.002).

To evaluate the functional impact of circulating eosinophil infiltration, the investigators implanted eosinophils into the mammary fat pads of mouse models of HR-positive/HER2-negative breast cancer. The results showed significantly reduced tumor growth in animals that received eosinophils versus those that did not.

Finally, Shao and colleagues investigated circulating eosinophils' association with RFS and DMFS in a validation cohort of 23,177 patients, 10,488 with eosinophil counts exceeding the median and 10,689 with values at or below the median. They had a median follow-up of 32.6 months. The high-eosinophil group had a statistically significant 17% lower risk of distant metastasis (95% CI 0.70-0.99, P=0.034). The association remained significant in a multivariable analysis that included age, nodal status, tumor size, and Ki67 value.

A separate analysis of 3,471 high-risk patients treated with adjuvant CDK4/6 inhibitors showed that the therapy improved DMFS overall, but patients with higher eosinophil counts derived greater benefit (HR 0.25, 95% CI 0.12-0.48, P<0.0001) than those with lower counts (HR 0.59, 95% CI 0.38-0.93, P=0.022).

#5847. From Social Leasing to Social Fleets: A Proposed Law Lends Credibility to the Goal of One Million Affordable Electric Cars

Social leasing could soon change in scale, name, and business model. The bill introduced by MP Christine Arrighi aims to create a public service offering one million “low-cost electric vehicles” (ALM). Scheduled for December 3rd in the National Assembly, its examination is expected to place at the heart of the debate a now crucial question: how to make social leasing sustainable and widespread?

Several presidential candidates have made reaching one million vehicles (or even more) one of the hallmarks of their campaigns¹. While this convergence is excellent news, the battle of figures says nothing about how to achieve this goal.

The current system cannot simply be scaled up twentyfold. Its cost per vehicle, intermittent financing, imperfect social targeting, lack of a guaranteed solution after three years, and the resale risks borne by dealerships are all structural limitations. The 2026 edition also revealed its dependence on Renault and Stellantis, which can at any time restrict volumes to favor more profitable traditional sales.

The proposed law changes its approach. Sponsored by MP Christine Arrighi, it draws inspiration from the proposals of T&E France and Bernard Julien, stemming from their study on the future of social leasing, published in June 2026.

In this new model, the government would directly purchase the vehicles at an average net cost of €15,000. These vehicles would be kept for twelve years in a public fleet and leased successively to one or more households for an average monthly rent of €150 before being resold. The day-to-day management of the fleet would be delegated to groups of public or private operators within the local areas.

The initial investment to build the fleet would be financed by borrowing, with a maximum outstanding amount of €7.8 billion in 2035. Lease payments and resales, supplemented by €350 million in annual public support (equivalent to the current social leasing budget), would cover service costs and repay the debt within eighteen years. By then, the system would be self-financing, and the state would own one million cars worth approximately €10 billion.

“This model is much more beneficial for public finances,” explains Diane Strauss, director of T&E France. “It requires an initial funding effort, but it eventually pays for itself. Instead of subsidizing at a loss, we build up an asset that generates its own revenue. This is what makes the move to a million vehicles financially viable.”

In addition to providing long-term protection for low-income motorists against rising and volatile fuel prices, the proposal represents a genuine industrial planning policy. The announcement of a public order for two million vehicles over twenty years (including fleet renewal) would indeed give the French automotive sector the visibility necessary to invest and prepare for the future.

“This will also allow us to direct our production towards the vehicles that households truly need: affordable, fuel-efficient, repairable electric cars, produced in France and Europe,” concludes Léo Larivière, automotive transition expert at T&E France. “This proposal must now open a substantive discussion among all stakeholders. It is high time to prepare the next steps for one of the rare examples of policies that reconcile climate, social justice, and industrial planning.”

¹ Raphaël Glucksmann and Marine Tondelier aim for one million vehicles. Jean-Luc Mélenchon proposes a public fleet of 1.4 million cars by 2032, and François Ruffin raises the target to three million. Gabriel Attal and Dominique de Villepin also advocate expanding social leasing, without specifying a target.

Transport & Environment’s (T&E) vision is a zero-emission mobility system that is affordable and has minimal impacts on our health, climate and environment. Created over 30 years ago, we have shaped some of Europe’s most important environmental laws.

#5846. Texas finalizes ERCOT CEO contract extension, pay raise tied to performance

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

CEO Pablo Vegas could take home more than $4 million next year. The contract extension and pay raise were put on hold in September following public outcry.

The Public Utility Commission of Texas on Thursday filed an order finalizing a roughly 7% base pay raise and potential performance-based bonuses worth millions more for Pablo Vegas, the CEO of the Electric Reliability Council of Texas.

PUCT officials said the contract is “in line with professional peers nationwide.”

The contract was signed in September but put on hold following public outcry, including objections from Texas Lt. Gov. Dan Patrick, who said on social media, “during a time of increasing utility costs, this is not the time to give the CEO of ERCOT a multi-million-dollar pay raise.”

Neither Patrick nor Texas Gov. Greg Abbott responded to a request for comment on the contract’s finalization. Both lost elections in November and will leave office in January.

ERCOT filings with the IRS show Vegas earned a total of about $3.6 million in 2024 and 2025, with a base salary above $1 million for each year.

His new contract could see him earn $6.47 million this year, according to a September presentation to the ERCOT board, though only about $4 million would be paid in 2027 with some potentially due later based on long-term incentives. The new contract runs from 2027 through 2032 and includes base pay of $1.16 million, plus an annual short-term performance incentive of up to 100% of his base salary and an annual long-term incentive up to 180% of his base pay. There is also a $1.38 million “make-whole” payment due in 2027, related to agreements in his previous contract.

The performance incentives are set by the ERCOT board. The compensation agreement “ensures that pay follows performance, where incentive compensation is earned each year only by meeting key grid reliability and performance targets and unpaid incentives are forfeited if the CEO is terminated for cause or resigns without good reason,” PUCT Chairman Thomas Gleeson said in a statement. “This is not a guaranteed payout.”

Vegas has served as president and CEO of the Texas grid operator since August 2022.

Nextnova celebrates the inauguration of its new solar cell manufacturing facility in Oklahoma City. Credit: Nextnova

#5844. Solar Insure Launches 20-Year Commercial Battery Warranty, Expanding Protection for Solar & Storage Projects

Launching at RE+ Las Vegas, November 17–20 | Visit Solar Insure at Booth C4190

Commercial property owners invest in solar and battery storage to manage energy costs and strengthen resilience. One question can shape that investment from the start. Who pays when equipment needs repairs years after installation?

Solar Insure today expanded its SI-Commercial Suite with two new warranties. SI-Commercial Battery is a 20-year limited warranty for commercial battery storage systems, and SI-Commercial Total covers solar and battery storage under one warranty. Together with the existing SI-Commercial Solar warranty, these additions allow Certified Providers to offer protection tailored to each project, whether it includes solar, battery storage, or both. Solar Insure protects more than 100,000 systems nationwide.

“Commercial projects are built for the long term, and their protection should reflect that,” said Ara Agopian, CEO of Solar Insure. “By expanding our commercial warranty suite, we’re helping solar companies offer protection that fits the systems their customers are investing in. It’s about giving property owners greater confidence not just in the installation, but in the years of ownership that follow.”

A failed inverter or declining battery capacity can lead to costs an owner did not budget for. Even when a manufacturer covers replacement equipment, the owner may still be responsible for labor and service costs. The expanded suite helps Certified Providers explain which repair expenses are covered so customers can evaluate their potential repair costs alongside system performance and installation price.

“Commercial battery owners are making a long-term investment, but some of the most significant expenses can emerge years after installation,” said Dean Chiaravallotti, Chief Revenue Officer at Solar Insure. “A battery replacement or unexpected repair can change the financial picture of a project. When providers can explain how they address those costs before the customer signs, they help buyers make a more informed investment decision. That’s the value we’re bringing to commercial storage.”

Solar companies must become Solar Insure Certified Providers to offer SI-Commercial warranties. Certification includes a review of the company’s qualifications, business practices, and commitment to customer service.

For qualifying claims, Solar Insure pays covered repair costs directly to the Certified Provider or service provider performing the work. If the original installer is no longer available, Solar Insure helps coordinate next steps, which may include identifying another qualified service provider. When a property changes hands, coverage transfers to the new owner at no cost.

“A lot can change over the life of a commercial energy system. Properties change hands, service providers change, and equipment eventually needs attention,” said Chiaravallotti. “Property owners need to know what happens w          hen those changes occur. Our goal is to give them a clearer path forward, with protection and support that extend well beyond the original installation.”

Solar companies can contact Solar Insure to learn about becoming a Certified Provider. Commercial property owners can speak with a Solar Insure Certified Provider about which warranty fits their project.

Solar Insure is a leading provider of solar and storage warranties, focused on protecting long-term system performance and elevating accountability across the renewable energy industry. Through rigorous provider certification and governance, Solar Insure delivers durable protection that residential and commercial installers can stand behind.

#5843. Market reforms bolster grid, may lower costs this winter: FERC

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Improved coordination in the West will help offset lower hydropower output due to historically low water levels in the Colorado River basin, while New England risks overreliance on oil, FERC said.

Electric market reforms in the West and Northeast may bolster grid reliability and, in some cases, lower costs this winter, according to the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission’s annual winter market and reliability report, released Thursday.

In the West, the California Independent System Operator launched the Extended Day Ahead Market and the Southwest Power Pool expanded into the Western Interconnection earlier this year.

“EDAM and SPP’s expansion should provide ratepayers in much of the West with production cost savings and improved reliability in winter 2026-2027, particularly during cold weather events,” FERC staff said in the report.

This winter, the markets’ improved coordination of generation and reduced need for reserve power supplies will help offset lower power output from the Glen Canyon and Hoover dams due to historically low water levels in the Colorado River basin, staff said.

“On a day-to-day basis, these conditions can create congestion and affect system operators’ ability to respond to voltage support challenges, event response mitigation needs, or any additional grid stressors,” staff said in the report.

About 45% of the continental United States and 95% of the Intermountain West region is in a drought, according to the report.

In the Northeast, ISO New England lowered its capacity performance pay rate, which should lower electricity prices during emergency conditions or capacity performance events this winter, staff said.

Also, the New York Independent System Operator changed its installed capacity market rules to reflect uncertainty about how much power Hydro-Québec will deliver over the 1,250-MW Champlain Hudson Power Express transmission line in the winter, they noted.

The U.S. subsidiary of the Canadian utility Hydro-Québec — the primary reservation holder — has an obligation to serve native load in its Canadian territory, which is a winterpeaking system.

This obligation, along with ongoing reduced runoff to its hydroelectric resources, “has left little or no surplus capacity to export to NYISO in recent years’ peak winter months,” staff said.

The Northeast — especially New England — also faces risks around its reliance on oil-fired power in the winter, according to the report.

On Jan. 25, for example, oil-fired generation reached 7,864 MW in a single hour, supplying about 40% of ISO-NE’s total net generation in that period, staff noted.

However, U.S. and regional distillate fuel supplies are low, according to the report. New England’s supplies were 46% below the prior five-year average as of early October, staff said.

FERC expects natural gas prices will be lower this winter compared to last winter across most of the U.S., in part because of higher storage inventories in some areas and forecasts for warmer weather caused by El Niño, according to the report.

As of Nov. 10, Henry Hub gas prices — a key U.S. benchmark — were trading at $3.19 per million British thermal units for the upcoming winter, 42% below last winter’s settled price of $5.52/MMBtu, staff said.

FERC staff expects power plant gas demand to increase by 0.8 billion cubic feet per day, or about 2%, to 35.7 Bcf/d on average this winter compared to last winter. Power burn is expected to account for 25% of total gas demand this winter, according to the report.

Large-load tariffs and other measures to weed out speculative projects are taking shape. Public backlash to data centers, shifting state-level policies and the utility profit model are complicating those efforts, stakeholders say.

NERC’s Board of Trustees on Monday voted to adopt reliability standards for AI data centers and other computational loads. The proposed rules now go to the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission for approval.

#5842. First siRNA Therapy Approved for Myasthenia Gravis

— Cemdisiran is the second C5 inhibitor the FDA has authorized in a week

The FDA approved subcutaneous cemdisiran (Livsayis), a small interfering RNA (siRNA) therapy, to treat generalized myasthenia gravis in adults who are anti-acetylcholine receptor (AChR) antibody-positive, Regeneron announced Wednesday.

Cemdisiran targets complement component 5 (C5), inhibiting terminal complement activation, a key driver of AChR-positive myasthenia gravis. It is the first approved siRNA therapy for myasthenia gravis.

The drug is the second C5 inhibitor the FDA has authorized to treat myasthenia gravis in a week, following the agency's approval of gefurulimab (Klygefa) last Friday.

The approval of cemdisiran was based on data from the 24-week phase III NIMBLE trial. For the primary outcome, Myasthenia Gravis-Activities of Daily Living (MG-ADL) scores improved from baseline by 4.5 points in the cemdisiran group versus 2.2 points in the placebo group, corresponding to an adjusted improvement of 2.3 points (P=0.0005). MG-ADL improvements were seen as early as 2 weeks after starting treatment.

A key secondary outcome showed a 4.2-point improvement in Quantitative Myasthenia Gravis (QMG) total scores at 24 weeks compared with a 1.5-point improvement with placebo, corresponding to an adjusted improvement of 2.8 points (P=0.0015).

The MG-ADL is a patient-reported measure of disease severity and functional impairment, with scores ranging from 0 to 24. The QMG is a physician-administered evaluation of muscle function, with scores ranging from 0 to 39.

In the NIMBLE trial, adverse events occurred in 69% of participants in the cemdisiran group and 77% in the placebo group. The most common adverse event in the cemdisiran group was upper respiratory tract infection (12%), which occurred at a similar rate in the placebo group (11%).

Serious adverse events were less frequent in the cemdisiran group (3%) than in the placebo group (14%). No serious or meningococcal infections were reported in the cemdisiran group.

No deaths were reported during the double-blind treatment period. Two deaths occurred afterward: one, due to pneumonia, was considered treatment-related by the investigator but not by the sponsor.

Cemdisiran is administered subcutaneously four times a year and is available only through a risk evaluation and mitigation strategy (REMS) program, Regeneron said.

The drug carries a boxed warning for serious and life-threatening infections caused by Neisseria meningitidis. Besides gefurulimab, three other C5 inhibitors have been approved by the FDA for myasthenia gravis: eculizumab (Soliris), ravulizumab (Ultomiris), and zilucoplan (Zilbrysq). All carry a similar warning, and patients are required to comply with recommendations for meningococcal vaccinations.

People with generalized myasthenia gravis "experience profound muscle weakness that can affect how they talk, see, breathe and move, and with it their ability to carry out everyday activities," said Samantha Masterson, president and CEO of the Myasthenia Gravis Foundation of America. "This approval is a positive milestone for the [generalized myasthenia gravis] community, reflecting continued progress on outcomes that matter most to patients."

#5841. CA Governor Talks Climate Action And Punches Nazis On European Tour

Tesla CEO and former California resident Elon Musk has been taking quite a bit of heat over his Nazi-adjacent rhetoric, compounded in recent weeks as reports continue to surface that he has manipulated Starlink military satellite service to Ukraine again, enabling Russian President Vladimir Putin to extend his war of aggression into other states of the former USSR. Why? Who knows! Regardless, another powerful California resident has been gearing up to push back against both Elon Musk and Vladimir Putin…

…If you guessed that someone is California Governor Gavin Newsom, run right out and buy yourself a cigar. California would be the fourth-largest single economy in the world if it was a nation unto itself. It offers living proof that decarbonization is a driver of economic growth, not a drag, and Newsom is happy to bring that achievement to the attention of the world.

Newsom has been eyeballed as a contender for the Democratic nomination in the 2028 Presidential election cycle. If so, he is off to a roaring start. Gavin chose the COP 31 climate talks in Türkiye to raise his profile on the international stage, while throwing down the renewable energy gauntlet against Putin and also taking jabs at Elon Musk.

Ukraine has considerable fossil energy resources that Putin can fold into the Russian economy (and his allies’ pockets), which explains why Putin launched an attempt to take Ukraine by storm in  February of 2022. Ukraine also has considerable renewable energy resources that it can bring to bear as an aspirational member of the European Union. With that in mind, it’s no surprise to see longtime Putin ally and fossil energy fan Donald Trump, and America’s shadow President Elon Musk, align with Russia against the interests of Ukraine.

It’s not just Ukraine. All of the former Soviet Union states are at risk, including members of NATO, the North Atlantic Treaty Organization. I visited NATO member and former satellite state Estonia on a clean tech tour after Putin invaded Ukraine, to get an up-close look at the tiny but innovative nation’s outsized influence on the renewable energy transition in Europe and beyond (here’s another example). Humming in the background of a round of site visits and presentations was an ongoing conversation about Estonia’s strategy to fend off Russia.

One manifestation of the defensive posture came this week, when Estonia announced the closing of border crossings, effective December 1, after accusing Russia of orchestrating an arson attack on one of its defense firms. The independent Ukrainian news organization Hromadske is also among those reporting that Estonia has been moving government services into remote digital platforms, safe from Russian attacks on data centers and other critical infrastructure.

Now let’s take a look at Governor Newsom’s all-but-in-name campaign launch this week, where a stopover in Poland illustrates how he intends to conflate anti-fascism with the renewable energy transition over the next two years — in other words, saving the planet and punching Nazis, too.

Considering that Elon Musk has spent a considerable sum of money to vault President Trump into the White House for a second term in office while also stirring up Nazi-adjacent sentiment in Europe and elsewhere through his “X” social media platform, Newsom’s highly public visit to the POLIN Museum of the History of Polish Jews in Warsaw was particularly ripe with symbolism. Among other historical exhibits, the museum commemorates the the Warsaw Uprising of 1943, known as the first — and for a long time the only — major urban battle against Nazi occupation during World War II.

After laying a ceremonial wreath at the Monument to the Ghetto Heroes at the museum, Newsom deployed his @CAgovernor account on X to underscore what appears to be the unifying theme of his campaign, calling the museum a “powerful reminder of fighting against the darkest forces.”

“The museum is a powerful reminder that we have to remain vigilant to protect what we value even in the darkest of times,” Newsom also elaborated in a press statement.

Newsom also used the occasion of the visit to sign a new climate partnership with Poland. The Memorandum of Understanding covers pollution reduction, energy efficiency, grid modernization, energy storage, and climate resiliency as well as renewable energy.

“Energy security, economic strength, and a livable climate are not competing goals. They are one mission, and no nation or state can achieve it alone. California and Poland come from different histories but share the same responsibility: to protect our people, power our economies, and leave future generations a safer world. This partnership puts that responsibility to work,” Newsom emphasized in a public statement that supports the running theme of fighting fascism with decarbonization.

Poland already has a running head start on renewable energy through its Baltic Sea offshore wind resources. Other clean tech sectors are on the move as well. To kickstart the MOU, Newsom met with the leading Polish battery firm Impact Clean Power Technology. “Impact designs and builds batteries for electric and hydrogen buses, trucks, rail and industrial vehicles, as well as for mobile robotics, with a particular focus on humanoid robots,” Newsom’s office explains.

“This place is all about entrepreneurial innovation, and it’s about imagination, the secret sauce,” Newsom adds, referring to Impact’s rapidly expanding plans for the future.

Poland was also the scene of the America-Ukraine Clean Energy Forum in Warsaw, providing another opportunity for Newsom to draw the connection between anti-fascism and the renewable energy transition. Referring to the new MOU, Newsom told participants at the form that the energy transition represents an “opportunity to think anew and act anew.”

“It’s the opportunity to begin with a different mindset. It’s an opportunity to be more resilient in every way, shape or form. That’s why clean energy should be front and center, self-evident. It’s a strategic imperative. Period. Full stop,” Newsom emphasized (emphasis added).

In a press release recapping the event, Newsom’s office also noted that the Governor told his audience that American voters “spoke loudly in the midterm elections,” in a reaction to “the Trump administration’s rampant corruption and embrace of Big Oil.”

“The subtext, he said, was that clean energy was subconsciously on the ballot, too,” the press release continued.

Newsom was not done yet. On November 16, he announced a new contribution of medical aid to Ukraine from California, including a new field hospital, more than 1,300 additional hospital beds, and millions of bandages among other supplies.

He also toured Ukraine in person, an extended trip that underscored the role of innovation and technology in Ukraine’s ability to withstand Russian attacks, despite efforts by Donald Trump and Elon Musk to undermine its defense.

In response to Musk’s repeated manipulation of SpaceX satellite services, Ukraine has already developed a workaround in the form of its own drone-based guidance systems, as capably demonstrated just yesterday by the destruction of a key Russian military asset, the Crimea Bridge.

Thoughts? Before you drop a note in the discussion thread, note that Musk’s SpaceX venture has emerged as a new, leading consumer of natural gas in the US. The startup will need much more of the same to make good on its plans for launching 30 rockets per day from its new “Starbase” in Louisiana. So much for saving the planet…

#5840. Budderfly to invest $500M to finance and operate municipal energy upgrades

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The energy-as-a-service company will recoup its investment from savings over a contract’s life. Such agreements have become more common, especially for institutions like universities, one expert said.

Energy-as-a-service contracts are not new, especially for institutions like universities and hospitals, said Eric Smith, professor of practice and associate director of the Tulane Energy Institute at Tulane University. They can provide resiliency in addition to savings, he said.

He pointed to Tulane’s arrangement with Enfra, announced in 2022, in which the company paid for new energy assets and equipment, including a 1-MW solar generation facility, while the school got guaranteed annual cost savings over the 30-year term of the contract and help achieving its emissions-reduction goals.

The upgrades also make the school less vulnerable to power outages from hurricanes that regularly hit Louisiana, which ultimately helps protect students, he said.

“What you're really operating is a job shop to do that equipment optimization and manage it over some period of time,” Smith said of the services companies like Enfra and Budderfly offer.

“The way you get paid for that is to demonstrate that you're saving the ratepayer money,” he continued. “Now we have enough power on campus. We can island the campus and run the whole campus based on the power that they generate with our existing power plant.”

Budderfly’s Edwards said his company started out offering energy management contracts to small and medium-sized businesses before expanding to local governments.

Although governments have access to lower-cost financing options, municipal budget and staffing constraints are a challenge for long-term investments in energy efficiency, he said.

“There's very little appetite anywhere to increase taxes to pay for energy infrastructure,” Edwards said.

The company has already deployed the financing model in Cadillac, Michigan, where it invested $4.4 million in upgrades. Budderfly will use Schneider Electric’s energy management and automation technologies to integrate and manage the energy infrastructure improvements.

It is offering local governments a 15-year contract at a locked-in rate based on its 12- to 24-month historical use average. Budderfly becomes the utility’s customer of record and pays the customer’s utility bill, taking a cut of the savings achieved from upgraded, more-efficient equipment.

If bill savings extend beyond a 30% goal, those are shared with the municipality 50/50, Edwards said. Some contracts also have opportunities for virtual power plant programs and to share additional revenue from demand-side management programs, he added.

#5839. SolarEdge unveils U.S.-made optimizer for C&I solar

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 17, 2026

SolarEdge’s new Duo Power Optimizer promises to simplify commercial and industrial solar installations with 50% fewer optimizer units, independent module-level power tracking, and enhanced safety features.

The commercial and industrial (C&I) solar market is getting a new hardware option aimed at making installations simpler without sacrificing system performance. SolarEdge Technologies has unveiled its new Duo Power Optimizer, a U.S.-manufactured solution designed to deliver module-level energy harvesting and monitoring while cutting the number of power optimizers in half.

Announced November 16 ahead of RE+ 2026 in Las Vegas, the new product introduces a 2:1 architecture that allows one optimizer to connect two solar modules. Notably, each module retains its own independent maximum power point tracker (MPPT), which preserves the module-level performance benefits associated with the traditional one-to-one optimizer configurations.

For developers, EPCs and installers managing large-scale rooftop and distributed generation projects, the value here lies in fewer components to purchase, wire and install, with no trade-off in module-level optimization.

Simplifying installation without sacrificing performance

SolarEdge’s optimization tech addresses the challenge of conventional string inverter systems, which is that an underperforming module can affect the output of an entire string. But by allowing each module to operate independently, the system limits the impact of shading, degradation or performance differences between panels.

The Duo brings demonstrates that capability in a two-module configuration. By reducing the number of optimizers by 50% compared with one-to-one designs, SolarEdge is targeting an opportunity to streamline balance-of-system requirements and reduce the installation labor.

SolarEdge’s U.S.-made Duo Power Optimizer connects two solar modules while maintaining independent module-level power tracking for commercial and industrial solar installations. Credit: SolarEdge

The hardware also incorporates design changes intended to improve jobsite efficiency. Each optimizer features two input pairs: a shorter connection for the nearest module and a pre-extended connection for the adjacent module. The arrangement reduces excess wiring and eliminates the need for field-made jumpers.

The configuration also accommodates modules with different orientations, making it particularly relevant for east-west and dual-tilt racking applications.

Beyond installation efficiency, SolarEdge is emphasizing electrical safety as a central feature of the new product.

The Duo extends the company’s Sense Connect technology to its input and output connectors, monitoring connector temperatures to identify developing issues before they potentially escalate into overheating or electrical arcs.

Also, the optimizer introduces what SolarEdge describes as an industry-first approach to arc fault detection: every optimizer acts as an individual arc sensor. Rather than relying exclusively on detection at the inverter, the system distributes monitoring throughout the array, allowing potential arc faults to be identified closer to their source.

SolarEdge says the technology provides coverage across strings with up to 3,280 feet of total cable length between the inverter and optimizer. It can also help pinpoint problems to the optimizer area, potentially making troubleshooting and maintenance more efficient.

Manufactured at SolarEdge’s Florida facility, the Duo is designed to meet U.S. Domestic Content and Foreign Entity of Concern (FEOC) requirements, according to the company. SolarEdge says its domestic-content eligibility levels are consistent with those of its previous U.S.-manufactured power optimizers, subject to applicable tax guidance and project-specific requirements.

For SolarEdge, the Duo represents an effort to combine three priorities that are becoming more and more important in commercial solar: installation efficiency, system-level safety, and domestic manufacturing.

The technology does not fundamentally change the value proposition of module-level optimization. Instead, it looks to make that architecture more economical and easier to deploy at scale.

SolarEdge will showcase the Duo Power Optimizer at RE+ 2026.

#5838. Europe’s Inland Barges Need a Cargo Plan Before an Electric Fleet Plan

Coal throughput at Rotterdam rose 15.3% in the first nine months of 2026. Crude oil and petroleum-product volumes also increased. An inland vessel operator looking only at this year’s cargo market could see decades of work ahead. But an engine, battery conversion or replacement vessel bought now may still be operating in 2050, when the mix of cargo travelling through European ports and rivers will be different.

Rotterdam’s latest throughput release explains the coal rebound through recovering coking-coal movements, expensive natural gas and power-market conditions. Meanwhile, Eurostat reports that EU inland-waterway freight fell 3% in 2025 to 118 billion tonne-kilometres. Coal and crude-petroleum freight rose that year, while agricultural freight contracted. These changes are reshaping the services that ports, vessel owners and customers need to buy.

The Central Commission for the Navigation of the Rhine counted 6,224 dry-cargo vessels in Rhine countries in 2025, down from 6,410 in 2023. Smaller vessels account for much of the decline. Tankers moved the other way, rising from 1,400 in 2022 to 1,477 in 2025. The numbers describe a fleet already adapting to changing cargo and operating economics.

My TFIE Strategy Briefing on the inland barges Europe will need in 2050 uses a cargo-first model to work from the freight task to the future electric fleet. The underlying 98-page Inland Freight Electrification report is available to read.

A vessel owner has two connected capital decisions. The first is whether the freight service will still have customers through the useful life of the asset. The second is which propulsion and charging system can perform that service economically. Starting with equipment and then finding a market for it risks spending scarce investment capital on the wrong vessels.

I modelled a large European industrial port corridor from a 2025-style baseline to 2050. The central scenario assumes bulk cargo falls from 57.8 million tonnes to 34.4 million tonnes, a decline of about 40%. Wet bulk accounts for much of the loss, falling from 34.5 million to 15.0 million tonnes. Dry bulk declines from 23.3 million to 19.4 million tonnes. The model is a transparent scenario for investment decisions across this type of corridor, rather than a forecast of Rotterdam’s exact future cargo volumes.

The largest change is fossil-linked cargo. Fossil fuels and refinery streams fall from 19.0 million tonnes to 1.5 million tonnes, while coal and other solid fossil fuels fall from 2.5 million tonnes to zero. Combined, those reductions account for about 20 million tonnes of the scenario’s 23.4-million-tonne net decline. Any operator whose principal customers depend on moving coal or petroleum faces a different replacement decision from one carrying grain, construction minerals, chemicals or scrap.

Steel illustrates what cargo substitution means. The model reduces imported iron ore and other virgin iron units from 4.5 million tonnes to 3.0 million tonnes, while scrap and circular feedstocks rise from 1.8 million to 3.5 million tonnes. The industrial function continues as the materials moving to and from factories change. Existing barges and terminals may find new work, although parcel sizes, loading systems and routes will also change.

Biofuel feedstocks create a second trap. In the central scenario those movements peak near 5 million tonnes in 2030 before falling to 1.5 million tonnes in 2050. A temporary freight boom can support profitable operations without supporting a new vessel or terminal investment with a thirty-year life. The durability of the contract matters as much as the volume carried this year.

Cargo tonnage alone cannot size an electric fleet. The model translates commodities into powered vessel-equivalents using parcel sizes, routes, loaded calls and productivity. It starts with around 1,150 powered vessel-equivalents and ends with 664, a reduction of 42%. Dry cargo falls 17% in tonnes but 43% in powered vessel-equivalents because the modelled freight service changes. The starting 1,150 powered equivalents represent about 1,234 physical hulls after counting unpowered barges moved by pushers. Battery conversion decisions concern powered units; terminal and cargo-handling decisions also concern the additional hulls.

That distinction is particularly important for pushed formations. A pusher provides propulsion for several cargo barges. Converting one powered unit does not necessarily mean replacing every physical hull. Counting all hulls as propulsion conversions would inflate the equipment and financing requirement.

Low Rhine water levels bring a different constraint into the same calculation. A barge can remain on the register yet carry less freight per journey when draught is restricted. Cargo owners may have to buy more voyages, use different hulls or shift some freight to rail and road. Route depth, seasonal load factors and terminal access belong in the service model alongside cargo demand.

Ports should begin with a living cargo and route register showing which freight is declining, which remains, and which changes its material inputs. Vessel owners can connect that register to remaining hull life, annual utilization, cargo contracts and the cost of electric conversion. Utilities and terminal operators can then size grid connections, charging facilities and battery-container pools against vessels that will run on bankable routes. Infrastructure developed around dependable freight customers has a stronger prospect of earning a return.

Europe has good reasons to electrify established, high-utilization inland freight now. Battery-powered vessels are already serving container and bulk routes. The commercial opportunity is to invest in routes with surviving customers and electrify the services they will continue to buy. Expanding an electric fleet before measuring that freight task puts the order of decisions backward.

The complete TFIE Strategy Briefing works through the cargo substitutions, fleet denominator and investment decisions. The open Inland Freight Electrification report includes route-energy modelling, battery-pool sizing, terminal requirements and programme economics. TFIE Strategy also undertakes scoped corridor and fleet assessments for ports, operators and cargo owners.

#5837. Permitting reform bill heads to House as Democratic divisions persist

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Some Democrats say the party should not agree to a compromise bill that contains what they consider troubling changes to environmental law and state and tribal authority.

The Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act, which proposes sweeping changes to energy infrastructure permitting, faces an uncertain path in the U.S. House of Representatives after passing the Senate in a 62-33 vote Thursday night.

The bill was supported by 47 Republicans and 15 members of the Democratic caucus. It was negotiated by Democratic and Republican lawmakers, along with the White House — but that negotiation preceded the midterm elections where Democrats won control of both chambers of Congress. Now, some Democrats say the party should not agree to a bill that contains what they consider troubling changes to environmental law and the authorities of courts, states and tribes.

Sen. Raphael Warnock, D-Ga., was among the senators who voted against the bill. He said in a statement that he had “hoped that a robust amendment process could’ve addressed these shortcomings in the bill that Georgians have rightly raised ... but in its absence, I am voting no.”

“I believe we can get a better bill when a new Congress is sworn in this January,” Warnock added.

On the House side, Rep. Scott Peters, D-Calif., rebuked this line of thinking.

“There will be no better deal on the table,” he said in a statement. “With Donald Trump in the White House and 60 required votes in the Senate, bipartisanship is unavoidable, whether it’s this Congress or next. The status quo isn’t working.”

Peters also criticized Sens. Ron Wyden, D-Ore., and Jeff Merkley, D-Ore., for blocking voting on all other amendments after Wyden’s amendment to ban data centers on public lands was left off of the list of amendments to vote on.

“Unfortunately two Senators, at the last minute, opposed bipartisan amendments to make a good bill better for tribal partners,” Peters said. “We should make sure this bill gets across the finish line to lower costs and emissions and then continue our work to make the progress they opposed, and more.”

“It’s unimaginable that after this month’s election, when the entire country demanded lower costs, we would whiff on the opportunity right in front of us,” he added.

National Rural Electric Cooperative Association CEO Jim Matheson celebrated the bill’s passage in a Thursday statement, noting that earlier this month, NRECA sent a letter to the Senate “expressing strong support for the bill provided it was amended to exempt electric co-ops, which are locally governed and controlled, from unnecessary transmission regulation by the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission.”

“The final bill passed by the Senate includes this exemption, securing NRECA’s continuing support,” Matheson said. “This legislation will dramatically improve the federal permitting system, making it more manageable and predictable for electric co-ops.”

The Edison Electric Institute, which represents investor-owned utilities, previously expressed support for the bill on Nov. 9 when it passed its first cloture vote in the Senate.

Some prominent environmental groups continue to oppose the bill.

The Natural Resources Defense Council issued a statement Thursday saying the bill “remains unacceptable.”

“Lawmakers should oppose this bill and try again next year to pass legislation worthy of the American people, not for the fossil fuel industry and data center companies,” said NRDC President and CEO Manish Bapna.

Some of the criticism of the bill, as well as its support, is bipartisan.

Sen. Josh Hawley, R-Mo., who voted against the bill Thursday, said it was “stuffed with giveaways to data centers and industry at the expense of farmers.”

Sen. Catherine Cortez Masto, D-Nev., voted yes, but criticized the bill for a lack of consideration for tribal nations.

“The version of the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act that passed the Senate today ignores Tribal input and makes changes to the National Historic Preservation Act that Tribes across the country do not support,” she said. “I’m disappointed that Republicans wouldn’t allow this section to be struck from the bill.”

NERC’s Board of Trustees on Monday voted to adopt reliability standards for AI data centers and other computational loads. The proposed rules now go to the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission for approval.

#5836. ACC Approves Gas Plants, Rate Hike, and Allows Annual Rate Increase

Phoenix – Yesterday, the Arizona Corporation Commission voted to approve Salt River Project’s (SRP) proposed Marigold gas plant in Pinal County, and also approved Tucson Electric Power’s (TEP) proposed rate increase, as well as its proposed Annual Rate Adjustment Mechanism (ARAM) that would allow the utility to increase its rates every year with very little oversight. Yet again, the Commission’s decisions are at odds with the best interests of ratepayers, issuing rulings that collectively worsen air quality, raise costs, and limit transparency.

SRP’s gas-fired Marigold power plant is a part of the larger Marigold Energy Center, which would include solar and battery storage. But the 675-megawatt gas plant exposes customers to volatile fuel prices while worsening air quality in a county that already experiences some of the state’s most degraded air, and fails to meet federal air quality standards. The Commission did not adequately analyze the plant’s environmental impacts before approving SRP’s Certificate of Environmental Compatibility (CEC) application and approved it without any opportunity for public comment before the Commission.

The ACC also approved a significant rate increase for TEP, approving a 10% surge that customers will see reflected in utility bills starting in December. The utility had initially proposed a 14% increase. The decision comes as TEP’s parent company, Fortis, netted more than $1.7 billion in 2025 across its portfolio of utilities. The Commission also approved the utility’s adoption of “formula rates,” enabling TEP to fast-track rate increases every year without a public rate case, with limited oversight by the Commission and limited opportunities for public participation. However, Commissioner Lea Márquez-Peterson filed a proposed amendment in the case to deny approval of TEP’s formula rate mechanism.

In September, Sierra Club published a report documenting various decisions the Commission has leveraged against the best interests of ratepayers despite the fact that Arizonans experience some of the highest electric bills in the country, with some of the most planned gas in the country.

“Today’s decisions continue a pattern Arizonans have become all too familiar with,” said Sierra Club Grand Canyon Chapter Director Sandy Bahr. “The ACC has shown us time and again that it will continue to prioritize utility profits and polluters over the community it is supposed to protect. Rising energy costs, climate, and clean air are top of mind for Arizonans. The ACC clearly isn’t considering this as it continues to approve rate hikes and gas plants that pollute the air, and threaten groundwater.”

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.
