#5835. Doctor's Mass Shooting Stuns Medical Community

— Colleagues never perceived Matt Lambert as a danger to himself or others

Doctors who knew Matt Lambert, MD, the emergency physician at the center of last week's mass shooting in Washington, D.C., were shocked by what happened, and said they never expected him to do something so dire.

Those who had interacted with him described a collegial, friendly person who showed no signs of any distress or any mental health conditions. Physicians spoke with MedPage Today about Lambert on the condition of anonymity.

One said Lambert spoke about problems with his condo unit, but it was "no different than if I said I was frustrated with a building problem. There was never any dark humor or anything that seemed off."

Nor did Lambert ever talk about guns, they said -- despite the fact he had at least three of them in his apartment the day of the shootings. On Nov. 12, he killed the building's property manager, then shot and wounded four other people before killing himself.

Joel Braithwaite, an attorney who represented Lambert, said at a press conference that he had spoken with Lambert not long before the shooting and said he was in "great spirits. ... I'm at a loss for how things took any sort of turn that would have us here today."

Braithwaite described Lambert as a "humanitarian" who helped people with scholarships for medical school. "He was also a great thinker, an author, someone who's very calm and collected."

Michael Brice-Saddler, a reporter for the Washington Sun, spoke with Lambert about a year ago about his ongoing issues with his condo unit. In a video posted with his report, Brice-Saddler said there were "no indications to me that he was dangerous or capable of violence."

"I went back and visited him multiple times," he said. "If I thought my safety was in question, I would have pulled the plug."

Last December, Lambert said this to Brice-Saddler about his ongoing condo unit fight: "It's affected every part of my life -- my sleep, dietary habits. I pride myself on my resilience, and I've been kind of surprised at how much it has been affected."

Brice-Saddler wrote that that Lambert did sometimes use "intense, combative language -- and at one point, his frustration veered into what at the time felt like dark humor." He made a reference to Luigi Mangione, the suspect in the 2024 shooting death of UnitedHealthcare's CEO who is accused of inscribing "delay, deny, depose" on his ammunition. Lambert said those words described the condo association's legal strategy, according to Brice-Saddler's report.

Lambert also posted something similar last month on the X account he created for his unit: "After months of 'Luigi' tactics (delay, deny, defend) by the HOA, we were finally able to submit an independent engineering report into the record," it stated in part.

Brice-Saddler uncovered something else about Lambert in his reporting -- the sad fact that he was raised in Appalachia by a single mother who died of cancer during his first year of medical school at Marshall University in Huntington, West Virginia.

Physicians who knew Lambert told MedPage Today it was unclear if he had a partner.

It's well established that physicians with mental health conditions don't often seek help, which is particularly concerning given that doctors experience elevated rates of burnout, depression, anxiety, and suicide compared with the general population, according to a special communication published last year in JAMA.

Jesse Ehrenfeld, MD, MPH, a past president of the American Medical Association and a co-author of that communication, said that much work stands to be done on reducing barriers to mental healthcare for clinicians. That includes expanding confidential care options and removing intrusive questions on licensure questionnaires.

"We need to make that a best practice everywhere instead of just in a handful of states," Ehrenfeld told MedPage Today.

N. Adam Brown, MD, MBA, an emergency physician and healthcare executive who writes about clinician mental health, noted that "having letters behind our names as physicians does not grant us any special privilege of not having an underlying mental health condition," and that the U.S. needs to "take very seriously the health and wellness of clinicians."

"We also need to recognize the role that gun violence plays in our society," Brown told MedPage Today. "We need to take a look at both sides of the coin -- the mental health aspect, as well as the accessibility of guns -- to prevent acts like this in the future."

#5834. Mission Solar Energy puts supply chain transparency in the spotlight at RE+ 2026

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 18, 2026

With new high-efficiency residential and commercial modules and an integrated supply chain spanning polysilicon to power conversion, Mission Solar Energy is making a case for U.S. manufacturing, domestic content and FEOC-compliant sourcing.

As the solar industry continues to navigate shifting trade policies, domestic content incentives, and supply chain scrutiny, Mission Solar Energy is bringing a message of manufacturing strength and transparency to RE+ 2026. The San Antonio-based manufacturer showcased its latest commercial and residential modules while highlighting the advantages of its relationship with fellow OCI Holdings subsidiaries OCI TerraSus and OCI Power.

The announcement puts the spotlight on a growing priority for solar developers, installers and distributors: knowing exactly where components originate and having the documentation to prove it.

The three companies collectively cover significant portions of the solar value chain. OCI TerraSus produces high-purity, solar-grade polysilicon in Sarawak, Malaysia; Mission Solar designs, engineers, and assembles modules in San Antonio, Texas; and OCI Power manufactures inverters and power conversion systems in South Korea.

According to Mission Solar, all three companies are compliant with Foreign Entity of Concern (FEOC) requirements and are not classified as prohibited foreign entities. The integrated structure offers customers access to a traceable supply chain backed by OCI Holdings, a global clean energy group headquartered in Seoul.

For an industry that’s becoming more and more focused on sourcing eligibility and domestic manufacturing credentials, that positioning could prove commercially important.

On the product front, Mission Solar is highlighting two modules aimed at different market segments.

For commercial and industrial (C&I) applications, the company is featuring its 595W N-Type Bifacial 144HC module. The dual-glass design captures sunlight from both sides, potentially increasing energy production without requiring additional project footprint. Designed and assembled in the United States, the module carries 11.5% domestic content, meets FEOC requirements and supports front-side loads of up to 5,400 Pa.

Residential installers will get a look at the 440W N-Type 108HC rooftop module, which Mission Solar says will be the first of its kind on the market with 12% domestic content. Building on an existing 6% domestic-content version, the module combines N-type cell technology and a multi-busbar design to deliver efficiency of up to 23%. It is also approved on residential financing platforms including LightReach and GoodLeap.

Looking further ahead, Mission Solar plans to preview two next-generation G12R modules, with commercial launches anticipated in late 2027. The company will also display a lower-domestic-content U.S.-produced C&I module option expected in 2027.

Although module performance is still critical to the industry, supply chain credibility is increasingly part of the overall purchasing decision.

As developers evaluate project economics, tax credit eligibility and sourcing risk, manufacturers that can offer transparent documentation alongside competitive technology may have an advantage.

Jeff Landry’s “lighting speed” initiative and a previous decision by a majority of PSC commissioners, the agency is rushing this proposal through and planning to provide only one opportunity for the public to speak out against this proposal: a December meeting when commissioners are scheduled to also vote on the proposal minutes later. Much of the analysis Entergy has submitted to the PSC has been shielded from public view by NDA restrictions.

“It’s clear by now that Meta and Entergy don’t care much for normal Louisianans, but the PSC has a mandate to do just that. They must vote no on this outrageous corporate favor.”

“Any claims that Meta is fully paying its own way are false,” said Darryl Malek-Wiley, a senior field representative for Sierra Club and a Louisiana resident. “These seven gas plants will guzzle our water, pollute our air, – and customers will be on the hook to pay if Meta walks away. We’re here to tell commissioners that we are done footing the bill for big tech – especially considering the extreme lack of transparency surrounding this project.”

In negotiations, Entergy showed little willingness to consider or agree to any of these stipulations.

Separately, in Meta’s application to the LDEQ, it wants to modify its existing permit to add 44 fossil-fuel water boilers that will operate and emit pollutants around the clock. Because this would send total pollution for the permit beyond the minor-source permit limits, Meta said that – instead of applying for a major source permit as required by the Clean Air Act – it simply wouldn’t count emissions from the previously approved 31 diesel generators when those are operating during emergency hours.

Sierra Club has urged LDEQ that it must consider emissions from those diesel generators running during emergency operations.

#5832. US to install 36% more wind turbines in 2025 than previous year

The United States is expected to add more than 7 GW of wind installations in 2025, a 36% increase over the previous year, and the five-year outlook remains unchanged quarter-over-quarter from previous forecast, according to the “U.S. Wind Energy Monitor” report released by Wood Mackenzie and the American Clean Power Association (ACP).

Amid a challenging market environment, the report shows that the United States is on track to add 46 GW of new wind capacity from 2025 to 2029, with total projected volumes unchanged quarter-on-quarter from previous forecasts. However, timing has shifted, as 2026 and 2027 will deliver significant gains, at 10.7 GW and 12.7 GW, respectively, as more assets advance through the development pipeline.

While Q3 installations came in 23% below forecast at 932 MW, the market has shown progress with 3.8 GW queued for Q4 2025 — representing 52% of the year’s total expected capacity. This back-loaded installation pattern is consistent with typical project-commissioning timelines.

U.S. turbine order intake has rebounded to pre-One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBBA) levels quarter-over-quarter, supported by 2+ GW of firm commitments in Q3, the biggest intake in the region in the last nine months and a 79% quarter-over-quarter increase. However, true visibility remains limited as OEMs increasingly withhold project details and much qualifying “start-of-construction” activity occurs through off-site component manufacturing.

The market will then see a noticeable drop off, as 2029 weakens meaningfully quarter-over-quarter following project cancellations and inactive designations for late-decade capacity, driven by permitting and broader development challenges.

“The U.S. power market is facing mounting strain after a decade of flat demand, with utilities committing to 160 GW of large-load additions. This represents a significant opportunity for wind energy, which benefits from strengthened economic fundamentals and a compelling business case driven by its competitively low LCOE,” said Leila Garcia da Fonseca, director of research at Wood Mackenzie. “However, turbine costs remain elevated due to tariffs and mid-term wind growth will depend on resolving permitting and policy uncertainty.”

Power demand growth through 2029 is expected to average around 3% compared to just 0.7% over the previous decade, with data centers accounting for approximately 59 GW of the 90 GW total peak demand growth. This surge in baseload demand positions wind as a natural fit to meet rising power needs.

The five-year capacity outlook remains unchanged at 39.8 GW of added capacity quarter-over-quarter. The 2025-2027 pipeline is fully committed with all projects having turbine orders in place. More than 60% of the three-year capacity outlook has been commissioned or is under construction.

Activity is led by western states, such as Wyoming, New Mexico and others, that will account for 34% of activity in this time period. Major projects driving the outlook include Pattern’s 3.5-GW SunZia project in New Mexico, which will position the developer as 2026’s top installer, and Invenergy’s 998-MW Towner Energy Center in Colorado, the largest single project expected in 2027.

The market continues expanding geographically, with Arkansas bringing its first utility-scale onshore wind project online through Cordelio’s Crossover Wind.

The repowering market remains strong, as Wood Mackenzie projects that 18 projects will drive 2.5 GW of capacity additions in the next three years.

Wood Mackenzie expects the offshore installation pace to slow in Q4 2025 due to harsh winter weather conditions, pushing remaining capacity into 2026. Despite these near-term adjustments, Vineyard Wind has demonstrated strong execution, connecting 15 turbines in Q3 and delivering 200 GWh through nine months.

“U.S. offshore wind shows diverging momentum,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Projects under construction with COD estimated for 2026 continue to hit key milestones, but post-2027 developments face potential delays amid constrained wind turbine installation vessel capacity, driving delays and contract terminations.”

The offshore sector is also experiencing significant financial pressure.

The report highlights that uncertainty around tariffs is a threat to projections. Wood Mackenzie projects that tariffs will drive up turbine costs in 2026, before moderating in subsequent years. In total, U.S. onshore wind capex is projected to increase by 5% through 2029.

“U.S. wind turbine pricing is experiencing unprecedented uncertainty as conflicting market and regulatory forces interact,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Domestic manufacturing overcapacity relative to permitted project volumes, particularly after 2028, would normally place downward pressure on prices. Despite this, onshore wind costs are expected to continue rising due to tariff exposure on raw material inputs and subcomponents.”

#5831. Biofuels in the EU Post-2030 Renewable Energy Framework

A deep-dive exploring the impacts of potential EU biofuels policy scenarios

The discussion around the future of the Renewable Energy Directive (REDIII) is underway as the European Commission devises what role renewable energy will play in transport in the post-2030 energy framework.

As the political priorities of the EU focus on energy independence and domestic industry, there is a renewed push for more first generation food and feed crop biofuels in the European transport energy mix. But these biofuels can have adverse impacts on the climate, environment and food supply chains.

To address these concerns, we have modelled three different policy scenarios to demonstrate the consequences of varying outcomes and their potential impact on the climate, environment and food security.

Our findings show that the current cap on first-generation biofuels set by the Renewable Energy Directive (RED III) can allow 72% more food- and feed-crop biofuels to be used in the EU transport energy mix, compared to what the bloc consumed in 2024.

When accounting for indirect land use change, even at current consumption, emissions from first generation biofuels already reach nearly 43 MtCO2e, about 23% more than the fossil fuels they replace.

When safeguards take the same form as stipulated in RED III and other key transport regulations, food- and feed-crop biofuel volumes, land-use area and emissions could all potentially double by 2040, emitting an equivalent to more than 5 million diesel cars.

In an unfavourable scenario, whereby the cap on first generation biofuels is harmonised at 7% across Member States’ transport sectors, volumes, land use and emissions would almost triple.

In a worst case scenario, where the cap on first generation biofuels is expanded beyond 7%, the negative consequences would be far heavier. We have used 10% as an example: volumes, land use and emissions could be four times today’s levels in 2040. Land dedicated to biofuels could reach 17.7 Mha, 18% of the EU arable land, an area larger than Greece, and emissions would exceed those of fossil fuels by 33 MtCO2e.

For advanced and waste biofuels, which are limited in supply, we show that the road sector is currently overconsuming available feedstocks, which should be directed towards sectors that cannot be electrified, particularly aviation. Furthermore, recent cases of fraud have highlighted the risks of relying on third country exports, making the case that all palm oil derivatives should be phased out and that effective safeguards should be put in place to allow for the most sustainable feedstocks.

Transport & Environment’s (T&E) vision is a zero-emission mobility system that is affordable and has minimal impacts on our health, climate and environment. Created over 30 years ago, we have shaped some of Europe’s most important environmental laws.

#5830. Prostate Cancer Screening Is Changing. Health Systems Must Catch Up.

— Better preventive tools should be accompanied by accessible and affordable follow-up

For years, the medical community has been divided on prostate cancer screening. Urology organizations have continued to recommend prostate-specific antigen (PSA) testing, while other societies and groups moved to emphasize shared decision-making and individual choice, after careful consideration of potential risks and benefits.

The American Cancer Society's (ACS) new recommendations incorporate updated data and make a clearer suggestion in favor of screening. Longer follow-up research has confirmed that screening prevents prostate cancer deaths, and advances in diagnosis and treatment reduce complications.

To support that recommendation, we must make the safer approach available wherever men receive care.

ACS now recommends a PSA blood test every 2 years for average-risk men ages 50 through 69, and annually beginning at 45 for Black men and men with a strong family history. Decisions at 70 through 74 should be individualized. Informed patient agreement remains essential, and screening recommendations generally stop at 75 or when life expectancy is under 10 years.

The strongest evidence for this change comes from the European randomized screening trial. After 23 years, men invited to repeated PSA screening had a 13% lower risk of dying from prostate cancer. The absolute reduction was 0.22 percentage points: approximately two fewer deaths per 1,000 men invited. One death was prevented for every 456 men invited to screening. That benefit is meaningful, but modest.

Screening also finds cancers that would never threaten a man's life. Reasonable experts weigh those trade-offs differently: the U.K. National Screening Committee recently declined to recommend population screening. Recommendations for earlier screening in Black men also rely substantially on modeling because trials inadequately represent them. Supporting the new recommendations requires that we acknowledge these uncertainties.

What strengthens the case for screening today is our growing ability to separate an elevated PSA from a mandatory biopsy, and a cancer diagnosis from an automatic, potentially debilitating treatment.

An elevated PSA should first be confirmed before further evaluation. MRI can then help determine who needs a biopsy. In the Göteborg-2 randomized trial, an MRI-directed approach reduced detection of clinically insignificant cancers by 57% compared with routine systematic biopsy. That finding supports a more selective diagnostic process, although it does not yet prove equivalent long-term survival.

Biopsy itself can be made safer. Transperineal biopsy reaches the prostate through the skin rather than the rectum (e.g. transrectal biopsy, which is the historically more common approach). In the PREVENT randomized trial, infections occurred in none of the men receiving this approach without preventive antibiotics, compared with 1.6% undergoing transrectal biopsy with targeted antibiotics. Cancer detection was similar.

For appropriately selected low-risk cancers, active surveillance can spare or postpone treatment. In the prospective Canary PASS study, estimated prostate cancer mortality at 10 years was 0.1%, and metastasis occurred in 1.4%. About half had received treatment by then. Surveillance means scheduled reassessment and intervention when warranted; its safety depends on continued and adequate follow-up.

Treatment is improving too. In the randomized MIRAGE trial, MRI-guided radiation with smaller treatment margins reduced cumulative moderate-or-worse urinary toxicity over 2 years from 51% to 27%, compared with CT-guided treatment. Bowel toxicity also fell. These results support precision in radiation delivery, while longer follow-up remains important.

In surgery for radical prostatectomy, the NeuroSAFE PROOF trial used rapid examination of tissue during the operation to guide nerve preservation. Among men with good erectile function beforehand, the approach improved erectile-function scores 1 year later and early continence recovery. Neither technique eliminates treatment harms, but both show that preserving quality of life can be a measurable treatment objective.

Aligning these advances with a safe screening program requires changes in how we deliver and pay for care.

First, insurers should cover the entire recommended evaluation without financial barriers: repeat PSA testing, appropriate MRI or biomarkers, and biopsy when indicated. A study of privately insured men found that median out-of-pocket spending for MRI plus biopsy reached up to $620 in 2020. A screening recommendation loses value when patients cannot afford the steps needed to interpret its result safely. Making this a nationwide requirement may take an updated U.S. Preventive Services Task Force recommendation with an A or B grade, explicit protection for follow-up testing, or legislation. The task force's current C grade does not trigger the Affordable Care Act's no-cost preventive coverage requirement.

Second, health systems need reliable referral pathways. Primary care teams should know where to send patients, who tracks abnormal results, and how quickly evaluation should occur. MRI quality and interpretation require oversight. A negative scan cannot become a reason to abandon follow-up when other findings remain concerning. Regional imaging networks, specialist consultation and patient navigation can help extend expertise beyond major cancer centers.

Third, active surveillance must be supported as ongoing care. Patients need understandable explanations, dependable recall systems, and accessible reassessment. Payment should recognize the clinical work involved in monitoring a cancer carefully. Men also should have access to balanced consultations about surgery and radiation, including likely effects on sexual, urinary, and bowel function.

Finally, health systems need to measure whether screening delivers better outcomes. Quality programs should track unresolved abnormal results, biopsy-related infections, surveillance participation, treatment complications, and diagnoses of metastases. Results should be examined by ethnicity, insurance, and geography to identify gaps and guide improvement. Research programs should also expand enrollment of Black men in screening and surveillance studies, so future recommendations rest on evidence that better represents those at greatest risk.

I support the ACS recommendations. We now have stronger evidence that screening saves lives and better tools to reduce its harms. We need to make those tools available to every man we screen, with affordable follow-up, careful decisions about treatment, and support throughout his care.

#5828. Six new ways to simplify solar installs: Top Rack Technology brings mounting innovation to RE+ 2026

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 18, 2026

From smarter waterproofing to 360-degree rail adjustment, Top Rack Technology is putting installer efficiency, rooftop flexibility, and cost control at the center of its latest product lineup.

As the solar industry continues to look for ways to reduce installation costs and streamline residential projects, mounting hardware is getting a fresh dose of innovation. Top Rack Technology, Inc. (TRT) is making that case at RE+ 2026, where the solar racking manufacturer is showcasing six new mounting solutions designed to simplify rooftop installations, improve flexibility, and help installers work more efficiently.

Its latest lineup reflects a practical approach to product development: addressing everyday installation challenges while keeping system performance, aesthetics, and affordability in focus.

Six Products, One Common Goal: Better Installation Efficiency

Among the new offerings is TRT’s Full-Coverage Skirt System, which targets an important consideration in residential solar: appearance. By concealing exposed array edges, the system delivers a cleaner, more finished look that can help installations better complement residential properties.

The new TRT-G2 Bracket takes aim at installation simplicity and competitive pricing. Designed around practical field requirements, the bracket can be paired with TRT’s ButyLok waterproofing component, creating an integrated attachment and roof-sealing solution.

Waterproofing is particularly important in rooftop solar, where roof penetrations are still a critical consideration for installers and property owners alike. ButyLok introduces a shoe-like component containing pre-applied, gel-like butyl material. When compressed by the G2 bracket, the material spreads across the intended sealing surface, potentially reducing manual sealant application and simplifying the installation process.

TRT is also introducing SAVRIX, another residential attachment option developed with manufacturing efficiency, installation practicality, and cost competitiveness top of mind.. Meanwhile, the RF-2 Rail Fastener is designed to simplify rail-to-bracket connections.

Top Rack Technology’s FreePivot Bracket features a 360-degree rotatable rail fastener and built-in height adjustment to accommodate varying rooftop conditions and simplify solar panel installations. Credit: Top Rack Technology

But perhaps the most versatile addition is the FreePivot Bracket. Its 360-degree rotatable rail fastener allows installers to accommodate attachment points that aren’t perfectly aligned. Built-in height adjustment helps compensate for uneven roof surfaces, while its all-metal construction supports durability. The one-orientation design and single-socket installation concept are also intended to reduce tool changes and keep crews moving.

Beyond the six new products, TRT is showcasing its established portfolio, including the TRT 01 Rail-less Base System, TRT 02 Rail-Base System, and TRT 03 Tile Hook System.

The company is also highlighting its Online BOM Design Tool, which supports project setup, structural design inputs, module layouts, engineering verification, and automated bill-of-materials generation. Connecting digital planning with physical mounting solutions could offer installers and distributors a more streamlined path from project design to field execution.

Installation hardware doesn’t need to be flashy to make a difference. Improvements in attachment flexibility, waterproofing, assembly time, and aesthetics can translate into meaningful operational advantages.

#5827. Almost 90% of Europe’s Battery Production at Risk from Franco-German Car Deal

Europe has huge potential for battery plants but calls to weaken or delay the Industrial Accelerator Act and carmaker demands to weaken the EU’s CO2 standards, risk handing Europe’s battery value chain to foreign competitors

The Franco-German car deal will secure just 13% of Europe’s planned battery cell production in 2030, new T&E analysis shows. Weak Made-in-Europe rules, combined with weaker emission reduction targets, risks handing over the market for battery production to Europe’s competitors, says T&E.

The IAA introduces local content requirements to ensure scarce public subsidies and corporate tax incentives anchor homegrown EU manufacturing. 100 GWh of cells and 250 GWh of cathodes could leak abroad because of weak Made-in-Europe rules, the analysis shows — equivalent to 1.5 million battery packs.

At the same time, Europe’s battery industry faces a double blow from the EU Parliament’s proposals to weaken emission reduction rules which will cut cell demand by 247 GWh or approximately 3.7 million EU made EVs between 2027 and 2032 — almost three times the annual capacity of Europe’s largest battery plant, LG Energy Solution in Poland.

Franziska Grüning, Raw Materials Officer at T&E, said: “The Franco-German deal is a disaster for Europe’s battery industry. Securing a full European battery value chain, from mining to production, and the high value jobs it will provide, should be a top priority for Europe’s leaders. Instead, they are debating how far to weaken rules that would secure domestically produced batteries. Weak local content incentives for batteries in the IAA will send a terrible political signal for the future of Europe’s battery industry. Homegrown companies struggling to cross the valley of death should benefit from an IAA demand boost.”

With many cell factories only just beginning to ramp up, most EU production expected by 2030 comes from plants that have not yet entered production. The future of factories such as Lyten (formerly Northvolt) in Sweden, PowerCo Salzgitter in Germany, or Verkor Dunkirk in France, could be jeopardised by this deal.

The EU’s battery industry must not go the same way as its solar panels industry, says T&E, which calls on the EU to reject the Franco-German deal proposal on batteries, and stick to the timelines and battery requirements of the EU Commission’s proposal. In addition, a strict Made-in-EU scope, or at most a narrow list of trusted partners of the EU27 plus the European Economic Area (EEA) and the UK, must be introduced, and there should be no further weakening of the car CO2 standards.

#5825. The Return Of Project Hail Mary … And Next Steps For Artemis, Too

Word has just dropped that the mega-hit sci-fi film “Project Hail Mary” has returned to the big screen for the holiday season with its nail-biting tale of science, adventure and friendship. Catch a brand new Hail Mary trailer on YouTube while the action heats up here on Earth, including solicitations for the next class of astronauts, the formation of the new US Space Academy, and next steps for the Artemis mission to the Moon.

On November 7, NASA (the National Aeronautics and Space Administration) began taking applications for the next class of astronauts. The deadline is 11:59 PM ET on December 3.

NASA received more than 8,000 applications during the last round of astronaut recruitment, in 2024. Only 10 candidates were selected — but don’t be discouraged. Just look at the payoff as described by NASA:

The next generation of astronauts will train to operate on the lunar surface, live and work beyond Earth, and take on missions that will push the boundaries of human exploration farther than ever before.

“The next generation of astronaut candidates could help write history while inspiring future scientists, engineers, and explorers,” adds Scott Tingle, chief astronaut and chair of the NASA Astronaut Selection Board. Interested? Apply here.

If you blinked, you missed the formation of the new US Space Academy. However, the folks over at the Louisiana Economic Development agency were on the ball. On November 12, LED announced that Louisiana has thrown its hat into the ring for the privilege of hosting the the forthcoming Space Academy.

“The Space Academy is a proposed NASA-supported federal academy that will educate the next generation of astronauts, engineers, scientists, and leaders for America’s space programs,” LED explains.

If all goes according to plan, the first Space Academy class will occupy Louisiana State University in New Orleans with a roster of 300 students in the fall of 2028, with room for a total of 4,000 — or more — to follow.

If you’re wondering why Louisiana thinks it has a good shot at hosting such an endeavor, Louisiana Governor Jeff Landry summarized the case in a press statement. “The United States Space Academy belongs in a state that builds the rockets, supports our nation’s military and is now home to the world’s largest spaceport,” Landry enthused.

As described in an LED press release, the state’s qualifications include hosting “America’s Rocket Factory,” aka the Michoud Assembly Facility in New Orleans. The facility is owned by NASA and managed by the Marshall Space Flight Center, which among other projects has just released a new album of “baby star sounds.”

The idea of a dedicated center for space education and training bubbled up from the White House just a few months ago, in August. NASA promptly jumped on it, inviting the governors of all 50 states to compete for the new facility.

Louisiana is determined to beat them all to the punch. With LED in the lead, collaborators on the state’s Space Academy proposal include Louisiana State University, the City of New Orleans, the business organization Greater New Orleans, Inc., and the New Orleans subsidiary of the leading utility Entergy among others, with all of the state’s US Senators and Representatives enlisted in support.

“A dedicated state fund, the Louisiana U.S. Space Academy Fund, will cover startup needs and independent operating infrastructure, lowering the federal investment needed to launch and sustain the Academy,” LED adds, noting that two other schools — Southern University at New Orleans and Tulane University — are also lending their support to the LSU campus.

LED and its partners are leaning heavily on the presence of the Michoud Assembly Facility to leverage its claim to host the Space Academy. As LED notes, Michoud has been front and center in crewed space flight for 65 years, and it sits only about nine miles from the LSU campus

“Michoud built the Saturn V first stages for Apollo, every Space Shuttle external tank, and the core stages for the Space Launch System rockets that power NASA’s Artemis missions,” LED explains.

“The National Center for Advanced Manufacturing at Michoud develops the composite and metallic manufacturing processes behind that hardware,” the agency adds.

LED Secretary Susan B. Bourgeois also chipped in her two cents. “Louisiana is building one of the most consequential aerospace ecosystems in the country, and the U.S. Space Academy would give us the opportunity to build the talent pipeline alongside it,” Bourgeois said.

“Cadets would learn alongside leaders in government and commercial and space, including NASA’s Michoud Assembly Center and Stennis Space Center, SpaceX Starbase Louisiana, Michoud tenants including Boeing, Lockheed Martin, Textron Systems, and Vivace,” LED elaborates, also taking note of the presence of the Naval Information Warfare Center among other resources.

“Greater New Orleans anchors the middle of America’s southern space corridor, which runs from Kennedy Space Center at Cape Canaveral to Johnson Space Center in Houston and SpaceX’s Starbase in South Texas, with Marshall Space Flight Center in Huntsville to the north,” LED adds again for good measure.

As for the Artemis mission, as of October NASA seemed pretty optimistic about the chances for sending people to the Moon sooner rather than later. A new schedule calls for an Artemis III test flight in Earth orbit next year. The flight will include shakeouts of docking and other systems between NASA’s Orion spacecraft and lunar landers from both from SpaceX and Blue Origin.

“Four astronauts will fly aboard Orion, launched on NASA’s SLS (Space Launch System) rocket, for a planned 14-day mission in Earth orbit to reduce risk ahead of the Artemis IV moon landing mission,” NASA explained.

“The Artemis III mission will unfold in sequence,” NASA explained. “Blue Origin will launch a Blue Moon lander test article into Earth orbit, SLS will launch Orion and its crew, docking with the Blue Moon lander test article, then SpaceX will launch a Version 3 Starship lander test article while Orion will undock from Blue Moon and dock with Starship.”

Assuming that all goes off without a hitch, 2028 will see US astronauts on walking on the Moon, presumably before anybody else gets there.

The last time US citizens set foot on the Moon, millions of Terrestrials witnessed the fuzzy, flickering action on live TV. If you were among them, share the experience in the discussion thread What was it like? Did everyone cheer? Or did somebody say something random such as “I need a cigarette,” and is that the moment that burns especially bright in your memory? Asking for a friend…

#5823. Last Chance to Get It Right: The Industrial Accelerator Act Will Make or Break Europe’s Battery Industry

How the Franco-German auto deal, a wide Made-with-EU scope, and carmakers abandon Europe’s battery value chain to foreign competitors.

There is a growing consensus that Europe must reduce its reliance on Chinese cleantech, especially batteries

The IAA marks a major shift in EU industrial policy. It introduces Union content incentives to ensure scarce public subsidies and corporate tax incentives are used to support European manufacturing. This demand-side pull is crucial if the energy transition is to deliver high-value industrial jobs and lessen critical geopolitical dependencies. There is a growing consensus that Europe must reduce its reliance on Chinese cleantech, especially batteries. But several proposals currently before co-legislators risk severely undermining that overarching goal. Diluting battery criteria in the IAA means giving up on a strategic value chain for Europe, leaving it to China only.

T&E’s assessment shows that only an IAA with a calibrated timeline, robust battery requirements and a strict geographical scope will deliver substantial benefits for Europe. Without secured demand, announced industrial capacity will simply not materialise.

The Franco-German automotive deal from 16 October 2026 is a major blow for Europe’s battery industry as it severely dilutes incentives for batteries. It suggests battery sourcing requirements for eligible vehicles of 10% in 2028-2029, 20% in 2030-2031 and 70% from 2032 on. This is far below what the market already does: over 50% of cells in electric vehicles (EVs) sold in the EU are already Made-in-Europe. By 2030, the deal would only secure 13% (62 GWh) of Europe’s announced cell production: a further 206 GWh would likely find European buyers, though with no guarantee, while 220 GWh would find no demand at all. Even in 2032, the 70% would still not secure a substantial share of potential cell capacity (only 45%).

Another major risk is applying a wide Made-with-EU geographical scope to battery criteria. Compared to a strict EU-only scope, including free trade agreement (FTA) partners puts around 100 GWh of cell demand and 250 GWh of cathode active materials (CAM) demand at risk of leakage in 2030. Limiting the scope to trusted partners only helps to a limited extent. Since Europe can produce enough cells to meet demand under a Made-in-EU IAA, adding partner countries risks leaving EU production without offtakers: even with trusted partners only, a third of potential EU cell supply could be affected. For CAM, around 30% of expected EU supply could be left without a buyer under a trusted-partners scope, rising to 50% under an extended one.

The midstream, CAM and their precursors (pCAM), is the weakest link in Europe’s battery value chain. Boosting this key segment and diversifying away from China is essential for resilience as it shields European industry from supply disruptions and export controls. Under a strict Made-in-EU scope, reaching IAA CAM requirements is technically and commercially viable by 2030, requiring only limited sourcing from trusted partners (33 GWh cell-equivalent). From 2032, EU production can cover demand from EVs under support schemes. A Made-in-EU IAA would generate 323 GWh of extra EU CAM demand in 2032, around the output of six mid-sized EU CAM plants, bringing total EU-made CAM to 407 GWh (securing 94% of expected EU supply).

pCAM is just as vital for resilience and to establish local offtake for European refiners and recyclers. A 28% EU pCAM requirement in 2032 applied to a carmaker’s entire fleet sales (not only for vehicles eligible for public support) creates enough demand for expected EU supply to materialise and projects to reach Final Investment Decision (FID). Onshoring CAM and pCAM adds only a modest cost of $10.6/kWh for Nickel Manganese Cobalt (NMC) and $6.5/kWh for Lithium Iron Phosphate (LFP) batteries. This is a necessary sovereignty premium that will fall as the EU supply chain matures.

The car CO₂ standards are as critical to industrial delivery as the IAA. Even under a strict Made-in-EU scope, the European Parliament’s position as approved by the leading committee in November 2026 would sharply cut expected EV sales and reduce EU cell demand by 247 GWh between 2027 and 2032, which equals 3.7 million EU made EVs.

Finally, the proposed fleet booster mechanism fails to drive industrial onshoring and relocation if its compliance trigger is set below 85%. Carmakers’ efforts to lower that threshold aim at getting a stamp of approval on their sourcing strategy in China. A major risk for public subsidies ending up benefiting imported cars with Chinese batteries. Attempts to lower the Booster below a 85% threshold should be rejected, especially if combined with weaker battery criteria.

It’s key to reject the Franco-German deal proposal on batteries.

Battery criteria in this deal are too weak to create additional demand and undermine the IAA’s core purpose.

By Franziska Grüning, Raw Materials OfficerBrussels (EU), T&E.

Jordane Faizant, Analyst, FleetsBrussels (EU)

Jeanne Andre, Analyst, Raw MaterialsBrussels (EU)

Transport & Environment’s (T&E) vision is a zero-emission mobility system that is affordable and has minimal impacts on our health, climate and environment. Created over 30 years ago, we have shaped some of Europe’s most important environmental laws.

#5821. Smarter Cooling, Stronger Reliability: Advancing Wind Turbine Gearbox Performance

As the wind energy industry matures, operators and service providers are increasingly focused on maximizing turbine availability while controlling long-term maintenance costs. Although major drivetrain components often receive the spotlight, one overlooked system can have an outsized impact on turbine reliability and performance: gearbox oil cooling.

Heat exchangers play a critical role in maintaining proper oil temperature and viscosity within wind turbine gearboxes. When cooling systems become clogged with airborne debris, dust, or organic material, oil temperatures rise, lubrication performance declines, and component stress increases. Over time, these conditions can contribute to reduced gearbox efficiency, elevated maintenance demands, and avoidable downtime.

“Wind operators today are balancing increasing performance expectations with the realities of aging fleets and rising maintenance costs. Technologies that improve cooling efficiency and reduce service exposure can have a meaningful impact on long-term turbine reliability and operational profitability,” according to Mike Erickson, HYDAC’s Market Manager for the Wind Industry.

Traditional “panel cut” heat exchanger designs have long been susceptible to clogging due to their tightly packed fin structures. In many wind environments — particularly agricultural, dusty, or high-pollen regions — debris accumulation can severely restrict airflow. Oil leaks can further compound the issue by trapping contaminants within the exchanger core, making cleaning more difficult and labor intensive.

This operational challenge has driven the need for more resilient cooling technologies designed specifically for real-world wind farm conditions.

HYDAC’s new “Square Wave” heat exchanger design represents a significant advancement in addressing these reliability concerns. Engineered to allow debris to pass more freely through the exchanger fins, the design minimizes clogging while improving cooling efficiency under demanding operating conditions.

The importance of effective cooling cannot be overstated. Wind turbine gearbox oil typically operates within an optimal temperature range of approximately 45°C to 65°C. Even modest temperature increases can significantly impact oil viscosity. According to field data, a 12°C rise in oil temperature can reduce viscosity by nearly 28 percent — affecting lubrication quality and accelerating wear on critical drivetrain components.

Field trials have demonstrated measurable operational benefits from the Square Wave design. During a one-year trial on GE 1.x turbines, the upgraded exchanger consistently reduced clogging and lowered maintenance requirements compared to traditional panel cut cores. Operators reported reduced up-tower cleaning time and fewer temperature-related cooling faults.

Field validation across multiple turbine platforms has further reinforced the operational advantages of the Square Wave design. In one case study involving legacy 1 MW-class turbines operating in the Pacific Northwest, a major renewable energy operator installed pilot sets of Square Wave heat exchangers as part of a cooling performance evaluation. Installation teams reported the upgraded exchangers integrated seamlessly into the existing platform with no retrofit complications.

After initial operating data demonstrated improved cooling consistency, the operator expanded the evaluation program with additional units. Long-term SCADA analysis later showed turbines equipped with Square Wave technology operate approximately 20 to 25 percent cooler than comparable turbines using conventional heat exchanger designs under similar operating conditions. The data also highlighted another important advantage: the Square Wave cores continued to maintain airflow and cooling efficiency in debris-heavy environments where traditional exchanger designs became increasingly restricted over time.

The successful deployment also generated interest from additional wind service organizations seeking retrofit-ready cooling upgrades that could improve turbine reliability while reducing maintenance burdens across aging fleets.

“What operators are increasingly recognizing is that small improvements in thermal management can create meaningful gains in turbine reliability, maintenance efficiency, and long-term asset performance,” said Erickson. “The field data we’re seeing continues to validate the value of designing cooling systems specifically for real-world wind operating environments.”

More recently, long-term SCADA data from Mitsubishi platforms further validated the technology’s performance. After two years of operation, turbines equipped with Square Wave heat exchangers operated approximately 19 to 25 percent cooler than reference turbines using standard exchanger designs under comparable operating conditions.

For wind asset owners and service providers, the implications are substantial: Improved thermal management supports healthier oil viscosity, more stable gearbox operation, and potentially longer component life. At the same time, reduced cleaning frequency and fewer cooling-related service events translate directly into lower operational expenditures and increased turbine availability.

As the industry continues to prioritize lifecycle optimization and energy production efficiency, innovative subsystem upgrades such as advanced heat exchanger technology are becoming increasingly valuable. Rather than waiting for costly failures or performance degradation, operators are seeking practical retrofit solutions that improve reliability without requiring major system redesigns.

“Effective thermal management is critical to protecting gearbox health and maintaining oil performance over time. By addressing one of the most common causes of cooling degradation — debris accumulation — operators can improve uptime while reducing unnecessary maintenance interventions,” said HYDAC’s Erickson.

For wind stakeholders evaluating ways to improve gearbox reliability, reduce maintenance exposure, and enhance turbine performance, cooling system optimization deserves greater attention. Proven retrofit technologies like advanced heat exchanger designs can offer a practical path toward improved uptime and operational efficiency without requiring major system overhauls.

As fleets age and performance expectations continue to rise, collaboration between operators, service providers, and technology partners will be essential to building more resilient wind assets for the future. Now is the time to evaluate whether existing cooling systems are supporting — or limiting — long-term turbine reliability goals.

“HYDAC’s goal is to help wind owners and service providers solve practical operational challenges in ways that create long-term value across the entire asset lifecycle. Innovations like the Square Wave heat exchanger are designed to improve reliability, simplify maintenance, and support greater energy production over time.”

Don’t let cooling system limitations impact turbine performance.

Proactive cooling optimization can help operators reduce maintenance exposure, protect critical drivetrain components, and maximize energy production across the asset lifecycle. To learn how HYDAC’s Square Wave heat exchanger technology is helping wind operators improve reliability and lower operating costs, connect with our wind energy specialists today or visit: https://www.hydac.com/en-us/industry-solutions/renewable-energy/wind-power/

#5820. MG U9 EV Ute — It’s A Monster Truck

We visit the MG dealership and get to know the MG U9 EV ute up close and personal.

As I have said many times to CleanTechnica readers, Australia needs more BEV utes. The PHEV utes are taking a bite out of diesel sales, but we need some monster truck battery electrics to push electrification deep into the heart of the bush-bashing Aussie tradie. I think that MG may have achieved that with the launch of the MG U9 EV. Yesterday, Majella and I went to have a look. Even Russell Coight would be impressed and the Mitsubishi Outlander PHEV should be feeling threatened. Take a break and watch the funny video. I doubt it will affect the sales of the Shark though.

So, yesterday, Majella I set out to have an adventure visiting the various car yards near our home. We were actually planning to look at some new electric vans, but ended up in the MG showroom with a very knowledgeable sales rep exploring the MG U9 EV. What a surprise! An early morning google search assured me that there were none in Brisbane available to view. This dealership not only brought one out from the pre-delivery area for us to check out, but also told us they had already sold and delivered their first one and had six more on order.

The MG U9 EV (it also comes in diesel) is joining the thin ranks of BEV utes already available in Australia — the KGM Musso and the Toyota Hilux BEV. So far this year, 269 KGM Mussos have sold in Australia. 174 MG U9 EVs are listed as delivered, possibly mainly to dealers. The cost of the MG U9 EV is approximately AU$80,000, just a few thousand more than the Toyota Hilux BEV. The diesel version of the MG sells for around AU$50,000.

The sales rep told me about his first customer for the MG ute: “He was keen. He already had home solar and a battery and was producing far more electricity than he could use. His solution: trade in the old diesel ute on a U9 EV ‘rolling battery.’ It will complement his Kia EV6. With the 6.6 kWh of the U9 vehicle-to-load [capability], he plans to power his house.” The ecosystem expands. For those with a query, it is not worth selling power back to the grid.

The MG U9 has a 102.2 kWh LFP battery, big enough to give a range of up to 430 km and send this behemoth hurtling down the road — accelerating to 100 km an hour from zero in 5.8 seconds. That’s as fast as my new Tesla! And an advertised top speed of 190 km/hour. Just as well it has disc brakes all round — with the front ones ventilated. And to recharge — 42 minutes from 20–80%.

Tare weight comes in at 2815 kg, towing capacity is 3500 kg, and it has the capacity to carry 685 kg in the tray. Unladen ground clearance is listed as 230 mm, and it has a wading depth of 550 mm. Length is 5500 mm; width 2265 mm; height 1874 mm. Very similar dimensions to the GMC Hummer with half the battery capacity. Hummer: L 5507 mm; W 2201 mm; H 2055 mm.

The MG U9 BEV includes a fold-down rear bulkhead to extend cargo-bed length inside the cabin, plus a 1,200 L tub capacity and a 236 L front trunk (frunk).

Google tells me: “The MG U9 EV Dual-Cab Ute comes equipped from the factory with a four-wheel electronic air suspension system offering over 100 mm of vertical height adjustment (including an Off-Road Lift mode, and automatic lowering by -20 mm at 80 km/h and -40 mm at 120 km/h.”

Of course there is a lot more to know, especially about tech and safety features. I am just whetting your appetite. You can find out more here. Or watch a video review on YouTube here.

At the moment, the dealership is fielding 8 enquiries a week online, but it is expecting some headwinds in mid 2027 when the Australian federal government is planning to review Fringe Benefit Tax benefits for electric vehicles. My comment was that somehow car manufacturers seem to find ways of reducing costs when government support is modified.

While I peppered the sales rep with questions, Majella did her usual photographic exploration and analysis.

She was impressed by the massive frunk, as was I — you could fit a whole body in there! The frunk can be opened from the outside by a physical button.

… and it has a power point for your fridge or coffeemaker!

The ute features doors with physical buttons and handles for ease of access.

The gear lever reminded Majella of the ones we see in the cockpit of a plane.

A variety of charging options for your tech are provided: cigarette lighter, USB charger, and more.

Perhaps MG went overboard with buttons — lots of physical buttons on the dash and on the steering wheel, plus the screen. Majella thought that this was a comfortable wheel size and shape for a long drive.

Behind the back seats in a cubby hole we have the charge cords: a trickle charger and type 2 to type 2.

The back tailgate has a discreet button to push to fold it down so you can load the tray.

An emergency glass breaker and a seat belt cutter is provided.

Although MG appears to have thought of everything, Majella commented that the only thing they don’t supply is a first aid kit.

The air suspension is easily adjusted air suspension from the centre screen. The sales rep confided that the staff were playing with it the day before.

The 360 degree camera extends to the trailer, with an aerial view for easily reversing.

Those looking for a truck to haul stuff, power their tools, and store excess energy would do well to consider the MG U9 EV. It may not be a Hummer, but it’s close. For tradies and bush bashers, the future can now be bright and electric.

David Waterworth is a retired teacher who divides his time between looking after his grandchildren and trying to make sure they have a planet to live on. He is long on Tesla [NASDAQ:TSLA].

#5819. Europe Spent €16.5 Million Proving Again That Hydrogen Burns in a Turbine

Hydrogen burns. Turbines spin. Their exhaust can make steam. Kawasaki demonstrated hydrogen-fired electricity and heat production at Kobe Port Island in 2018, before Europe funded the first HYFLEXPOWER programme. Six years later, Siemens Energy is still issuing announcements about hydrogen combustion at a single turbine in a French paper mill. European and British taxpayers have supplied roughly €16.5 million for the two research programmes, and the same installation has generated at least 78 identified press appearances. Neither the grants nor the publicity have produced a commercial reason to make hydrogen from electricity and burn it for industrial electricity and steam.

The latest Siemens Energy announcement, dated November 16, 2026, describes an SGT-400 turbine at Smurfit Westrock’s Saillat-sur-Vienne paper mill operating on pure hydrogen through startup, load changes and full-load cogeneration. The equipment can operate under those conditions. Hydrogen combustion and heat recovery were already established. Extending the operating range of one manufacturer’s turbine has not changed hydrogen’s production cost, the losses involved in making it or the available alternatives. No second commercial installation has emerged from the programme.

Siemens’ own March 2024 technical article supplied the most important numbers. The electrolyzer installed at the paper mill produces 16.4 kg of hydrogen per hour. The turbine consumes about 810 kg per hour when producing 10 MW of electricity. Onsite production covers 2.02% of hourly fuel demand. Nearly 50 times that electrolyzer capacity would be needed to supply the turbine continuously at the stated operating point. Later testing used hydrogen brought to the site.

The 2023 campaign accumulated about 90 hours of hydrogen and natural-gas blending tests, including just over four hours on pure hydrogen. Siemens identified limited hydrogen availability as the constraint. The 2026 announcement supplies no cumulative full-load hydrogen operating hours, delivered fuel price or routine cost of electricity and steam. These are the figures an industrial customer needs before buying equipment. Announcing another combustion test avoids the question of who will supply hundreds of kilograms of affordable hydrogen every hour for years.

My complete TFIE Strategy Briefing investigation examines the funding records, Siemens’ published operating figures, the energy balance and an audited chronology of 78 press appearances surrounding the same turbine.

At an illustrative 50 kWh of electricity per kilogram of hydrogen, manufacturing the turbine’s 810 kg of hourly fuel requires approximately 40.5 MW of electricity. The turbine returns about 10 MW of electricity plus usable exhaust heat. Compression, storage and delivery add equipment and expense. The mill needs electricity and process steam; converting electricity into gas and burning it to recover those services introduces losses and costs that direct electrification avoids.

France is an especially poor location for this detour. RTE’s 2025 electricity review reports that 95.2% of French generation was low-carbon, with average generation emissions of 19.6 gCO₂e/kWh. Direct grid power can supply the electrical load. Industrial heat pumps, mechanical vapour recompression, electric boilers and thermal storage can supply process heat and steam in combinations designed around the mill’s operating requirements. The programme should have compared these complete systems before committing public money to more hydrogen turbine retrofits.

A paper mill in Finland is already doing the industrial work. Delfort’s Tervakoski plant has operated a Turboden heat pump and mechanical vapour-recompression installation since January 2026. It supplies about 12 MW of process steam at temperatures up to 170°C, with a reported coefficient of performance around two. It delivers useful steam without a hydrogen-production plant, hydrogen deliveries or a hydrogen-fired turbine. The exact plant integration will vary, but the alternative is operating commercially while HyCoFlex continues issuing test announcements.

The funding was directed at the wrong problem. HYFLEXPOWER received €10.48 million in EU support. HyCoFlex received another €4.44 million. A Siemens presentation identifies approximately €1.56 million more in UK support, bringing the public contribution across the programmes to about €16.48 million. Both programmes were designed around making existing gas-turbine cogeneration equipment compatible with hydrogen. The grants paid for additional turbine operating conditions instead of establishing that hydrogen was a sensible way to provide the plant’s energy.

The programme’s economics were weak before the second award. In October 2023, Siemens presented a scenario requiring electricity below €35/MWh to bring hydrogen fuel costs into parity with natural gas under its 2022 cost assumptions. The HYFLEXPOWER final workshop later examined favourable operating cases with hydrogen substitution and electrolyzer utilization both above 90%. European reporting also identified renewable electricity below €40/MWh as a condition for favourable deployment economics. France’s 2025 wholesale electricity averaged €61.1/MWh. Its negative-price hours were occasional. They could not supply a plant operating more than 90% of the time. These conditions were set out before the research programme ended, yet funding continued for more combustion trials.

Siemens also has a clear commercial interest in preserving turbine markets. Its 2025 presentation explicitly described protecting existing assets and future gas-turbine investments in a decarbonizing world as a benefit of hydrogen compatibility. A turbine supplier is entitled to pursue its products with its own money. The purpose of public industrial-decarbonization funding is to reduce emissions and costs, not to protect combustion-equipment sales from direct-electric competition.

The TFIE media audit counted 19 press appearances in 2020, 13 in the 2022 installation and blending phase, 35 around the 2023 tests and award, four in 2024 and seven through November 16, 2026. Those 78 appearances trace back to a handful of announcements about one installation. They are not 78 independent engineering advances or 78 deployments. The headline count swelled while the operating fleet did not.

Public funders should require equipment-neutral comparisons of delivered industrial energy services before underwriting another hydrogen retrofit. The evidence needed is familiar: a year’s reliable electricity and steam, actual operating cost, lifecycle emissions, fuel contracts, and performance against direct-electric alternatives. HyCoFlex has supplied more hydrogen-combustion publicity without establishing those outcomes. The problem was not that Europe failed to make a turbine burn hydrogen. It was that public money kept flowing after the relevant energy and economic questions already had answers.

The full TFIE Strategy Briefing investigation contains the audited funding and media evidence, the 2% onsite hydrogen-supply calculation and the direct-electric industrial steam comparison. TFIE Strategy provides evidence-based assessments of industrial energy investments and public programmes.

Michael Barnard has 1557 posts and counting. See all posts by Michael Barnard

#5818. Can Tonsillectomy Help for Non-Asian Patients With IgA Nephropathy?

— Study showed more achieved early remission when adding surgery to immunosuppression

Immunoglobulin A nephropathy (IgAN), a chronic autoimmune kidney disease, is driven by mucosal immune activation that produces galactose-deficient IgA1, forming immune complexes that deposit in the glomeruli and trigger progressive kidney injury.

While tonsillectomy combined with pulsed corticosteroids has long been an established treatment in Japanese practice, international guidelines have explicitly steered nephrologists away from surgical intervention in non-Japanese patients due to a lack of evidence outside Asian cohorts.

However, a recent prospective comparative study in Nephrology Dialysis Transplantation suggested that pairing tonsillectomy with immunosuppressive therapy may substantially improve remission rates and curb kidney function decline in white patients at high risk of IgAN progression.

"One could not be surprised at all, considering IgAN as the kidney's response to aberrant activation of MALT [mucosa-associated lymphoid tissue]," study author Vladimir Dobronravov, MD, PhD, of Pavlov University in St. Petersburg, Russia, told MedPage Today. "It would be logical to assume that removing the trigger concurrently with targeting of glomerular inflammation might lead to a reduction in disease activity and better outcomes."

In the single-center, nonrandomized study, Dobronravov and a co-author assessed 221 white patients with primary IgAN at high risk of progression -- 85 who underwent tonsillectomy alongside immunosuppressive therapy and 136 who received immunosuppression alone.

Nearly all patients (94.6%) received renin-angiotensin system inhibitors, and 72.3% were treated with glucocorticoid monotherapy (intravenous methylprednisolone induction followed by a 6- to 12-month oral prednisolone taper). Average patient age was 35 years, and 52% were male.

High risk of disease progression was defined as proteinuria over 1 g/24 hours with hematuria, or proteinuria under 1 g/24 hours with hematuria plus either an estimated 5-year renal replacement therapy probability above 11% or at least one active histological lesion.

Over a median follow-up of 33 months, patients who underwent tonsillectomy were significantly more likely to achieve early overall (complete or partial) remission (HR 3.36, 95% CI 2.30-4.92) and early complete remission (HR 6.32, 95% CI 2.62-15.22) compared with those who received immunosuppression alone. Results remained consistent after 1:1 propensity-score matching to account for baseline differences between groups.

Nearly all (95.2%) in the combination group achieved early overall remission (vs 72.6% of those on immunosuppression alone) and 33.9% achieved complete remission (vs 11.3% on immunosuppression alone).

"Such a substantial rate of early remission can unlikely be expected at the 30% to 62% decline in baseline proteinuria, as reported in recent and ongoing studies of targeted-release budesonide, complement inhibitors, and anti-[B-cell activating factor/a proliferation-inducing ligand] therapies," said Dobronravov.

Disease progression -- defined as at least a 40% decrease in estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) from baseline, an eGFR below 15 mL/min/1.73 m², or initiation of renal replacement therapy -- occurred in three patients (3.5%) on combination therapy compared with 39 patients (28.9%) in the immunosuppression-alone group (adjusted HR 0.13, 95% CI 0.04-0.43).

"In contrast to the current guidelines, the combination therapy may represent a reasonable and safe practical approach to the improvement of long-term prognosis in the selected subpopulation of white patients with severe IgAN," said Dobronravov. "The study results raise a broader question about how the control of other potential MALT activation factors, beyond tonsillectomy, might influence the course of IgAN."

Despite the promising findings, Dobronravov noted that "we are far from setting the suggested approach in opposition to others," particularly given the rapidly evolving field of novel IgAN therapies.

"The future of effective IgAN management is likely to be based on individualized, mechanism-based treatment selection," he suggested. "Ideally, the predominant pathogenic MALT pathways should be identified in each patient and therapeutically addressed at multiple complementary levels."

George Bonifant, MD, training program director for the nephrology fellowship at Northwell Staten Island University in New York City, who wasn't involved with the study, urged similar restraint, stating he would "place [tonsillectomy] in the category of a biologically plausible intervention that deserves further investigation, but not alongside established medical treatments for non-Japanese patients."

"The tonsils and other upper-airway mucosal lymphoid tissues are considered important sites of IgA1 production, so removing tonsils was proposed to reduce a source of pathogenic IgA production," he told MedPage Today. "Thus, the rationale for this treatment choice is immunologic."

However, Bonifant said it is too early to recommend adding tonsillectomy solely to improve IgAN outcomes on the strength of this single study.

"What would be persuasive is a multicenter randomized trial in non-Japanese patients, with both groups receiving contemporary background treatment. It should measure sustained proteinuria reduction, kidney function decline, kidney failure, surgical complications, quality of life, and total exposure to immunosuppression," he noted. "The clinical question is not just whether the urine looks better after surgery; it is whether patients keep their kidney function longer with an acceptable overall treatment burden."

"This is encouraging research, but it is not yet a reason to have your tonsils removed to treat your kidney disease," said Bonifant.

#5817. $1199 — Anyland Rev Pro: An Electric Dirt Bike With a Wheelie Assist Built for Beginners

When I was a kid, I loved to ride. Riding a bike, for me, is synonymous with childhood, in fact. We used them to get ourselves places, but we also just used them to entertain ourselves. Inevitably, this led to popping wheelies, building ramps and trying jumps, and doing all sorts of other stuff my Mom just didn’t want to know about, no matter how much I wanted to brag. As an adult, I still love to ride — as the famous saying from Animal Farm goes, “four wheels good, two wheels mo’ bettah!” (I’m paraphrasing, a lot, but you get the point).

So when Anyland hit us up to do a review of their new Anyland Rev Pro, an all-electric dirt bike capable of 34 mph and assisted wheelies, the inner child in me jumped at the chance.

Wheelies look effortless until you try one. Anyone who has learned on a dirt bike knows the usual lesson plan: too little throttle and the front wheel never lifts, too much and you’re flat on your back. Anyland, a startup electric dirt bike brand, created the Rev Pro as an entry-level electric dirt bike with a controller that lifts the front wheel to a preset angle and holds it there, so a new rider can focus on balance instead of juggling throttle and brake.

The system, which Anyland sells as WELIXON and says it co-engineered with controller maker Moxin, offers three angle settings: 25°, 35°, and 45°. Riders start at the lowest angle and move up as their balance improves. According to Anyland, the controller delivers continuous, smooth power to keep the front end at the chosen angle, which is designed to reduce the risk of a “loop out,” where the bike flips backward, one of the most common ways wheelie learners get hurt. Once you’re ready, you can switch the assist off, and the Rev Pro behaves like a regular throttle-controlled electric dirt bike for practicing manual wheelies.

Underneath the trick, the Rev Pro is a lightweight, mid-drive electric dirt bike. Anyland lists:

The speed modes set the first speed caps as low as 12 mph, and matter for the audience Anyland is targeting: teenagers and adults who are new to dirt bikes. Starting in the lowest mode lets a rider build skills before moving up, and gives parents a way to keep a first-timer in a gentler setting.

The Rev Pro arrives in a compact box and includes all the tools you’ll need to set the bike up. My friend and I (two untrained professionals) did it in about 40 minutes:

In short, the ride is really fun. I found the suspension incredibly responsive and comfortable, the acceleration smooth and quick, and the riding position much more comfortable than I anticipated. It’s been raining non-stop where I live, so while I got this bike a few weeks ago in the mail, I haven’t had a chance to get it up onto the trails yet, so stay tuned for that. Testing it on the dirt sidewalks and running over curbs shows me it will likely be a great addition to my weekends.

A wheelie assist doesn’t make any dirt bike risk-free. Wear a helmet, gloves, and protective gear every time, and start in the lowest speed mode at the 25° setting. Practice using the rear brake, supervise younger riders, and stick to private land or designated off-road areas. Check your local rules before riding anywhere else, since many electric dirt bikes aren’t legal on public roads.

Anyland’s Black Friday and Cyber Monday price starts as low as $1,199, and prices have varied, so check the current price before you buy.

For a startup brand in a crowded field of lookalikes, the Rev Pro’s pitch is simple: a beginner-friendly bike built around one clever feature. If you’ve always wanted to learn a wheelie without the bruises, it’s worth a look.

#5816. Sierra Club Submits Comments Urging SEC to Preserve Shareholder Rights & Climate Accountability

WASHINGTON, D.C. — On Thursday, Sierra Club submitted two comment letters opposing the Securities and Exchange Commission’s (SEC) proposal to rescind Rule 14a-8, which allows shareholders to put proposals addressing climate risks and other corporate issues before fellow investors for a vote, and amend Rule 14a-4(c), which governs how companies may exercise discretionary voting authority over shareholder proposals. The Sierra Club submitted both a technical comment and a grassroots comment, reflecting concerns from over 1,100 of its members and supporters.

Together the submissions urge the SEC to withdraw its proposed changes and preserve investors’ ability to raise concerns directly with corporate management and press companies to address material risks, including climate-related risks, that can affect their long-term financial interests.

Jessye Waxman, Campaign Advisor on Sierra Club’s Sustainable Finance Campaign, released the following statement:

“Climate change and other systemic risks have real consequences for companies, investors, and the broader economy. Shareholder proposals give investors an important way to bring those risks to management’s attention, push for meaningful action, and hold companies accountable when they fail to act. The SEC’s proposals would weaken those tools, making it harder for investors to protect their long-term financial interests and leaving corporate management with less accountability for the risks its decisions create. The SEC’s proposals would shield corporate management from meaningful shareholder scrutiny, while undermining the very investor protections the Commission is charged with upholding.”

Rule 14a-8 establishes when public companies must include qualifying shareholder proposals in the materials they send investors ahead of shareholder meetings. The SEC’s proposed repeal would leave decisions about shareholder proposals to state law and individual companies’ governing documents, eliminating a federal framework that investors have used for more than 80 years.

Sierra Club’s technical comments explain that material risks, like climate change, can both have significant financial consequences for companies and create systemic financial risks that extend across investment portfolios and the broader economy. Shareholder proposals give investors a way to bring these concerns to company leadership and fellow shareholders, including by surfacing risks that management may overlook or have limited incentives to address. The letter emphasizes that, for decades, this process has served the broader market by helping investors collectively identify and address financially material risks.

The letter warns that replacing a consistent federal framework with varying state laws and corporate bylaws could make shareholder engagement less predictable and push disputes over proposals into costly litigation. It points to the increase in shareholder lawsuits challenging proposal exclusions during the 2026 proxy season as an indication that disputes do not disappear when the SEC steps back from administering the process; instead, they can shift to more costly and uncertain avenues.

The submission also opposes proposed amendments to Rule 14a-4(c), which would expand company management’s discretionary authority to vote shareholders’ proxies on proposals excluded from company proxy materials, unless shareholders affirmatively opt out. Sierra Club’s letter warns that these changes could allow management to vote on proposals that investors have not had an adequate opportunity to evaluate.

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.
