#5814. Pacific Power Begins New Customer Protections Against Data Centers

Regulators approve new agreement to ensure data centers pay for their own energy costs

SALEM, Oregon — The Oregon Public Utility Commission has approved an agreement between advocates and Pacific Power. Now, Pacific Power customers have a strong suite of protections against data centers’ impact on Oregonians’ energy bills.

Starting Jan. 1, 2027, new Pacific Power data centers will be held responsible for their own energy costs. These new rules will only apply to future data centers and existing ones making significant investments, which regulators identified as the most pressing challenge to address. Right now, the utility has very few existing data centers on its system.

“With a long line of data centers waiting at Pacific Power’s door, I’m confident that we have the right protections at the right time,” said Bob Jenks, Executive Director of Oregon Citizens’ Utility Board. “We were able to stop more extreme impacts on power bills just in time.”

Data centers over 100 MW will also have to pay a small surcharge to fund energy efficiency upgrades for low-income households. This includes funding for deep home retrofits, lowering energy use across the grid while saving customers on monthly bills.

“Many of the households we serve are already struggling to afford their energy bills and risk having their utilities shut off. Data centers will help fund weatherization, energy efficiency upgrades, and the critical home repairs that often prevent our clients from accessing these improvements in the first place,” said Charity Fain, Executive Director of Community Energy Project. “Requiring data centers to cover the costs of the energy infrastructure they need, rather than passing those costs on to residential customers, is an important protection for low-income households, seniors, and people with disabilities.”

Under this order, if there is a power emergency, Pacific Power can prioritize critical energy use for other customers, like renters and homeowners, by curtailing service to data centers. Increased electric demand by data centers strains the grid and increases the potential for blackouts during heat waves or hard freezes.

“This is a big win that puts Oregon at the forefront of managing data center growth the right way,” said Joshua Basofin, Oregon Clean Energy Program Director at Climate Solutions. “It protects Oregonians from rising costs and strengthens grid resilience. Data centers should not harm our ability to access essential electricity to power our lives and keep us safe all year.”

Regulators also ruled that new data centers can only be connected to Pacific Power’s grid if there is enough emissions-free electricity available to serve them. Under HB 2021, Pacific Power is required by law to serve all customers with 100% emissions-free electricity by 2040. This new rule will help manage costs for other customers outside of data centers during the transition to clean energy.

“We have long known that clean energy is the most affordable form of energy,” said Ben Brint, Senior Climate Program Director at Oregon Environmental Council. “This ruling is a win for data center accountability, and for Oregonians across the state — especially frontline communities already bearing the brunt of climate impacts and rising energy costs. It will save ratepayers money in the short and long run, allocating costs appropriately while advancing Oregon’s 100% clean energy goals.”

This change also comes at a time when many Oregonians are calling for broader changes to how the state handles data centers. Impacts on data centers are poised to be an issue again at the Oregon State Legislature in 2027.

“The POWER Act demonstrated the people’s power to fight back against the rising energy rates and ecosystem harms that large data centers inflict,” said Emily Bowes, Policy Strategist & Acting Director at Sierra Club Oregon. “In 2027, alongside new and returning climate champions, we will continue to fight for a clean energy transition in Salem and statewide.”

In 2025, the Oregon Legislature passed the POWER Act to help utilities rein in data centers’ added costs to Oregonians’ energy bills. Last fall, Portland General Electric began the work to roll out this new law in practice. In January, Pacific Power started a similar process for its customers. The next step is to address the few existing data centers on Pacific Power’s system, starting as early as summer 2027. It is unclear if the final phase will include rate adjustments.

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5813. Cardiovascular Screening Test Called Into Question

— MedPage Today reporters offer further insights into recently covered topics

The following is a transcript of the podcast episode:

Rachael Robertson: Hey everybody! Welcome to MedPod Today, the podcast series where MedPage Today reporters take a deeper look into the week's biggest healthcare stories. I'm your host, Rachael Robertson.

Cheryl Clark is kicking off today's episode with her reporting on an ongoing debate on whether to test all adults for Lipoprotein(a). Then, Kristen Monaco will fill us in on major developments in the GLP-1 medication space. To close out, I'll give us a rundown on the latest measles numbers and what it means that the U.S. lost its measles elimination status after 26 years.

A March recommendation from the American College of Cardiology (ACC) and 10 other medical societies for the first time said all adults should have their blood tested for something called Lipoprotein(a), also called Lp(a) or "little a," which is a risk factor you may have never heard of. But many cardiologists and primary care doctors are strongly resisting doing this. Cheryl Clark is here to tell us more.

Cheryl Clark: Thanks, Rachael. Yeah, "little a" – this is a component in blood that is believed to be strongly associated with one's risk of getting an earlier cardiovascular event or aortic stenosis. A growing number of studies seem to show that. But it's genetically inherited, so the score you get basically stays the same throughout your whole life. Diet, exercise, lowering low-density lipoprotein (LDL), body mass index (BMI), they don't change it. So for a long time, doctors said there was really nothing they could do about it. So why measure it?

Scores, it turns out, that are higher than 125 nanomoles per liter might just scare people, especially those without established disease or symptoms. And some doctors are reporting otherwise healthy patients, some even in their 70s, with scores in the high 300s or even higher. So it seems like a high score is survivable, but it is scary.

Now, European cardiology associations in 2022 and again in 2025 began to rethink this. The idea was that if you knew your score, you could change other elements in your life that raise your risk, like trying harder to lose weight. I mean, like really try harder to lower LDL, blood pressure, improve your diet, exercise, do all of that stuff.

The American groups, including the ACC and the American Heart Association, in March joined in. They issued similar recommendations in a 134-page document, basically agreeing with the Europeans. So now this is a standard recommendation that everybody, all adults, should get this at least once in their lifetime.

It might be that their thinking was rooted in the fact that now pharmaceutical companies have been developing drugs that lower this "little a," but they needed clinical trials to prove it. They also needed to prove that it would prevent heart disease and strokes, which of course is the point. Why would we do this otherwise? There are at least three drugs in development, so now lots of smart people are pretty darn sure they'd be successful with this drug and save lots of lives. So a few weeks ago, the first drug trial for a drug called pelacarsen was released, but the results were shockingly disappointing.

Clark: Well, it was called the HORIZON study. It was a large placebo-controlled trial, and it found no reduced risk in these cardiovascular events. It tested pelacarsen, which did lower "little a," but participants were still getting diagnosed and dying. So there is something else clearly in play that's causing heart disease. Maybe Lp(a) is a marker for something else, or maybe something else is protecting some people. Who knows?

So I asked a lot of doctors, were they ordering these tests? Especially now that we have the HORIZON results, and several said they were. Some said they were just ordering these tests in people with established familial linkages, like genetic predisposition. But many said they vehemently opposed such a universal recommendation or aren't ordering it at all. My own doctor orders it, but said other doctors in her office practice are not.

One cardiologist said otherwise healthy patients are seeing their high scores online in their portals and are freaking out, crying, and thinking they're going to die tomorrow, and they have to spend time reassuring them. One physician told me the committee should have prioritized calcium CT scoring instead of "little a." He thinks CT scoring shows real disease that can be treated. And Rita Redberg, MD, MSc, a prominent cardiologist at UCSF – she's known for less is more approach – called "little a" a useless test.

And by the way, the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services has not joined the bandwagon on testing for "little a." It allows regional Medicare jurisdictions across the country to make case by case determinations about whether to cover the tests. So some beneficiaries are going to get stuck with paying for this out-of-pocket.

Robertson: It sounds like there's still a lot of confusion.

Clark: Yes, the bottom line seems to be: live a healthy life. If you get a high score, be thankful and adhere to strategies that we know reduce your LDL and your other risk factors. We'll see in a few years if any of these other three drugs now in clinical trials clarify this complicated issue.

Robertson: Well, thank you so much for your reporting here, Cheryl.

Robertson: Pediatric obesity took center stage at ObesityWeek in National Harbor, Maryland. There were two major phase III trials showing strong weight loss results for GLP-1 medications in kids and teens. This was just days after the FDA added a new vision-related safety warning across the entire drug class. Kristen Monaco is here to break down the latest developments.

So, Kristen, starting with the adolescent data from ObesityWeek, what did the SURMOUNT-ADOLESCENTS trial show for tirzepatide?

Kristen Monaco: SURMOUNT-ADOLESCENTS was a phase III trial simultaneously published in JAMA that looked at the very popular dual GLP-1/GIP receptor agonist tirzepatide, also known as Zepbound, in 160 adolescents ages 12 to 17 with overweight or obesity. All participants also received lifestyle intervention. At week 72, teens who were taking tirzepatide saw an average BMI reduction of 18.5%, compared with a 0.5% increase in those taking placebo. Nearly half on tirzepatide achieved at least a 20% reduction in BMI, and over half were no longer considered to have obesity by the end of the trial.

Notably, researchers also saw improvements in quality of life and physical comfort scores, with no signals of disruption to growth or pubertal maturation, which is a concern of using these agents in this age group. Tirzepatide is currently only approved for adults with obesity or kids 10 and older with type 2 diabetes. So these data lay the groundwork for potential obesity indication in teens. However, rival GLP drug semaglutide is already approved for teens with obesity.

Robertson: Those are really impressive results. Researchers also presented data looking at even younger children, those ages six to 11. What did the STEP Young trial find for semaglutide?

Monaco: STEP Young, which was published in the New England Journal of Medicine, looked at semaglutide, also known as Wegovy, in 167 children, ages 6 to 11, with obesity alongside lifestyle counseling. Right now, semaglutide is only approved for obesity in patients ages 12 and up. There are no GLP-1 therapies right now that are FDA approved for this very young age group. So treatment options are largely limited to lifestyle modifications.

In the trial at week 68, semaglutide reduced BMI by 13.5%, and nearly 40% of children on the injectable no longer had obesity by the end of the trial. Children on treatment also saw improvements in cardiometabolic markers like waist circumference, triglycerides, and blood pressure. Gastrointestinal side effects like vomiting, diarrhea, and nausea were more common, especially during the dose escalation phase, which is expected with GLP-1 drugs. Though they were mostly mild to moderate and transient, with no safety signals affecting growth or pubertal development. Drug maker Novo Nordisk hasn't announced yet whether it plans to submit these data to the FDA, so that will be something to look closely for.

Robertson: That's super interesting. So stepping back from your conference coverage, you also reported on a major regulatory update involving GLP-1 drugs and eye health. What is this FDA warning, and what led to it?

Monaco: Yes, so the FDA is now requiring manufacturers of all currently marketed GLP-1 medications to include a warning about the potential risk of non-arteritic anterior ischemic optic neuropathy, also known as NAION, which is a rare but potentially blinding eye condition caused by reduced blood flow to the optic disc. This update follows a wave of observational studies, including a 2024 JAMA Ophthalmology study on semaglutide, that prompted this FDA review. The agency concluded from its review that there is indeed a rare but potentially increased risk of NAION events in patients taking GLP-1 drugs. Cases have been reported for all currently marketed GLP-1 products.

So the FDA told healthcare providers to tell their patients to report any sudden, painless vision loss or visual field changes in one eye immediately, and then to refer them promptly to an ophthalmologist. They should also consider discontinuing the GLP-1 drug unless the treatment benefits clearly outweigh the risks.

Robertson: Well, thank you so much for this important context as these medications continue to expand into younger populations. Thanks for being here, Kristen.

Robertson: The U.S. has officially lost its measles elimination status after 26 years. It's no surprise, considering there have been at least 830 hospitalizations and 13 measles-associated deaths in the past 2 years.

Losing this status is a big deal. Our editor-in-chief, Jeremy Faust, MD, sat down with pediatric infectious disease expert Paul Offit, MD, of Children's Hospital of Philadelphia, and public health researcher Noel Brewer, PhD, of the University of North Carolina in Chapel Hill. They talked about the resurgence of measles outbreaks, declining vaccination rates, timing of the measles, mumps, and rubella vaccine, the influence of vaccine misinformation, and the challenges facing public health officials as they work to restore confidence in vaccination and prevent further transmission. It's a lot, but I really recommend you listen to the full conversation. We've linked it here.

Last week, there were 334 cases of measles, and there were 319 the week before that. Pennsylvania is still really struggling with more than 100 cases in the last 2 weeks, but things are also really heating up in Wisconsin, which had more than 200 cases in that same time period. The other states that we're keeping an extra close eye on are New York, Indiana, and Minnesota. With holiday travel coming up, we'll see in the coming weeks how that influences the spread of measles.

As always, check out our interactive measles map, which is updated at the top of each week, and we've linked it right here.

This episode was hosted and produced by me, Rachael Robertson. Sound engineering by Greg Laub. Theme music by Palomar. Our guests were MedPage Today reporters Cheryl Clark and Kristen Monaco. Links to their stories are in the show notes.

#5811. Leapmotor Produces Its 2 Millionth EV

Leapmotor just produced its 2 millionth vehicle, all EVs of course. The EV startup has seen a meteoric rise in the highly competitive Chinese EV market, and increasingly abroad. Of course, most of us in the US and even in Europe know nothing or next to nothing about Leapmotor, but it has clearly been more effective than hundreds or even thousands of other companies that have tried to launch and scale up EV production.

One interesting thing to note regarding this milestone: it’s peanuts compared to Leapmotor’s plans for the next few years. In 2026 alone, Leapmotor plans to produce one million vehicles, and it’s on track to do so. That means that, this year, the company plans to produce as many vehicles as half of Leapmotor’s cumulative total at the moment. So, it will just be sometime in the middle of next year that the company will reach 3 million cumulative EVs produced.

The company is now producing more than 100,000 vehicles a month — for four months running.

Naturally, the company’s plan is to keep growing. As part of that effort, it just launched a new model, the Lafa 5 compact hatchback, with pre-sales starting earlier today. Also, another new model, the D19 SUV, just had its 50,000th unit produced.

So, yes, keep an eye on Leapmotor. It’s already a wildly successful young EV company, and it looks like it is going to continue growing fast.

#5809. AGCO Celebrates Construction Milestone at Olmedo Solar Project in Spain

OLMEDO, Spain, Nov. 19, 2026 — AGCO (NYSE: AGCO) today announced that Project Olmedo, the solar installation at the center of its first Virtual Power Purchase Agreement (VPPA), has completed construction and commissioning and is expected to begin delivering renewable electricity to the Spanish power grid in the coming months, pending final regulatory approvals. BRUC, one of the largest renewable energy groups in Spain, built the new solar installation in the northwest province of Valladolid.

The milestone follows AGCO’s September 2025 announcement of its 10-year VPPA with BRUC. The installation’s two solar projects, Los Silos and El Granero, span approximately 300 hectares (741 acres) and hold approximately 150,000 solar panels. AGCO’s portion of the installation’s annual production is expected to be approximately 150 gigawatt-hours (GWh) of renewable electricity a year, equivalent to the annual electricity consumption of approximately 40,000 European households, based on Eurostat data.

“Project Olmedo will give AGCO long-term price certainty for the majority of the electricity certificate cost in Europe,” said Tim Millwood, Senior Vice President, Chief Supply Chain Officer. “In volatile energy markets, securing that stability for 10 years strengthens the resilience of our supply chain while supporting new renewable generation.”

A VPPA lets a company support new renewable energy projects without taking direct delivery of the electricity they generate. Because AGCO’s agreement supported the construction of a brand-new installation, it will add new renewable capacity to the grid rather than drawing on existing sources, a principle known as additionality. The agreement also helps reduce AGCO’s Scope 2 greenhouse gas emissions from purchased electricity in its Europe/Middle East (EME) region and supports its commitment to reduce absolute Scope 1 and 2 emissions by 55% by 2033 from a 2022 baseline.

“Additionality is what makes this agreement truly meaningful,” said Roger Batkin, Senior Vice President, Corporate Affairs, General Counsel & Corporate Secretary. “We did not simply buy renewable electricity that already existed; we supported the construction of a brand-new renewable solar project. Accomplishing this project required over two years of involvement from many teams, functions, and areas of expertise across AGCO, along with technical advisory support from Schneider Electric and a strong partnership with BRUC.”

Reflecting AGCO’s Farmer-First strategy, Project Olmedo supports local farmers to further diversify their land use by leasing land for the project while maintaining the option for livestock to graze among the panels, maintaining the vegetation in place of machinery.

AGCO and BRUC leaders, joined by local community representatives José Emilio Escudero García, Mayor of Bocigas, and Ursino Vara Pérez, Mayor of Almenara de Adaja, marked the milestone with a ribbon-cutting ceremony at the site in November. Additional drone footage from Project Olmedo and the ribbon-cutting event are available on AGCO’s YouTube page.

For more details on AGCO’s sustainability initiatives, visit the company’s sustainability webpage and read the 2025 Sustainability Report. For more information about BRUC, visit the company’s website at brucenergy.com.

AGCO (NYSE: AGCO) is a global leader in agricultural machinery and precision agriculture technologies. Driven by a Farmer-First strategy, AGCO delivers value through its differentiated leading brands, Fendt™, Massey Ferguson™, PTx™ and Valtra™.  AGCO’s high-performance equipment and smart farming solutions, including brand-agnostic retrofit technologies and autonomous offerings, empower farmers to drive productivity while sustainably feeding the world. For more information, visit www.agcocorp.com.

#5808. Alzheimer's Light and Sound Stimulation Therapy Misses Main Goal in Pivotal Trial

— But gamma wave approach hints at slower decline in mild Alzheimer's disease

A novel Alzheimer's therapy platform that delivered 40 Hz light and sound stimulation to engage gamma-frequency brain activity did not meet its primary endpoint, the HOPE pivotal study showed.

In the primary analysis population of 663 people with mild-to-moderate Alzheimer's, the change from baseline on a composite measure of Mini-Mental State Examination (MMSE) and Alzheimer's Disease Cooperative Study-Activities of Daily Living (ADCS-ADL) scores did not differ between those who used the Spectris stimulation goggles or sham at 12 months, reported Marwan Sabbagh, MD, of the Barrow Neurological Institute in Phoenix.

Secondary endpoints in the overall study population also showed no significant differences. However, a prespecified subgroup analysis of 395 people with mild Alzheimer's disease (MMSE scores of 21-28 on the 30-point scale) showed favorable results for the device on some measures, Sabbagh said at the Clinical Trials on Alzheimer's Disease conference in Boston.

In the mild subgroup, the device was associated with a 55.8% slowing of decline in ADCS-ADL scores (nominal P=0.016) and a 46.8% slowing of decline in Clinical Dementia Rating-Sum of Boxes (CDR-SB) scores (nominal P=0.004), Sabbagh noted.

Results in the moderate subgroup (MMSE scores of 15-20) did not favor the device, and some outcomes nominally favored sham.

The difference in outcomes between mild and moderate disease suggests that earlier intervention may be key to clinical benefit, observed Laura Nisenbaum, PhD, interim chief science officer at the Alzheimer's Drug Discovery Foundation in New York City, which provided funding for the trial.

"While the HOPE study did not meet its primary endpoint, the signal in the mild Alzheimer's subgroup raises important questions that are worth exploring further," Nisenbaum said. "Daily function is what patients and families tell us matters most, so it will be important to see whether the early signs of slower decline on that measure in people with mild Alzheimer's hold up as more data become available."

The device delivers audiovisual stimulation intended to evoke steady-state gamma oscillations across broad cortical regions, Sabbagh explained. "Spectris was developed as a non-invasive, home-use investigational medical device that delivers daily 1-hour treatment sessions," he said.

Earlier research showed that sensory-evoked 40 Hz neural activity engages brain mechanisms essential for cognition and daily function, he added.

The HOPE trial evaluated the device in participants who had baseline MMSE scores of 15-28 and Alzheimer's biomarker confirmation. Participants were randomized within stratified mild and moderate disease subgroups.

The primary outcome was the integrated Alzheimer's Disease MMSE and ADCS-ADL Rating Scale, an equally weighted composite of global cognition (MMSE) and daily function (ADCS-ADL) scores, Sabbagh said.

In the overall study population, adverse device effects were reported in 18.3% of participants who received active treatment and 15.5% who received sham. The most common were headache, tinnitus, fatigue, dizziness, and ear pain. Seizures occurred in 0.9% of the active treatment group.

The gamma wave approach to treating Alzheimer's disease was first discovered in 2014 at the Picower Institute for Learning and Memory at the Massachusetts Institute of Technology (MIT). Executives at Cognito Therapeutics, an MIT spin-off, said they will pursue FDA approval for the 40 Hz light and sound stimulation device based on the nominally significant benefits seen in the mild Alzheimer's population.

An open-label extension of the HOPE trial is ongoing.

#5807. 1968 Chevy Camaro SS For Sale With 6,300 Miles Shown After New Odo Packs 350 V8 And 4-Speed Stick

As more people recognize the value of owning older cars that are easier and more affordable to repair, options like this thoughtfully updated '68 Camaro SS get more appealing on a regular basis. Even if car repair costs are rising at an alarming rate, it's still worth looking at simple old cars. This particular 1968 Chevrolet Camaro SS is currently listed for auction on Cars and Bids, and it is powered by a 350 small block (possibly a 383 stroker engine — the seller isn't quite sure), with shifting done via a four-speed manual transmission. It has an Odometer Brand Discrepancy on its Massachusetts title, though, with unknown true mileage, which could end up making it an affordable, daily-drivable example of this classic muscle car.

It has smart upgrades like electronic fuel injection, an electronic distributor, four-wheel power disc brakes, power steering, and traction bars that should all serve to make it a friendlier car to drive on a daily basis, and an aftermarket head unit and speakers to make it friendlier to live with. It's got too much character to be winning any concours any time soon, so whoever ends up winning the auction might as well enjoy driving it.

Okay, so the odometer currently displays 6,300 miles, but it was replaced at some point, so that only indicates mileage put on after the odometer was replaced. That said, the ad says the current seller purchased it in 2025 and has added about 1,200 miles since then, which bodes well for its drivability. This is not a perfect car, with flaws including a sagging headliner, an inoperable parking brake, missing rear window cranks, holes in the glove compartment, and a disconnected fuel gauge, but it would make a great project for a dedicated gearhead.

From the photos, its exterior looks serviceable, with its share of little imperfections, but overall it looks pretty clean for a 58-year-old car. There's a bit of rust on the Cragar wheels, but nothing too serious. Unfortunately, it does have aftermarket LED headlights, which I'm not the biggest fan of, but it doesn't look as bad as it could. In the video included in the ad, the Camaro starts right up and idles smoothly without any puffs of smoke or any other immediately concerning symptoms — another good sign. I think I'd be willing to overlook its flaws if the price is right. What about you?
