#5800. Novel Oral HAE Drug Measures Up to Injectable Options

— Randomized trial hits the mark for reducing hereditary angioedema attack rates

Prophylactic use of the novel oral bradykinin B2 receptor antagonist deucrictibant substantially reduced hereditary angioedema (HAE) attacks, a pivotal phase III trial showed.

For the primary endpoint, extended-release deucrictibant lowered the attack rate by a relative 83%, to 0.35 events per month compared with 2.06 in the placebo group (P<0.0001), reported Marc A. Riedl, MD, of the University of California San Diego, at the American College of Allergy, Asthma & Immunology annual meeting.

During the 24-week treatment period in the CHAPTER-3 trial, 45% of study drug-treated patients were attack free, compared with 3% of the placebo group (P=0.0018).

"We don't have head-to-head studies, but it is in the same ballpark of what the subcutaneous long-term prophylactic drugs have shown," Riedl told MedPage Today. "This is what we were hoping to see as investigators -- this high level of efficacy in a once-daily pill."

That's meaningful given what's been seen with the only approved targeted oral HAE prophylactic medication, berotralstat (Orladeyo), Riedl noted.

"The overall efficacy, at least in the pivotal trial for berotralstat, didn't measure up quite as well compared to some of the subcutaneous treatment options for long-term prophylaxis," he said. "So this is an effort to try to look at another oral agent that might be more effective across the population, just given that we know some patients really prefer oral medication for convenience and less burden of treatment and so forth."

Of the 85 patients in the trial, five had genetically confirmed HAE with normal C1 inhibitor levels. While two of three of these patients on deucrictibant had good results with the drug -- one had a greater than 80% reduction and the other had 100% reduction in HAE attacks -- the third patient had only a very small reduction in attack rate.

A post-hoc analysis looking at only patients with deficiency or dysfunction of C1 inhibitor levels showed an 87% reduction in attack rate with deucrictibant, a rate that Riedl said "reflects the type 1 and type 2 HAE population that every other study has reported when they report out their efficacy data for prophylaxis."

The international trial enrolled patients ages 12 years and older (mean age 39) who had at least three attacks within 3 months before screening and at least two attacks during the up to 8-week run-in period. Participants were randomly assigned 2:1 to 24 weeks of double-blind treatment with extended-release deucrictibant (40 mg once a day) or placebo.

Baseline HAE attack rate was 3.8 per month in the study drug group and 3.5 per month in the placebo group. Attack rates dropped within the first week with deucrictibant and remained lower than in the placebo group through week 24.

The relative reductions in events that required rescue medication and in moderate-to-severe HAE attacks were 83% and 77%, respectively (both P<0.0001). Nearly all patients on deucrictibant had their attack rate reduced by at least 70% (96% vs 23% on placebo, P<0.0001). The drug increased the proportion of patients who had at least a 90% reduction in attack frequency as well (65% vs 10%, P<0.0001). Deucrictibant also resulted in significantly greater improvements in mean Angioedema Quality of Life questionnaire total score and all domain scores as compared with placebo.

Riedl called the safety profile favorable overall. One treatment-emergent serious adverse event occurred in the deucrictibant group, a case of laryngeal dyskinesia that resulted in a stay in the intensive care unit for evaluation but was deemed not related to the study drug.

The sole discontinuation in the active treatment group was due to asymptomatic elevation in liver function tests. Riedl noted that this is in line with what has been seen in most of the long-term prophylactic studies with HAE medications. "These patients do sometimes have a history of fatty liver. They've been on androgens, which we know can affect their liver function," he said.

Gastrointestinal side effects seen with berotralstat were not observed with deucrictibant (1.8% vs 6.7% on placebo).

An immediate-release version of deucrictibant is also being developed for on-demand treatment of acute HAE attacks and is under review by the FDA for that indication. Riedl suggested that if the extended-release formulation were to be approved as an additional prophylactic treatment option, it could be appealing for patients who prefer oral medications over injections.

"This would potentially be an oral treatment that gives very similar efficacy in terms of preventing attacks compared to what we've seen with the subcutaneous drugs, and we don't actually have that at the moment," he said.

He pointed to the increasing options for HAE management as good for patients. "Trying to make that preventative treatment as easy, as least burdensome as we can is part of the goal," Riedl noted. "And I think this is one way to potentially do that for people -- just a once-daily medication that's portable, easy to travel with, and makes their lives easier."

#5799. HLTH 2026: American Hospital Association, West Health Institute announce new collaboration with BD

HLTH 2026: American Hospital Association, West Health Institute announce new collaboration with BD" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The American Hospital Association (AHA) and West Health Institute announced Tuesday a new collaboration with medtech company Becton, Dickinson and Company (BD) aimed at accelerating the adoption of technologies shown to improve patient care.

The collaboration builds on the West Health Accelerator at AHA’s Health Research & Educational Trust (HRET), a three-year initiative to aid healthcare organizations in scaling technology-enabled solutions.

“No single organization can close the gap between innovation and everyday care on its own,” said Zia Agha, M.D., West Health Institute chief medical officer, in a statement. “This collaboration brings hospital reach, clinical technology expertise and performance intelligence together from the start. We’re beginning with a focused group of hospitals, learning what it takes to make changes last, and building a model that can reach hospitals of every size and resource level.”

The new collaboration will begin with an initial cohort of 10 hospitals and health systems. It will incorporate advanced physiologic monitoring with clinical protocols in practice, specifically targeting issues associated with postoperative complications and longer hospital stays. Additionally, Vizient will provide performance benchmarking and analytic capabilities, the organizations said.

Takeaways from the early cohort will help to identify adoption barriers, refine implementation approaches and outline necessary support to expand the model to a broader group.

BD Advanced Patient Monitoring Worldwide President Tim Patz said in a statement that the company believes innovation “has the greatest impact when it translates into meaningful improvements in patient care.”

“Our experience working with healthcare systems has shown us the value of bringing together advanced hemodynamic monitoring, data and the expertise of hospital care teams to support better-informed patient care,” Patz said. “This collaboration gives us an opportunity to build on that experience, further integrate proven solutions into clinical practice and help more patients benefit from them.”

#5795. Payer mix, non-labor expenses dragged hospital margins in September

Payer mix, non-labor expenses dragged hospital margins in September" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Hospitals’ operations took another dip in September amid a slew of ongoing headwinds including weakened payer mix and rising costs, healthcare advisory firm Kaufman Hall reported alongside warnings of a “historically large portion” of the nation’s hospitals running on negative or near-zero operating margins.

The firm’s latest monthly benchmark report, released Tuesday, showed a calendar year-to-date operating margin index of 1.4% and a single-month operating margin index of 2.4% in September, the latter of which was a decline from August’s 3.0% operating margin (all including health system allocations for shared services costs).

The performance decline wasn’t for want of patients. Daily discharges and adjusted discharges both rose 3% from August to September, and are generally above the numbers from last year. Operating room minutes per calendar day also rose 6% from August—an encouraging sign for hospitals whose surgical slowdowns had hamstrung acuity—but remain 1% below September of last year.

The increased foot traffic also grew revenues, with daily net operating revenue rising 4% from August and 6% year over year while gross operating revenue rose 4% month to month and 8% year over year. Much of the increase came on the outpatient side, where daily revenue increased 5% from August and 10% compared to September 2025. Net patient service revenue per adjusted discharge was more mixed, rising 2% from August but remaining 1% below September 2025.

However, hospitals’ margins are being challenged by persistent bad debt and charity care burdens, Kaufman Hall’s data show. Daily bad debt and charity care may have remained flat from August, but is 17% higher than September 2025 and 16% higher when comparing both years’ first nine months. As a percentage of gross operating revenue, they’re 6% higher than Sept. 2025 and 8% higher year to date.

On the spending side, daily total expense rose 3% from August and is 5% higher than September 2025, primarily reflecting the heightened volumes even as hospitals held their average length of stay flat month over month or 1% shorter year over year. The expenses were more pronounced on the non-labor side, where increases on a per adjusted discharge basis were higher than labor’s per adjusted discharge decline.

"Hospitals continue to operate in an environment marked by significant financial variability," Erik Swanson, managing director and Data and Analytics group leader at Kaufman Hall, said in a release. “Many organizations continue to face mounting pressure from uncompensated care and rising expenses, underscoring the importance of proactive operational and financial strategies that build long-term resilience."

Another amplifying market trend is the “bifurcation of hospital performance” often highlighted by the firm and others that monitor multi-organization financial trends, such as credit rating agencies.

"The margin gap between higher and lower-performing hospitals has widened to 19 percentage points, with a historically large portion of hospitals operating at negative or very thin margins,” the report reads. “Even slight revenue reductions threaten organizations, which face increasingly difficult decisions on future operations, care delivery, and expense control.”

The firm encouraged hospitals to take “deliberate, proactive steps” to strengthen their financial situations, particularly as macro reimbursement trends stiffen and the site of care delivery shifts from the hospital to outpatient settings.

Kaufman Hall’s hospital reports pull data from more than 1,300 nationwide hospitals as collected by parent company Strata Decision Technology.

Accompanying the monthly hospital benchmark was also Kaufman Hall’s quarterly numbers on more than 200,000 employed physicians and advanced practice providers, which painted a much rosier picture for health system and hospital employers.

It showed, for the first time in five years of data collection, that healthcare employers’ investment/subsidy per provider and per physician has declined year over year (by 1%), and “across every cohort, including primary care, medical specialty, surgical specialty and hospital-based specialty.”

Kaufman Hall attributed the rare turnaround to revenue growth and sustained productivity increases that together outpaced expenses.

"Medical groups made meaningful performance improvements this quarter, achieving gains in revenue, productivity and thoughtful workforce management," Matthew Bates, managing director and Physician Enterprise Service Line leader at Kaufman Hall, said in a release. "The growing utilization of [advanced practice providers] and ongoing focus on operational and team effectiveness are helping organizations manage financial pressures while increasing access to care.”

#5794. The Metals Company Has a Seabed Mining Production Gap That Its Pilot Has Not Closed

In 2022, The Metals Company (TMC) and offshore contractor Allseas recovered 3,021 wet tons of nodules from the Pacific seabed. Their integrated test system moved material up a riser stretching more than four kilometres from the ocean floor to the Hidden Gem. During production runs, the collector achieved 86.4 wet tons per hour. Four years later, TMC is discussing a million tons a year from an upgraded system and eventually 12 million tons annually across four vessels.

What matters between those endpoints is the operating record: sustained collection rates, marine maintenance, vessel availability and delivered tons sufficient to carry fixed costs. TMC has yet to demonstrate those economics.

The November 12, 2026 corporate update describes a phased approach: begin with an upgraded pilot collector and limited modifications to Hidden Gem, then invest in larger systems as operations develop. Phasing reduces the initial capital commitment. It also pushes the demonstration of the projected commercial production rates into the future.

The annual arithmetic is straightforward. At 5,000 productive hours, about 57% of a calendar year, a system collecting 200 wet tons per hour delivers one million tons. The second-year target of 1.5 million tons requires 300 tons per hour. Three million tons from one production system requires 600 tons per hour. TMC’s 2022 production-run rate of 86.4 tons per hour would deliver 432,000 tons at that utilization.

The upgraded machines are expected to be different from the 2022 test collector. Their performance must be demonstrated, not assumed from the pilot. The operating gap is central to the project economics, and it grows with production ambitions.

The full TFIE Strategy Briefing examines TMC’s production gap alongside Impossible Metals’ autonomous collection model and the investment consequences of underperforming equipment. The source assessment and fleet sensitivities are linked there.

A collector, a multi-kilometre hydraulic riser and a production vessel have to work together. The crawler encounters uneven ground and varying nodule coverage. The riser and pumps handle abrasive slurry. The vessel must remain on station while crews inspect, maintain and repair equipment. Offshore access and weather affect when those activities can occur.

A system can produce at its test rate and still lose the year on maintenance and interruptions. Fewer productive hours push the required hourly rate higher. Repeating a successful short production run does not establish a year’s availability, let alone multiple years of fleet productivity.

The difference matters because capital and offshore operating costs continue while production is interrupted. Ships, crews, marine maintenance equipment and financing cost money whether or not nodules are arriving on deck. Smaller annual production spreads fixed expenses over fewer tons and raises the cost of every ton recovered.

TMC’s August 2025 prefeasibility presentation estimated offshore collection and transport at $179 per dry ton, or about $129 per wet ton under its 28% moisture assumption. Those are projections for a future system. They are not an audited cost record from continuous industrial mining. The revised first-production configuration needs its own cost data.

The missing commercial evidence is a long enough operating record to estimate productive hours, rate degradation, planned and unplanned maintenance, retrieval time, replacement costs and the delivered cost per ton. Until then, moving from a pilot to a production forecast remains a major assumption.

Impossible Metals proposes to collect individual nodules with autonomous underwater vehicles rather than a tracked crawler and riser. A vehicle would descend, select nodules, fill its cargo compartment, return, undergo surface recovery, unload and prepare for another mission. Every trip must be repeated across an entire fleet.

Its September 2026 scientific trial, reported by Reuters, recovered approximately 450 grams of nodules from more than 4,000 metres depth. It demonstrated selective scientific collection. A commercial robot would have to carry vastly more material and sustain cycle times and availability that the experiment did not measure.

In the 2025 commercial concept examined by metallurgical specialist Lyle Trytten and me, each proposed vehicle carried 12 tons of nodules and had 18 collection arms. At one nodule every 1.5 seconds per arm and an average nodule mass of 100 grams, filling the cargo requires roughly 120,000 successful picks. With all arms continuously working, collection alone occupies approximately 2.8 hours.

The proposed complete mission cycle was four hours. Descent, ascent, searching, repositioning, failed picks, recovery, unloading, inspection and relaunch also consume time. The architecture has too many interdependent activities to fit those requirements into that cycle at the assumed performance.

Autonomous navigation and manipulator tests cannot substitute for evidence of an entire fleet repeatedly executing commercial missions. Increasing payload changes vehicle weight, buoyancy, energy use and recovery equipment. Faster picking leaves the surface operation and deepwater transit to be solved.

Our 2025 independent techno-economic assessment tested the financial effects of weaker-than-assumed robot productivity. At the illustrative $180 per dry ton nodule sales value, the model required approximately $2.52 billion in capital with the originally assumed robot throughput. Halving throughput while maintaining output increased required capital to approximately $4.51 billion.

At a 10% discount rate, the modeled net present value moved from positive $863 million to negative $769 million. Even at two-thirds of original productivity, NPV fell to about $15 million, a negligible margin for a multibillion-dollar first-of-a-kind industrial fleet.

These are scenario calculations, not actual bills or calibrated probabilities. They identify the key failure mode: more robots, recovery capacity, batteries, vessels and maintenance are needed to compensate for each production shortfall. A financial model based on high productivity can look attractive until realistic availability and turnaround times are included.

TMC is further along technologically than Impossible Metals, but its future-scale production economics are also unsupported by a sustained operating record. Neither company has demonstrated the productive hours, throughput and maintenance costs needed to support its advertised commercial proposition.

Policymakers and investors should ask for extended end-to-end offshore operating records and audited unit economics before treating future production targets as investable output. The technology risk exists before accounting for nodule processing, metals markets, permitting or environmental liabilities.

The complete TFIE Briefing and linked assessment show the fleet calculations, technical assumptions and capital sensitivities. The remaining articles in the five-part series address processing, metals markets and American Samoa’s exposure.

#5793. You Can Get A Free Classic Car And Garage In GTA 6, But Only Until Tomorrow

It's finally here. Can you believe it, fans of video game carnage? The internet will have to find some other source of amusement other than the "we got so-and-so before we got 'GTA VI,'" because guess what? Thirteen years after the release of the previous iteration, Rockstar Games has finally made the sixth installment of the wildly popular "Grand Theft Auto" franchise available to the masses. Well, those members of the masses with access to a PlayStation 5, a PS5 Pro, an Xbox Series X, or an Xbox Series S, that is. If that describes you, Rockstar might just have a surprise waiting for your first visit to Vice City in "GTA 6."

Of course, with a name like Grand Theft Auto, the automotive element of the action-packed title hasn't taken a back seat. We've been spotting the new cars in "GTA 6" since the extended trailer dropped, and connecting them to their real-life counterparts. For a very specific group of early adopters, logging onto the game will yield more than just the excitement of playing the long-awaited title. Rockstar is giving some players a free classic car and a garage to park it in.

Like it or not, you've probably encountered the "GTA 6" hype in some manner or another. That is, unless you've been living under a rock. On a deserted island. An island on another planet. The twice-delayed game is a big deal, and it's been available for pre-order since June 2026. Rockstar Games is giving out the Vintage Vice City Pack to those players who pre-ordered "GTA 6," and it comes with a handful of goodies. Most notably, the pack includes the '55 Vapid Stanier, a two-tone yellow-on-white classic two-door hardtop.

You might take one look at the Vapid Stanier and think, "huh, that looks awfully familiar." There's a reason for that. Not just any Super 88, but the very specific landmark car parked in front of the Avalon Hotel on Ocean Drive in Miami. And why not? You'll likely want a garage to safely store your Stanier, too. If the new game is anything like "GTA Online," things will likely get quite explode-y.

So, are you afraid you missed your chance? Fret not. If you place your order for "GTA 6" today, you can still get your hands on the Vintage Vice City Pack. For now, the offer expires at the end of today. It also doesn't matter if your physical copy of "GTA 6" is in the mail; as long as you place your order before the end of the day, you can get the expansion pack.

Now, it might seem outlandish and unrealistic to expect a free classic car and a complimentary garage anywhere other than the fictional state of Leonida. But Rockstar seems to be trying to dial up the realism factor in its latest blockbuster game. Don't believe it? Unlike the campaign or online free-for-all of the fifth "Grand Theft Auto" title, players will have to fill up cars with gasoline virtually in "GTA 6" to keep driving. That's right. No infinitely full gas tanks anymore. We'll just have to see if the Vapid Stanier is a dainty fuel sipper or a guzzler.

If that wasn't enough, Rockstar also wants players to experience more life-like collisions. How? Well, the developers made sure "GTA 6" leaned into advanced physics for more realistic car crashes. So, you know, try not to hit stuff in your classic car.

#5792. HLTH 2026 Daily Tracker: F1 mania takes over HLTH; Why DJ Steve Aoki is investing in innovative science

HLTH 2026 Daily Tracker: F1 mania takes over HLTH; Why DJ Steve Aoki is investing in innovative science" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The Fierce Healthcare team is on the ground at HLTH 2026. Stick with this news tracker to catch all the highlights from the show floor, and click here for additional coverage from the conference.

DJ and music producer Steve Aoki told the HLTH audience that his father's passing was the catalyst for his interest in healthcare.

Learning more about what caused his death, and if it could have been prevented, drew him to the impacts of dementia and cancer. And in exploring that, he said he decided he wanted to invest in the people who are pursuing the "science fiction concepts" in healthcare, and support cutting edge innovation.

Aoki said that his celebrity status made it easier to get into a room with people leading the charge on innovation, and learn as much as he could.

"I was just as intrigued as working with a big artist in the studio or playing a big show," he said.

It was with that learning that he established Aoki Foundation, an organization he said is dedicated to bringing back the science and investing in what's next.

"People are excited about science fiction, about the possibility of doing things that are impossible," Aoki said. "And I feel like the superheroes of our time are like right right in our midst, and it's about it's about highlighting their journey and giving them a spotlight."

It’s been several weeks since Doximity integrated Wolters Kluwer Health’s UpToDate into its AI clinical decision support tool.

Doximity VP of Clinical AI Partnerships Kamal Menghrajani, M.D., told Fierce Healthcare at HLTH said the integration gives her “a little bit more confidence” in her own practice.

“Now I can use UpToDate to supplement and feel like what I'm doing at the point of care really is the best thing for the patient,” she said.

Menghrajani, who previously served as assistant director for cancer innovation and public health at the White House, took on the role with Doximity approximately four months ago.

“[I get to] be the physician voice and represent the interests of doctors within the company,” she said. “And think about what it looks like to actually take the tools we're building and serve doctors better.”

Menghrajani said the rapid expansion of the company’s AI suite has been a key takeaway, adding that a focus for the company is what physicians want out of AI. “I've seen Doximity evolve to meet all of those needs,” Menghrajani said.

As a range of AI tools saturate the industry, Menghrajani said the biggest use she has seen amongst clinicians is ambient scribes. “I think that has been a real game changer in terms of actual practice,” she said.

On the HLTH show floor, you may have noticed a theme.

Las Vegas is abuzz with the upcoming Formula 1 race, which is taking place on Saturday, Nov. 21. The city has been preparing for the Las Vegas Grand Prix, which is on the Strip, for weeks, and HLTH is taking place at one of the hubs for F1 excitement, The Venetian.

So at this year's conference, each of the stages is themed around racing: the Velocity Stage, the Ferrari Stage, the Monacco Stage. And each breakout track is referred to as a "circuit." In the conference livery, HLTH mascot Petunia is show driving a race car.

Activations on the show floor also play into the F1 theme. A racing simulation game is set up for attendees near the Pharma Force stage, and swag giveaways allude to the race.

Panelists at a HLTH session discussing the uptake of ICHRA — scratch that, CHOICE — faced a challenge around the nomenclature.

Individual coverage health reimbursement arrangements, or ICHRAs, represent a growing area of interest for employers and insurers participating in the individual market. But on Sept. 3, the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services announced that it would instead refer to these models as CHOICE, which stands for Custom Health Option and Individual Care Expense.

In these models, an employer offers enrollees a stipend to purchase coverage on the individual market rather than securing a traditional employer-sponsored plan. ICHRA (or CHOICE) has gained traction with employers that may otherwise struggle to offer health coverage.

Moderator Joseph Mercer, senior managing director at Marwood Group, joked that the panelists had all agreed to call these plans CHOICE rather than ICHRA. But throughout the session, slip-ups drew a chuckle.

Even the panel's title made reference to the evolution of the name: "The ICHRA (CHOICE) Debate."

Healthcare platform Simple HealthKit announced Tuesday a partnership with Waters Corporation to expand access to at-home cervical cancer screening.

The collaboration aims to expand access to BD’s Onclarity HPV Self-Collection Kit, which is tested with the BD Onclarity HPV Assay  and Simple HealthKit's artificial intelligence-powered direct-to-patient-provider platform.

"We envision a future where personalized, world-class healthcare is accessible to everyone, regardless of where they live,” said Sheena Menezes, Ph.D, Simple HealthKit CEO and co-founder, in a statement. “Our AI-powered direct-to-patient and provider platform brings that vision to life by connecting people with the right education, intelligent personalized engagement, and clinical care at every step.”

Menezes continued, “Through partnerships with innovators like Waters Corporation, we are empowering individuals to take charge of their health while making seamless, personalized care accessible at scale.”

Oura CEO joined Counsel Health CEO and founder Muthu Alagappan Tuesday morning to discuss the wearable company’s recently announced $625 million acquisition of the virtual primary care company.

Hale said Oura selected Counsel as a partner after reviewing the landscape, and “uptake” from customers was “very positive.”

“We started talking seriously about taking that partnership,” Hale said. “And while we had been dating for a while, we thought maybe it was time to get married.”

Hale said the agreement was “really driven” by Oura customers using Counsel in “really novel and interesting ways.”

Alagappan said one of the most exciting aspects of the partnership is the potential impact of AI-fueled access to physicians on preventive care, adding the daily engagement of wearing Oura rings struck him.

“The buy-in from almost all users is tremendous, and so you enable primary care with the thing that has always been missing, which is engagement,” he said. “And now we have a way to incentivize, motivate patients to make the changes that actually prevent disease, alongside the screenings and lab tests that are also very important.”

During a Monday fireside chat, CMS Deputy Administrator and Chief Product Officer Amy Gleason said HHS and OCR colleagues “have done an amazing job” at looking at the issue to “remove friction from the patient experience.”

Gleason said there are three main components of the rule: the designation of a “personal health app,” identity verification and speed. “It should be almost instantaneous with electronic data, and it also says that this information is free to the patient,” she said.

Throughout the session with Zus Health CEO and co-founder Jonathan Bush, Gleason also touted the agency’s voluntary CMS Health Tech Ecosystem initiative.

“The idea of this was to stop waiting on regulation, like healthcare does for some reason, and to just charge forward and get the technology and people that are willing to go faster to work on it,” Gleason said. “And we set up a bunch of different technology working groups that are really about taking the friction out of the system.”

Evvy CEO and co-founder Priyanka Jain spoke with Fierce Healthcare Monday about research gaps in women’s health and how her company looks to bridge them through vaginal microbiome testing.

Jain said women were excluded from medical research from 1977 to 1993 after a drug being prescribed off-label for morning sickness was causing birth defects, adding the ramifications ar still being seen today.

“Women to this day are diagnosed on average four years later than men across over 700 diseases,” Jain said. “And it's hard to not point right back to the fact that we didn't study women until 1993.”

To bridge these gaps, Evvy offers an at-home vaginal health test with clinician-reviewed results and personalized treatment plans. “If we've essentially never studied women, then there must be so many biomarkers in the female body that we under leverage in our definition of health and disease, and so that's our focus,” Jain said.

Jain added Evvy uses AI and precision medicine to “actually predict the right treatment programs” for each patient using the biomarkers.

And, Jain said women’s health needs attention, awareness and funding when asked about recent Trump administration initiatives to focus on women’s health.

“I’m really excited to see the attention and awareness that has been happening,” Jain said. “But now we need to actually move dollars.”

Department of Health and Human Services Chief Counselor Chris Klomp said the administration is focused on bringing in top tech talent as it looks to make the massive, but aging, government insurance system work better.

Klomp spoke on a panel Monday morning at HLTH, where he discussed the challenges in harvesting the data necessary to transform Medicare, Medicaid and other key government programs. Klomp was recently nominated to serve as Deputy Secretary at HHS and is a rising star in the White House.

He said that technology can play a critical role in accelerate processes that have been historically inefficient and time consuming. At the Food and Drug Administration, for example, tech is hastening the process of developing drugs and making approvals, a timeline that previously taken a decade or more.

But embracing technology in this way isn't compromising safety for these medications, Klomp said.

In addition, he said the administration is leaning on the personal nature of healthcare to bring in workers with the tech know-how necessary to drive critical advancements and change.

"Most people don't care about healthcare unless you work in it," he said. "People also start to care about healthcare when you lose your health, or someone you love loses their health. And than it quickly becomes the most important thing to you."

"We're using that as an opportunity to drive requirement, drive amazing talent," Klomp said.

Houston Methodist announced Monday it has expanded its advanced inpatient monitoring care to include rural hospitals throughout Kansas.

Participating hospitals will provide patients with Medtronic’s BioButton, which continuously monitors vital signs, while the Houston Methodist remote monitoring team will review the data and work with bedside nurses.

The vital monitoring devices are used at all eight acute-care hospitals in the Houston Methodist system, across more than 2,500 inpatient beds, according to the Nov. 11 announcement.

“Innovation is most meaningful when we identify patient needs, take what works or doesn't work and find new ways to extend the positive impact,” said Sara Loewy, Houston Methodist virtual care vice president, in a statement. “What we’ve built in Houston has the potential to reach much further. By thoughtfully extending this model into new care settings, our goal with this program is to demonstrate how our system’s existing technology and clinical expertise can strengthen access and improve outcomes in rural communities.”

Loewy added the expansion is an opportunity to “explore how the capabilities of a major health system can be shared across geographic boundaries.”

While 72% of health tech startups lead with “AI-powered” in their descriptors, zero surveyed venture capitalists in a new report from HLTH and SolComms said the phrase increased their confidence.

Independently surveyed health tech reporters also ranked “AI-powered” as one of the most overused phrases in pitching–in addition to “revolutionizing/transforming” and “disrupting.”

Alongside these survey responses, researchers compared–and scored–the messaging of 72 U.S.-based, venture-backed health tech startups at series A to series C for the “Healthtech Attention Economy Report.” Startups’ messaging across their website and social media was scored on four main dimensions: clarity, differentiation, proof and policy awareness.

SolComms Vice President of HealthTech Isabella Morreale said in a statement the companies that will get attention within the next year “are leading with proof, not adjectives.”

“That means speaking to clinical data, measurable outcomes, and validation from third parties,” Morreale said. “Founder-led storytelling will also continue to stand out. Among the data, one common through-thread for investor and reporter interest was founders with a real point of view on the industry.”

Morreale continued, “Reporters don't need another company description; they need a source who can help them explain what's happening in healthcare.”

Less than half (42.2%) of patients say communications from healthcare providers “often” or “always” feel relevant to their needs, a new report from Lirio found.

Analysts at the behavioral science and AI-powered behavioral intelligence platform polled 1,500 adults across the U.S. for the survey. While 58% of respondents report communications not consistently feeling relevant to their needs, 55.6% rate overall communication experiences with healthcare organizations as “good” or “excellent.”

When asked why healthcare communications are ignored altogether, respondents report generic or irrelevant content (24.4%), too many similar messages (21.8%) and confusing, jargon-filled messages (18.8%) as top reasons.

Nearly half of respondents (47.9%) report the potential value of more personalized, better-timed messages as an “8, 9 or 10” for how much it could help them make better care decisions and stay engaged.

Lirio Chief Behavioral Officer Amy Bucher, Ph.D., said in a statement patients “are not rejecting healthcare communications outright.”

“They are telling us, in specific and measurable terms, that the system is talking at them instead of to them,” Bucher said. "The instinct in healthcare has long been to solve engagement with more outreach - more emails, more reminders, more portals. This data shows that volume is not the problem. What is missing is relevance — content and timing designed for where a specific patient is in their own health journey, rather than a single message broadcast to an entire population.”

Bucher added the healthcare organizations that close the communication gap will be “the ones that use behavioral data to determine the right message, the right channel, and the right moment for each individual patient, which is the model Lirio was built to deliver."

Community-based specialty palliative care provider Vynca is expanding its reach into four more states.

Patients will now be able to access Vynca’s services in Georgia, Hawaii, New Jersey and Texas. Vynca is one of the first practices approved to provide services under New Jersey’s Community-Based Palliative Care Medicaid benefit, launched in April, according to the Nov. 16 announcement.

Founded in 2013, the company’s hybrid care model combines in-home visits with virtual clinical support and predictive technology, and executives say it has shown 43% fewer emergency department visits and 52% less hospital stays within six months of enrollment.

“Health plan and government program leaders understand the value of community-based palliative care for people with serious illness,” said Darren Schulte, M.D., Vynca CEO, in a statement. “Our expansion in four key states reflects growing momentum to expand specialty outpatient palliative care outside of hospices. The key is to broaden access and convenience for the entire eligible population by offering home-based options. Too often, patients are limited to seeking palliative care when available in the community, at hospital-based clinics and facilities.”

#5790. Google Maps Is Trying To Shoehorn AI Into Your Road Trips Now

If planning that next day trip is just too burdensome a task, don't worry, like every other aspect of our lives now, there's AI for that. Google announced its latest and greatest AI-driven planning feature that will do the heavy lifting in planning your next trip.

Google's new Ask Maps feature uses its Gemini AI to lay out an entire itinerary for your next trip in Google Maps, whether it's a day-long adventure, to a journey of several days. Obviously the announcement was timed to provide enough runway for people to put it to use for the upcoming holiday season in the U.S. AAA expects 81.5 million people to travel for the Thanksgiving and the capitalism-fueled Black Friday holiday weekend.

To get started, all you have to do is Ask Maps to plan your trip for so many days and suggest what you'd like to do for the trip. Google puts it like this:

Use Ask Maps to plan a day trip for holiday shopping or something more complex — like a three-day road trip with stops for coastal hikes and oceanfront dining. You'll get a day-by-day schedule with insider tips and numbered map pins for each stop, making it easy to visualize the plan. Once you're happy with the itinerary, share it with friends and family, or export it to Google Docs on desktop.

It's that easy. This could be handy for planning a long trip with an electric vehicle and get help with finding charging stations. Or planning that automotive day trip in Detroit. From my quick search, it could use some edits.

In the U.S. you can modify your itinerary in real-time. If some place ends up closed, or you need to make a detour or additional stop, Ask Maps to add or adjust accordingly. It'll also make appointments for you. Need an upcoming day off to be filled with appointments and errands or just let it order the takeout you wanted instead of using whatever other delivery apps are on your phone. Ask Maps will do it all. The only finger you may have to lift is the one typing in the query to Ask Maps to do the work for you. Although, to be honest, you're more likely to ask it to do the task via voice command.

Perhaps the most curious thought surrounding this, aside from humanity's continued loss of curiosity and self-sufficiency while letting Google do the work, is how long will Google serve these trip recommendations organically? It really is only a matter of time before Google feeds destinations and appointments paid for by the highest bidder like it has with its Search results, or in Google Maps (no Google, I do not want to eat Chick-fil-A, nor do I want to eat it at 8 a.m. in a snowstorm on my way to a doctor's appointment).

Lucky for the world, this feature is expected to expand to Google Maps in over 30 countries across Europe and approximately 200 countries and territories, so everyone can melt their brains by letting Google tell it how to do even more things. There's no way it could go wrong as AI has been so reliable in doing things like basic math and giving out parking tickets.

#5789. Electric Vans — Are They The Final EV Frontier? Kia PV5.

We took a trip to see the Kia PV5 electric van. Our brief takeaway: “Kool like a Kia.” Should keep the bean counters happy.

Inspired by a comment in the Electric Vehicle Council report about electric vans, Majella and I decided to have a short visit to a group of dealerships under the Norris Motors banner. We hoped to check out the LDV eDeliver 5 van, touted as selling for under AU$50,000. However, there was no stock to view and we ended up chatting to the dealer and then moving on to the MG U9 BEV ute. You can read about it here. And then we found the Kia PV5 delivery van.

Julie Delvecchio, the CEO of EVC, calls the lack of affordable electric vans in Australia “the single biggest unclaimed opportunity left.” We think that is about to change. Since its launch in May, 496 PV5 have sold in Australia.

The Kia PV5 reminded me a lot of the Renault Kangoo in both size and shape, and it is able to carry about the same payload. And the front looked like the BMW i3. The one we looked at had been sold and was awaiting a fitout as a camper van. The Norris Group Kia specialist was generous with his time and the information he shared. He has been selling the Kia EV5 for the past 5 months and delivered 5 of the vans. He sold his first one within a week of its arrival on the lot — to Ray, a driver working for Amazon.

Ray appreciated the 390 km range. My friendly sales rep tells me that this will be increased in 2027 models to 420 km. Ray was getting out of diesel and most of his questions were around charging and regen braking. Most of his trips were around the burbs and he was not intending to take the van on the highway. The Kia PV5 is a workhorse, with a passenger car feel and level of comfort. It even has electric lumbar support for the driver — great when you are spending so much time in the van delivering parcels in the lead-up to Christmas. It certainly has a lot more tech than the diesel he was trading in.

In fact, he was “blown away” by the standard features — the 360-degree camera, for example. “Vans are not known for their visibility.” On his return to the dealership for his first service, he commented on the smoothness of the torque: “It has no issues going uphill.” He reported that the van felt “well planted on the road.” Ray has gotten used to trickle charging at home and has lost his range anxiety. Like most new EV drivers, once he started living and working with the vehicle, his fears vanished. The PV5 supports fast charging also for when Ray needs a boost on the road.

But what about that all-important question of cost? Can we make the bean counters happy? The Kia PV5 retails for AU$56,000 (before on-road costs), which is about the same price as comparable diesel vans like the Ford Transit and the Toyota HiAce. Add to that the savings on fuel, the peace of mind from fuel security, and the reduced servicing and it is easy to build a business case for the Kia PV5 — cost per parcel delivery. The PV5 even comes with vehicle to load.

And it isn’t made in China! Can the PV5 replace Toyota’s HiAce? Find the answer in this video. The reviewer points out many features that make the PV5 an attractive option: the charge port is on the front. The van has lots of nooks and crannies for storage. She measures storage by capacity to fit in bottles of wine — for example, two in the glove box. There is a step provided to alight the van, aided by grab handles. More storage space is provided above the steering wheel, including a nifty phone slot. There are customizable buttons on the steering wheel to create shortcuts; for example, to the air conditioning settings. A wireless phone charger is nestled near the dash. The glovebox contains the V2L adaptor supplied with the van.

The fact that the van reverts to the lowest regen setting every time you restart the car is a little niggle. That, and there is no frunk — reducing the reviewer’s all-important need for wine storage. Full specs can be found here. Kia even gives you helpful advice on how to apply for electric vehicle subsidies (last page, bottom right). One of the pictures that caught my eye shows the PV5 as a flatbed truck.

The PV5 was recently named 2026 International Van of the Year. It measures 4695mm long, 2255mm wide, and 1899mm tall on a 2995mm wheelbase. Range is stated as 416 km, but will of course vary with driving style and load. The battery is a 71.2kWh nickel cobalt manganese (NCM) lithium-ion battery. Kia offers a 165,000 km warranty on the battery. The van is front-wheel drive.

The PV5 has over 4 cubic metres of cargo capacity, double both that of the ID Buzz and the Tesla Model Y. Space enough for two euro pallets. There are multiple access points, with sliding doors on both sides and barn doors in the rear. The rear barn doors open to 180 degrees, and a low 419mm load height improves accessibility.

Goods being shipped are protected by padding on the rear doors and insides of the back. The PV5 can carry up to 740 kg. Tiedowns on the floor of the rear of the van help to secure cargo. There is also a power point in the back of the van. Perhaps a tradie can recharge their tools on site. No frunk for wine.

From the spec sheet: “Standard driver assist features include blind-spot collision avoidance, navigation-based adaptive cruise control, lane-keep assist, lane centring with hands-on detection, and parking collision avoidance assist.”

A launch in the USA is financially difficult because of the 25% “chicken tax.” Kia builds the PV5 in South Korea and classifies it as a commercial vehicle, and it faces a 25% US import tariff on light trucks and commercial vans.

Sadly, the Kia PV5 doesn’t come in red, but only in white, grey and black. Most companies may take up the option of wrapping the van in their own livery. Overall, we were impressed with Kia PV5 and look forward to seeing more of them on the road throughout the suburbs as Australian delivery drivers move towards a bright electric future.

David Waterworth is a retired teacher who divides his time between looking after his grandchildren and trying to make sure they have a planet to live on. He is long on Tesla [NASDAQ:TSLA].

#5788. HLTH 2026: Teladoc Health's weight care program lets employers partially contribute to GLP-1 costs

HLTH 2026: Teladoc Health's weight care program lets employers partially contribute to GLP-1 costs" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Teladoc Health is expanding its Comprehensive Weight Care program with a new option that allows employers to contribute to GLP-1 costs outside of traditional pharmacy benefit designs.

Through the new offering, employers can give access to medications from Eli Lilly and Novo Nordisk—accompanied by support from licensed professionals, personalized lifestyle coaching, artificial intelligence-enabled food logging and more.

With each plan year, employers will set a fixed payment per employee per fill that applies consistently across covered medications, the company said. Employees then pay the remaining balance for each fill. Teladoc manages eligibility, program oversight and consolidated invoicing.

Pharmacy claims are administered on a 100% pass-through basis, including eligibility verification and audit-ready claims documentation.

Moreover, employers can opt in to tie contributions to program engagement, using metrics like food logging and regular weigh-ins, executives say.

The offering is available now, with initial contracted clients slated to launch programs for their employees beginning Jan. 1, 2027.

“Employers want to provide benefits that help their workforce achieve their health goals and need flexible strategies that work for their organizations,” said Kelly Bliss, Teladoc Health U.S. Group Health president, in a statement. “We are helping employers meet the needs of their employees while providing greater predictability and transparency around healthcare spending. Importantly, we give people a trusted path to high-quality care and affordable access to FDA-approved, authentic medication for meaningful, sustained health outcomes.”

Alongside the announcement, the company also unveiled new research examining two years of weight-loss outcomes among nearly 23,000 members enrolled in its programs.

Results indicated “clinically meaningful weight loss” increased over time, with 47.7% of members achieving at least 5% weight loss by 24 months. And among those who lost weight during their first year, 53% continued losing additional weight during the second.

#5787. If You Want GTA 6's Analog For A Pontiac Trans Am With The Screaming Chicken, Do This

If you're just reading this, you were likely not one of the 43 enlisted soldiers taking advantage of a four-day leave to play "Grand Theft Auto 6," and instead worked a full day at your job and/or took care of other real-life responsibilities. Being late to the party, though, means there's more insight on the internet for your playthrough, telling you to make sure you fill your gas tank, or remember that Flock-like cameras will track you during your crime sprees. In this case, we found a quicker and easier way to take on one of the first car theft missions in the game.

The mission, called "Unwanted Attention," is to steal a beloved red Phoenix from a police impound lot to acquire some misplaced "cargo," i.e. the "like 20 pounds or something" of weed that Cal tells you is hidden somewhere in the car. According to Polygon, this can be done one of two ways. The first — which is not recommended — is to make your presence very known with weapons and a show. The other, much subtler method is acquisition by stealth.

Now, the version Polygon points you toward is doable, but looks to be pretty involved. Its version has you using special machines to get onto the roof and sneaking into an office where you might have to take down a mechanic. However, a favorite YouTuber of mine found a way that's even more chill, with far fewer steps.

The fun begins when you pull up to the impound lot in Hamlet. First, you'll want to ensure that Jason doesn't have any weapons on his person. Meanwhile, in gameplay, Cal sends a text: "Help her rise from the flames (fire emoji x3)." Polygon's guide tells you your best bet from here is to not hop the impound lot fence, but if no one is around, you can go ahead and scramble over it, like Jacksepticeye did. From here, casually walk through the little lot to the far back right corner, where you'll see some shipment boxes stacked together in a convenient way that leads up to a storage container on the other side of the fence. Climb up and enjoy a view for a beat.

It's not easy to spot upon first glance, but you'll essentially want to look for the white tow truck under the awning tucked toward the farthest wall from where you are standing. The elusive Phoenix is attached behind it. Carefully climb down from the container and walk casually toward the right of the tow truck. You'll want to be careful to avoid being seen or detected by the cop on the left side of the lot.

Just to be safe, you can use your Waink car scanner on your phone to verify the car attached to the truck is indeed the Phoenix. It'll tell you it's a 1977 Imponte Phoenix (or, in real life, a Pontiac Firebird Trans Am, recognizable thanks to the large gold "Screaming Chicken" on the hood). You can easily unhook the Phoenix from the truck.

Now all you have to do is get in the car and hot-wire it — the game will tell you how to do so. Take the car toward the impound lot gate (where you came in) to open it, which will take a painstaking minute, especially thanks to all of the attention you have now drawn trying to drive the car out of the lot. Once it's open, get going! Keep an eye out, however, with your newly acquired flock of enthusiastic followers: the police.

GTA gamers will know you just have to keep on driving to lose these guys. Once you do that, the game will find ways to let you know to take the car to the Hamlet Pay 'N' Spray shop, where you can fix your likely very dented car as you get acclimated to the game. You'll also want to give it a little makeover  so as to avoid drawing any additional attention. Then it's time to deliver the finished product to Brian's garage where you'll collect a $150 cash reward, likely to fund your own makeover, as you'll also need a way to be less identifiable to outsmart the cops. With that all done, your mission is finally complete.

#5785. Optum Rx rolls out new pharmacy tools aimed at transparency

Optum Rx rolls out new pharmacy tools aimed at transparency" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Earlier this year, Optum Rx teased the rollout of new pharmacy tools designed to address medication cost and access.

Now, the company is offering a look at how those tools work.

The pharmacy benefit manager said Tuesday that the tools build on Optum's new and more transparent pharmacy model, which the company announced in May and shifts to a fee-based structure. The model moves away from more traditional approaches linked to manufacturer pricing and prescription volume and offers clients a clearly defined fee structure.

The first platform, called Shop MyScript, allows members to compare prices for their medications as well as their options for pharmacies or delivery before the prescription is dispensed. The tool will notify members of potential cost savings shortly after a script is written.

In addition, Shop MyScript embeds in the prescribing workflow, Optum said, so that the member does not have to make comparisons after the fact. If they choose to keep the existing prescription, it will then be sent to the pharmacy.

Optum said that early adopters have averaged $28 in savings through the program.

"People should have clear information about their medication costs and options when they can actually use it, not after a decision has already been made," said Katte Carey, president of Optum Rx, in the announcement. "These tools give people more information earlier in their journey, so they can make choices that work for them and get the support they need along the way."

In addition, the pharmacy benefit manager announced Optum Assistant, a conversational AI guide that helps members complete routine tasks and track down key information. The tool can triage concerns on a range of topics, from questions about medication and benefits to pharmacy experience challenges.

If the AI guide can't address the individual's needs, they can choose to be connected to live support to find a solution. Optum said about 80% of conversations using the tool led to a resolution without needing a live advocate.

The final new launch aims to support cash-pay options, according to the announcement.

Optum Price Wise will offer members a complete cost breakdown, including the price of a drug and any fees. That total, all-in price allows members to fully understand what their options may be and make the choice that works best for them at the pharmacy counter.

The PBM said new tools like these build on Optum's new and more transparent pharmacy model, which the company announced in May and shifts to a fee-based structure. The model moves away from more traditional approaches linked to manufacturer pricing and prescription volume and offers clients a clearly defined fee structure.

#5784. GTA 6 Ultimate Edition Has A Car Upgrade You Can Unlock Right From The Start

Like other installments in the long-running and beloved series, "Grand Theft Auto 6" is as much about the autos as it is about the grand theft. Sure, the game has a campaign, and the open-world setting makes it tons of fun to run around and commit crimes. But for some people, the biggest draw has always been Rockstar's sweet, sweet, non-licensed but clearly real-world-inspired cars (and planes, bikes, and boats, of course). Throughout the game, you'll find and unlock car customization options of all types, from decals to lighting and beyond.

If you want one of the coolest rides in all of gaming, though, you don't have to work very hard. You just have to shell out the extra $20 to get GTA 6 Ultimate Edition. Doing so gets you early-game access to the coveted and beautiful Ganado Retro Build. This is an exclusive customization of player character Jason's personal '70 Ganado, a Ford Ranchero-like pickup truck that's become an instant classic. Of all the cars of GTA 6, the Ganado Retro Build is especially noteworthy because of what it replaces. It's a major glow up from the well-loved, drab-colored Ganado Jason starts the game with.

While Ultimate Edition owners are spoiled with perhaps the best-looking car in the game, they still have to work for it just a bit. No matter which version of the game you buy, you start out with Jason's base Vapid Ganado, which isn't in the best condition. To upgrade to the iconic Retro Build, you'll have to drive the Ganado to a location called Coastal Auto Care, found in Key Lento. Just don't forget to fill up on the way, because cars in "Grand Theft Auto 6" don't have infinite fuel. The player is introduced to Coastal Auto Care early on via a mission, and you can find it on your map by filtering for "Mod Shops."

You can upgrade your Ganado during the "Just a Scratch" mission, or you can visit Coastal Auto Care any time after completing the mission to apply the upgrade. Once you've driven into the garage's entrance, you get the car customization screen. This screen offers you a choice of different customization options, like cosmetics, bodywork, performance, and interior. Instead of choosing any of those, select the "Pro Builds" tab, which offers you the choice between full kits and wheel kits. For Ultimate Edition players, the Ganado Retro Build kit is found under "Full Kits," and it's free to apply. Unfortunately, this classic design and slick color palette is specific to Jason's Ganado, so you can't bring in other cars and apply the Retro Build kit to them.

Once you're cruising around in the sickest car in the game, you'll want to practice defensive driving. GTA 6 has an advanced physics engine for more realistic car crashes, and nobody wants to see such a beautiful car smashed to bits in a fender-bender.

If you're the type of person who likes to try out every car possible, you'll have your work cut out for you in "GTA 6." There are over 300 different rides you can find throughout the course of the game. Some, like the Declasse Akane (inspired by the Chevy Sonic) are pretty unexciting, albeit still fun to commit major crimes in. Others are breath-takers that can help under-thrilled enthusiasts live their exotic and classic driving dreams.

Take the '95 Grotti Cheetah, inspired by the Ferrari Testarossa. This is another Ultimate Edition-only vehicle, but boy is it sick. If you figure you'll probably never drive a real Testarossa without renting one in Vegas, driving the Cheetah around is a fun second-best. Another eye-grabbing option is the obnoxious green Canis Kamacho, inspired by the Jeep Crew Chief 715 concept. It's a solid in-game off-roader that's available in the base game.

For players with aspirations that extend beyond the asphalt, the Shitzu Squalo is a special boat available to owners of the Ultimate Edition of the game. It's based on the Christ-Craft Stinger 390x and can be found at the docks of Washington Beach. It has a striking pink and blue gradient color scheme, and despite its name, it's not squalid at all. You'll also get access to biplanes, jets, helicopters, and even airboats as you play the game, allowing you to explore the world in whatever style fits your fancy.

#5783. Medicare Shared Savings Program saved $2.5B last year: CMS

Medicare Shared Savings Program saved $2.5B last year: CMS" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The Medicare Shared Savings Program has reached the highest savings number in its history, according to new federal data.

The Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services said Tuesday that the program generated more than $2.5 billion in net savings for Medicare in 2025, making for the ninth year in a row that it led to cost savings. The agency added that every accountable care organization met or exceeded quality reporting requirements.

ACOs participating in MSSP earned $4.9 billion in shared savings payments during 2025, CMS said, with 80% of organizations earning performance payments.

As of 2026, 511 ACOs representing more than 700,000 providers are participating in the Medicare Shared Savings Program, which represents 12.6 million people enrolled in traditional Medicare, another record.

"Patients want doctors who work together, care that puts their needs first, and fewer unnecessary hurdles—that is what accountable care is designed to deliver," said CMS Administrator Mehmet Oz, M.D., in the announcement. "These record savings show the power of rewarding providers for quality and coordination. We want more people with Medicare, and more of the providers who care for them, to benefit from this approach."

CMS said that it wants to make it easier for providers to participate in MSSP if they choose, especially given the program's results to date. It included changes in the latest Physician Fee Schedule to encourage participation, including increased opportunity for certain ACOs to earn shared savings and new financial incentives to draw in first-time participants.

In addition, CMS said that would create more predictable spending targets that make planning and participation straightforward. The agency is also aiming to streamline patient notices and simplify required technology.

“We are excited to see continued progress on both savings and enrollment for Medicare beneficiaries. Beneficiaries are best served when a provider is watching out for them and coordinating their care,” said Center for Medicare Director John Brooks in the announcement.

Emily Brower, CEO of the National Association of ACOs, said in a statement that the 2025 results "build on years of progress and underscore what the accountable care community can accomplish through collaboration, innovation and the widespread adoption of proven best practices."

"NAACOS looks forward to partnering with CMS to build on this momentum," said Brower. "Policies that unleash innovation, reduce burden, and create sustainability through fair and predictable budgets will allow ACOs to deliver even greater value for patients and the Medicare program."

#5782. Medicare for All Has Capitol Hill Abuzz, but It's Unlikely to Pass Soon, Experts Say

— Instead, gradual additions like lowering the eligibility age are seen as a better bet

The idea of expanding Medicare to cover the entire population -- otherwise known as "Medicare for All" (MFA) or "single-payer" -- has been a popular catch phrase among the incoming Democratic members of Congress.

"In the richest, most powerful country in the world, you should not have to suffer for your healthcare," Sen.-elect Abdul El-Sayed, MD, DPhil (D-Mich.), an MFA supporter, told a roomful of nurses last month, The Guardian reported.

"Medicare for All is at the top of my list," Rep.-elect Peggy Flanagan (D-Minn.) told the Minnesota Women's Press last month.

But is that a realistic possibility, especially when Democrats -- although they gained large numbers of seats in the midterms -- don't have enough votes for a veto-proof Senate majority? Probably not at this point, Katie Keith, JD, MPH, director of the Center for Health Policy and the Law at Georgetown University, in Washington, said in a phone interview. "There is a lot of talk ... that there's a need to improve traditional Medicare before you get to open it up and transition to a single-payer system," she said.

This is best illustrated by Sen. Bernie Sanders (I-Vt.) -- a long-time champion of single-payer -- who recently came out with a plan to get to MFA, but incrementally. "It doesn't seem to be an abandonment of MFA itself but that's what Sanders came out with -- lowering the Medicare eligibility age; focusing on [adding] dental, vision, and hearing coverage; and covering kids," Keith said. "These are all significant changes."

An incremental approach also appeared in a memo sent in September to House Majority Leader Hakeem Jeffries (D-N.Y.) -- widely expected to be elected Speaker of the House in the incoming Congress. The memo from the Democrats' Cost-of-Living Healthcare Working Group called for capping Medicare beneficiaries' annual out-of-pocket costs at $5,000 as well as adding dental, vision, and hearing coverage -- all part of the group's "First 100 Hours" plan. Then, during the first year in office, the proposal calls for Democrats to "commit to achieving universal coverage" through "comprehensive examination of various single-payer and public option models."

But aspirations alone won't determine the fate of incoming Democrats' legislation, Keith added. "Who knows what committees these new members will be on? Sure they'll be committed to their campaign pledges, but where will they each be in the Senate or the House to push on policy positions of their choice? If El-Sayed is on the [Health, Education, Labor, and Pensions Committee], that gives him a platform, but he might have other priorities. On the House side, there will be a change in leadership at the Energy and Commerce Health Subcommittee -- what new members will be joining that? It will be interesting to see who lands where and what their priorities are."

Mark Weller, JD, shareholder at the Polsinelli law firm in Washington, doubts that MFA will make it across the finish line any time soon. "The most ambitious healthcare proposals have little chance of becoming law," he said in an email. "In the Senate, Democrats will have 56 [votes], but it's not 60, and it's certainly not the 67 needed to override a veto. President Trump still has the veto pen, and Republicans can still block things in the Senate."

In addition, "there are competing factions in the Democratic caucus," he said. "The challenge is for Democrats to deliver on the affordability mandate. The alternative is spending the next 2 years explaining why divided government prevented them from doing so. If voters feel the economy is not working for them, hearings on Medicare for All aren't going to make their life better. The trick will be to find some opportunities for bipartisan progress."

MFA also has plenty of influential detractors inside and outside of Congress. "MFA is grabbing lots of attention while keeping candidates from discussing much more important issues like our $40 trillion public debt, how to reduce our $2 trillion-per-year federal budget deficits, solvency of Social Security and Medicare, child and elderly poverty, and climate change," Wendell Primus, PhD, visiting fellow at the Brookings Institution's Center on Health Policy, wrote prior to Election Day in an unpublished opinion article, which he shared with MedPage Today. "Let me explain why it should never be enacted."

First of all, compared to the Affordable Care Act (ACA), "MFA is an even more mammoth undertaking because the 10-year cost of $32 trillion is impossible to finance," wrote Primus, who, as the senior health adviser to former House Speaker Nancy Pelosi (D-Calif.), was the lead House staffer for getting the ACA passed in 2010. "While the health plan is generous and would provide more coverage than most health plans today, there will be many employers and individual voters who would consider themselves losers when they compare their tax bill to their out-of-pocket health costs. Those who lose because of MFA will stand in the way of enactment."

"In addition, many key stakeholders will balk at the initiatives to lower healthcare costs that will necessarily result," he added. "Specifically, it will be extremely difficult to get hospitals, doctors, insurance companies, and all other care providers to be convinced that MFA would be better than the status quo. And the fear of the unknown will also be a powerful force against enactment."
